	<item>
		<title>Batch write and discover records in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/batch-write-and-discover-records-in-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Harshil Shah]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 28 Aug 2026 19:31:05 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Machine Learning]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Artificial Intelligence]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ac351c6a258d9c6b09e4e26e7198133fc26a60a3</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker Feature Store now supports two new APIs: BatchWriteRecord writes up to 25 records across multiple feature groups in a single call, and ListRecords enumerates record identifiers within a feature group. In this post, we walk through each API with code examples you can use to get started.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt; is a fully managed, purpose-built repository to store, share, and manage features for machine learning (ML) models. It provides low-latency online serving for real-time inference, an offline store for historical retention and training feature data, and supports both &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/using-streaming-ingestion-with-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store-to-make-ml-backed-decisions-in-near-real-time/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;streaming&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/scale-ml-feature-ingestion-using-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;batch&lt;/a&gt; ingestion patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As ML platforms mature, two operational gaps surface repeatedly. First, teams running high-throughput feature pipelines must call &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_PutRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PutRecord&lt;/a&gt; (which writes a single feature record to the online store) in a loop. This means one API call per record, per feature group, which creates connection overhead and poor throughput. A fraud-detection pipeline ingesting 10,000 records per second across five feature groups must sustain 50,000 individual API calls per second only to keep features current. A second challenge is that teams using the In-Memory storage tier have no way to browse or enumerate records stored in the online store. If record identifiers are lost through a bug or pipeline failure, those records become permanently unrecoverable. There is no offline store for the In-Memory tier to fall back on, no &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt; query to run, and no API to discover what exists.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we are announcing &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_Operations_Amazon_SageMaker_Feature_Store_Runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;two new APIs&lt;/a&gt; for Amazon SageMaker Feature Store:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BatchWriteRecord&lt;/a&gt; — Write up to 25 records across multiple feature groups in a single API call, with partial-success semantics, per-record time-to-live (TTL) control, and the same EventTime-based ordering guarantees as PutRecord.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_ListRecords.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ListRecords&lt;/a&gt; — Enumerate record identifiers within a feature group using pagination. Works with both Standard (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt;-backed) and In-Memory (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticache/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Redis&lt;/a&gt;-backed) storage tiers.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through each API with code examples you can use to get started.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along with the examples in this post, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/free/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with permissions to create Amazon SageMaker AI resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI execution role&lt;/a&gt; with access to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue&lt;/a&gt;, and permissions to interact with Feature Store data plane APIs. The following AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policy shows the minimum required permissions: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "sagemaker:BatchWriteRecord",
                "sagemaker:PutRecord",
                "sagemaker:ListRecords"
            ],
            "Resource": "arn:aws:sagemaker:*:*:feature-group/*"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://boto3.amazonaws.com/v1/documentation/api/latest/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Boto3&lt;/a&gt; (latest version) or &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/sagemaker-python-sdk/releases/tag/v3.8.0" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Python SDK v3.8.0&lt;/a&gt; or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;One or more existing feature groups with records ingested (if you are new to Feature Store, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/amazon-sagemaker-feature-store-end-to-end-workshop/blob/main/01-module-feature-store-foundations/m1_nb1_introduction_to_feature_store.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;end-to-end workshop notebook&lt;/a&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="batchwriterecord"&gt;BatchWriteRecord&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The BatchWriteRecord API tackles the throughput limits of single-record ingestion. The following sections explain the problem it solves and how it works.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-challenge-with-single-record-ingestion"&gt;The challenge with single-record ingestion&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The existing &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_PutRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PutRecord&lt;/a&gt; API in Feature Store writes one record to one feature group per call. Each call performs a conditional write: the record is persisted as the “latest” version only if its EventTime, included in the request, is newer than the existing record. If the condition fails, the record is still written as a historical version for the offline store.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This design provides strong ordering guarantees, but at scale it forces an N×M calling pattern (N records × M feature groups), creating connection overhead and tail latency that limit throughput.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-batchwriterecord-works"&gt;How BatchWriteRecord works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BatchWriteRecord&lt;/a&gt; accepts up to 25 entries in a single request, targeting one or more feature groups simultaneously. Each record succeeds or fails independently. This is a partial-success API, meaning individual record failures do not fail the entire request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The API preserves the same EventTime-based ordering as &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_PutRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PutRecord&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If the incoming record’s EventTime is newer than the existing record, it becomes the latest version in the online store.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If not, the record is written as a historical version to the offline store (for feature groups with offline storage).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Records that fail for other reasons (authentication/validation errors, service throttling) are returned in the response with error details and the original record.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The requests that are unprocessed will be returned in response as &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html#API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord_ResponseSyntax" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UnprocessedEntries&lt;/a&gt; which can be retried.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="request-structure"&gt;Request structure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Entries": [
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "click-features",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": "user-123"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T12:00:00Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "click_count", "ValueAsString": "42"}
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore", "OfflineStore"],
            "TtlDuration": {"Unit": "Days", "Value": 7}
        },
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "login-features",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": "user-456"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T12:00:01Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "login_count", "ValueAsString": "18"}
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore", "OfflineStore"]
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The response returns only the records that failed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Errors": [
        {
            "Entry": {
                "FeatureGroupName": "string",
                "Record": [
                    {
                        "FeatureName": "string",
                        "ValueAsString": "string",
                        "ValueAsStringList": ["string"]
                    }
                ],
                "TargetStores": ["string"],
                "TtlDuration": {
                    "Unit": "string",
                    "Value": number
                }
            },
            "ErrorCode": "string",
            "ErrorMessage": "string"
        }
    ],
    "UnprocessedEntries": [
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "string",
            "Record": [
                {
                    "FeatureName": "string",
                    "ValueAsString": "string",
                    "ValueAsStringList": ["string"]
                }
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["string"],
            "TtlDuration": {
                "Unit": "string",
                "Value": number
            }
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Records not listed in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html#API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord_ResponseSyntax" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Errors&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html#API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord_ResponseSyntax" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;UnprocessedEntries&lt;/a&gt; succeeded. Your application should retry only the failed records using exponential backoff for retriable errors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="code-example-batch-ingestion-with-boto3"&gt;Code example: Batch ingestion with Boto3&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

featurestore_runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-featurestore-runtime")

response = featurestore_runtime.batch_write_record(
    Entries=[
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "click-features",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": "user-123"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T12:00:00Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "click_count", "ValueAsString": "42"},
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore", "OfflineStore"],
        },
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "login-features",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": "user-456"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T12:00:01Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "login_count", "ValueAsString": "18"},
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore", "OfflineStore"],
        },
    ]
)

if response["Errors"]:
    for error in response["Errors"]:
        print(f"Record {error['Entry']}, ErrorCode: {error['ErrorCode']} Failed: {error['ErrorMessage']}")

if response["UnprocessedEntries"]:
    for unprocessed in response["UnprocessedEntries"]:
        print(f"Unprocessed: {unprocessed['FeatureGroupName']}")

if not response["Errors"] and not response["UnprocessedEntries"]:
    print("All records written successfully.")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="code-example-writing-across-multiple-feature-groups"&gt;Code example: Writing across multiple feature groups&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can target multiple feature groups in a single request. Records are grouped by feature group and processed independently:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;featurestore_runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-featurestore-runtime")

response = featurestore_runtime.batch_write_record(
    Entries=[
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "user-profile-features",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": "user-123"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T12:00:00Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "age", "ValueAsString": "34"},
                {"FeatureName": "region", "ValueAsString": "us-west-2"},
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore"],
        },
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "click-features",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": "user-123"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T12:00:00Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "click_count", "ValueAsString": "42"},
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore", "OfflineStore"],
        },
    ]
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A failure in one feature group does not affect records destined for other feature groups.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ttl-time-to-live-support"&gt;TTL (Time-to-Live) support&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BatchWriteRecord&lt;/a&gt; supports &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/feature-store-time-to-live.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;TTL&lt;/a&gt; at three levels of precedence, shown in the following priority order:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Record-level TTL — Set with TtlDuration on individual entries. Takes highest priority.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Request-level TTL — A default TtlDuration at the top level of the request, applied to entries without a record-level TTL.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Feature-group-level TTL — The TTL configured on the feature group itself, applied when neither record-level nor request-level TTL is set.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-considerations"&gt;Key considerations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Maximum 25 entries per request. This limit applies to the total number of entries across all feature groups in a single request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Partial-success semantics: Unlike transactional APIs, BatchWriteRecord does not roll back successful writes if some records fail. Design your retry logic to re-submit only the records returned in Errors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Similar IAM model as PutRecord: The caller must have sagemaker:BatchWriteRecord and sagemaker:PutRecord permission on the Amazon Resource Name (ARN) of each target feature group. Per-feature-group authorization is checked before processing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;EventTime ordering is preserved: BatchWriteRecord uses conditional writes to maintain the same latest-record-wins semantics as PutRecord. A stale record cannot overwrite a newer one in the online store.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;TargetStores flexibility: Each entry can independently target OnlineStore, OfflineStore, or both (defaults to the feature group’s enabled stores), giving you fine-grained control over where each record lands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="listrecords"&gt;ListRecords&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The ListRecords API closes the gap in record discovery for both storage tiers. The following sections explain the problem it solves and how it works.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-challenge-with-record-discovery"&gt;The challenge with record discovery&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Feature Store supports &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_PutRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PutRecord&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_GetRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GetRecord&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_DeleteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeleteRecord&lt;/a&gt;, but all require the caller to know the exact record identifier. There is no API to browse or enumerate records within a feature group.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the Standard tier, the workaround is querying the offline store by using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt;. This requires offline store configuration, adds cost, and is not real-time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the In-Memory tier, the situation is critical. There is no corresponding offline store by default. If record identifiers are lost, those records are completely unrecoverable. You cannot discover them, and you cannot delete them. This leads to phantom data, wasted storage costs, and potential compliance risks when data subjects request deletion.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-listrecords-works"&gt;How ListRecords works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_ListRecords.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ListRecords&lt;/a&gt; enumerates record identifiers within a feature group using pagination. It returns only active, non-deleted, non-expired records that are ready to be used with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_GetRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GetRecord&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_DeleteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeleteRecord&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The API works with both storage tiers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Standard tier (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/a&gt;): Scans the online store, returning identifier of the latest version of each record. Soft-deleted and expired records are automatically excluded.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In-Memory tier (&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticache/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Redis&lt;/a&gt;): Scans keys and filters out soft-deleted records and internal system keys. Returns record identifiers extracted from key names.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="request-and-response-structure"&gt;Request and response structure&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;POST /FeatureGroup/{FeatureGroupName}/ListRecords&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Request body:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Initial call&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "MaxResults": 50
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Or&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "MaxResults": 50,
    "NextToken": "eyJjdXJzb3IiOi4uLn0="
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Response:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "RecordIdentifiers": [
        "user-001",
        "user-002",
        "user-003"
    ],
    "NextToken": "eyJuZXh0IjoiLi4ufQ=="
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When NextToken is absent in the response, pagination is complete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="code-example-enumerate-all-records-in-a-feature-group"&gt;Code example: Enumerate all records in a feature group&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

featurestore_runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-featurestore-runtime")

all_identifiers = []
next_token = None

while True:
    params = {
        "FeatureGroupName": "user-profile-features",
        "MaxResults": 100,
    }
    if next_token:
        params["NextToken"] = next_token

    response = featurestore_runtime.list_records(**params)
    all_identifiers.extend(response["RecordIdentifiers"])

    next_token = response.get("NextToken")
    if not next_token:
        break

print(f"Found {len(all_identifiers)} active records.")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="code-example-clean-up-orphaned-records"&gt;Code example: Clean up orphaned records&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A common use case is identifying and deleting records that are no longer needed. This is critical for In-Memory tier feature groups, where orphaned records persist indefinitely:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

featurestore_runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-featurestore-runtime")

# Step 1: Enumerate all record identifiers
all_ids = []
next_token = None
while True:
    params = {"FeatureGroupName": "session-features", "MaxResults": 100}
    if next_token:
        params["NextToken"] = next_token
    response = featurestore_runtime.list_records(**params)
    all_ids.extend(response["RecordIdentifiers"])
    next_token = response.get("NextToken")
    if not next_token:
        break

# Step 2: Compare against your application's active session list
active_sessions = get_active_sessions()  # Your application logic
orphaned = [rid for rid in all_ids if rid not in active_sessions]

# Step 3: Delete orphaned records
for record_id in orphaned:
    featurestore_runtime.delete_record(
        FeatureGroupName="session-features",
        RecordIdentifierValueAsString=record_id,
        EventTime="2026-06-05T12:00:00Z",
    )

print(f"Deleted {len(orphaned)} orphaned records.")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pagination-behavior"&gt;Pagination behavior&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Page size: Configurable through MaxResults (default 10, maximum 100).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Token format: Opaque, encrypted string. Do not parse or construct tokens. Pass them through unchanged.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ordering: Results are not guaranteed to be in any particular order.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Concurrent writes: If records are written or deleted during pagination, you may observe duplicates or gaps. This is documented behavior.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Token scope: Tokens are tied to a specific feature group and account and cannot be reused across either.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-considerations-1"&gt;Key considerations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Record identifiers only. The current release returns record identifiers without feature values. Use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_GetRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GetRecord&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchGetRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BatchGetRecord&lt;/a&gt; to retrieve full records for the identifiers you need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Automatic filtering. The API excludes soft-deleted records, expired records (Standard tier TTL), and internal system keys (In-Memory tier). You see only active, retrievable records.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;IAM permission. The caller must have sagemaker:ListRecords permission on the feature group ARN.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both tiers supported. ListRecords works identically from the caller’s perspective regardless of whether the feature group uses Standard or In-Memory storage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="putting-it-together"&gt;Putting it together&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These two APIs complement each other naturally. Consider a compliance workflow that verifies complete data deletion for a user across multiple feature groups:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import boto3

featurestore_runtime = boto3.client("sagemaker-featurestore-runtime")

feature_groups = ["user-profiles", "click-history", "purchase-signals"]
user_to_delete = "user-789"

# Step 1: Find and delete the user across all feature groups
for fg_name in feature_groups:
    all_ids = []
    next_token = None
    while True:
        params = {"FeatureGroupName": fg_name, "MaxResults": 100}
        if next_token:
            params["NextToken"] = next_token
        response = featurestore_runtime.list_records(**params)
        all_ids.extend(response["RecordIdentifiers"])
        next_token = response.get("NextToken")
        if not next_token:
            break

    if user_to_delete in all_ids:
        featurestore_runtime.delete_record(
            FeatureGroupName=fg_name,
            RecordIdentifierValueAsString=user_to_delete,
            EventTime="2026-06-05T23:59:59Z",
        )
        print(f"Deleted '{user_to_delete}' from {fg_name}")

# Step 2: Log the deletion event using BatchWriteRecord
featurestore_runtime.batch_write_record(
    Entries=[
        {
            "FeatureGroupName": "deletion-audit-log",
            "Record": [
                {"FeatureName": "request_id", "ValueAsString": "del-001"},
                {"FeatureName": "event_time", "ValueAsString": "2026-06-05T23:59:59Z"},
                {"FeatureName": "user_id", "ValueAsString": user_to_delete},
                {"FeatureName": "status", "ValueAsString": "completed"},
                {"FeatureName": "feature_groups_cleaned", "ValueAsString": "3"},
            ],
            "TargetStores": ["OnlineStore", "OfflineStore"],
        }
    ]
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleanup"&gt;Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete feature groups you created while following this walkthrough. For In-Memory tier feature groups, use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_ListRecords.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ListRecords&lt;/a&gt; to enumerate records and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_DeleteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeleteRecord&lt;/a&gt; to remove them before deleting the feature group.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_BatchWriteRecord.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;BatchWriteRecord&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_feature_store_ListRecords.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ListRecords&lt;/a&gt; provide key enhancements in the data plane of Amazon SageMaker Feature Store. BatchWriteRecord reduces the API call volume for high-throughput ingestion by up to 25x while preserving the EventTime-based ordering guarantees that keep your online store correct. ListRecords unlocks record discovery and lifecycle management. This is critical for In-Memory tier customers who previously had no way to enumerate or clean up their data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, these APIs support patterns that were previously difficult or impossible: bulk ingestion pipelines with fewer connections and lower latency, compliance workflows that can verify complete data deletion, and operational tooling that can browse feature group contents in real time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For more information, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/feature-store.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Feature Store documentation&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_Operations_Amazon_SageMaker_Feature_Store_Runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Feature Store API reference&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_OfflineStoreConfig.html#sagemaker-Type-OfflineStoreConfig-DisableGlueTableCreation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;offline store configuration documentation&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/07/amzn-sgm-feature-store-batch-write-list/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;What’s New announcement&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For background on Feature Store capabilities, explore these related posts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/understanding-the-key-capabilities-of-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Understanding the Key Capabilities of Amazon SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerate-ml-feature-pipelines-with-new-capabilities-in-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accelerate ML Feature Pipelines with new capabilities in Amazon SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/using-streaming-ingestion-with-amazon-sagemaker-feature-store-to-make-ml-backed-decisions-in-near-real-time/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Using Streaming Ingestion with Amazon SageMaker Feature Store&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Decathlon runs demand forecasting at scale with Chronos-2</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-decathlon-runs-demand-forecasting-at-scale-with-chronos-2/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vianney Bruned]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 28 Aug 2026 16:22:30 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">dae4053b37d7ed7b3612288d4b8268c30de807fd</guid>

					<description>Decathlon, one of the world's largest sporting goods retailers, forecasts weekly demand for tens of thousands of products across multiple continents. Learn how they deployed Chronos-2 on AWS to improve forecast accuracy by 11-15 points while cutting operational complexity and running weekly inference for about $0.03 on CPU-only instances.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Vianney Bruned, Filippo Giruzzi, Belkiss Saidi, and Carlos Ramirez from Decathlon.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon is one of the world’s largest sporting goods retailers, with more than 100,000 teammates and 400 million users worldwide. The company relies on accurate demand forecasting at scale to support the availability of the appropriate products in each store at the time customers need them. After evaluating multiple time series foundation models (TSFMs), Decathlon selected &lt;a href="https://www.amazon.science/blog/introducing-chronos-2-from-univariate-to-universal-forecasting" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Chronos-2&lt;/a&gt; as a core component of their forecasting stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we share the architecture Decathlon uses to run Chronos-2 at scale on AWS, the business impact on Decathlon’s supply chain operations, and practical lessons learned for other companies that want to adopt foundation models for forecasting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decathlons-forecasting-challenge"&gt;Decathlon’s forecasting challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Accurate demand forecasting is the backbone of retail supply chain operations. For Decathlon, this challenge is amplified by the sheer scale and diversity of their business: tens of thousands of products spanning over 80 sports, sold across multiple continents with highly seasonal demand patterns. A pair of ski gloves and a surfboard have fundamentally different demand signals, yet both must be forecasted accurately to avoid stockouts or overstock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon’s forecasting system predicts the weekly sales quantity of all products on two critical horizons. The first is a 12-week replenishment window used by purchase planners to order goods from industrial partners. The second is a 52-week strategic horizon for long-term stock projection and capacity planning. The forecasting system runs weekly across both horizons. It’s deployed across multiple supply zones, including Europe, India, China, South East Asia (SEA), Latin America (LATAM), and soon the Middle East and Africa. Each zone covers up to 25,000 products.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="previous-approach-and-its-limitations"&gt;Previous approach and its limitations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon’s demand forecasting system evolved over several years:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2021–2024&lt;/strong&gt;: A hybrid approach using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/deepar.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI DeepAR&lt;/a&gt; for the short-term forecast horizon (weeks 1–16) and Holt-Winters exponential smoothing for the longer-term horizon (weeks 17–52). DeepAR was retrained weekly to adapt to recent trend shifts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2024 onwards&lt;/strong&gt;: Introduction of &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1912.09363" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT)&lt;/a&gt; with covariates, offering improved long-horizon accuracy.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While these approaches served Decathlon well, they came with operational overhead: the system required weekly re-training and could not easily scale to new regions without extra engineering effort. The team needed a solution that could deliver higher accuracy with lower operational complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-chronos-2-model-evaluation-and-selection"&gt;Why Chronos-2: Model evaluation and selection&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The rise of time series foundation models (FMs) promised pre-trained models that could outperform classical approaches without training from scratch on domain-specific data. But a critical question remained: &lt;em&gt;would these models work on Decathlon’s specific retail datasets?&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To answer this, Decathlon designed a &lt;a href="https://medium.com/decathlondigital/the-tsfm-showdown-whos-winning-the-forecasting-battle-at-decathlon-e3ef17f3f247" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;rigorous, large-scale benchmark&lt;/a&gt; on its own retail data, evaluating multiple TSFMs against its production baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="benchmark-design"&gt;Benchmark design&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluation&lt;/strong&gt;: 101 rolling cutoffs spanning nearly 2 years (week 48 of 2022 to week 44 of 2024).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scale&lt;/strong&gt;: Approximately 25,000 unique products per cutoff (39,000 unique product time series across the full evaluation period).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Horizon&lt;/strong&gt;: 12-week and 52-week horizons at weekly frequency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Primary metric&lt;/strong&gt;: Weighted Absolute Percentage Error (WAPE) at 12-week and 52-week horizons, supplemented by root mean square error (RMSE), bias, and pairwise winning rates.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For full benchmark results including comparisons across multiple TSFMs, see &lt;a href="https://medium.com/decathlondigital/the-tsfm-showdown-whos-winning-the-forecasting-battle-at-decathlon-e3ef17f3f247" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Decathlon’s detailed analysis on Medium&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-findings"&gt;Key findings&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon evaluated multiple TSFMs in both zero-shot and fine-tuned configurations. In &lt;a href="https://medium.com/decathlondigital/the-tsfm-showdown-whos-winning-the-forecasting-battle-at-decathlon-e3ef17f3f247" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Decathlon’s published benchmark results&lt;/a&gt;, Chronos-2 fine-tuned consistently outperformed all other evaluated models across both horizons. Even in zero-shot mode, it matched or surpassed the fully trained production baseline. Fine-tuning further reduced forecast error by several percentage points.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Zero-shot viability&lt;/strong&gt;: Several TSFMs exceeded or approached the performance of Decathlon’s existing production model (retrained weekly) without any domain-specific training.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tuning delivered significant gains&lt;/strong&gt;: Even with a low-frequency fine-tuning schedule (once every 6 months), fine-tuning significantly improved performance. Chronos-2 fine-tuned showed the lowest error across both short-term (12-week) and long-term (52-week) horizons.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Computational efficiency&lt;/strong&gt;: Chronos-2 can run on both CPUs and GPUs, and met the benchmark’s efficiency requirement of under 2 minutes of inference per cutoff for 25,000 products.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond raw accuracy, Chronos-2’s native covariate support through its group attention mechanism was a key differentiator. Unlike most TSFMs that require workarounds, Chronos-2 natively incorporates covariates. The combination of leading accuracy, architectural elegance for covariate handling, and efficient fine-tuning made Chronos-2 the clear choice for Decathlon’s production stack.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-architecture"&gt;Solution architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon’s production deployment of Chronos-2 on AWS is designed to be efficient, cost-efficient, and reliable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the high-level architecture of the demand forecasting pipeline. A PySpark data preparation pipeline assembles the input time series. Every 6 months, a fine-tuning job built on &lt;a href="https://auto.gluon.ai/stable/tutorials/timeseries/forecasting-chronos.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AutoGluon&lt;/a&gt; adapts Chronos-2 to the latest data and registers the resulting model in an &lt;a href="https://mlflow.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MLflow&lt;/a&gt; model registry. In the intervening weeks, this step is skipped. The inference pipeline fetches the latest registered model and runs weekly batch forecasts, and a PySpark exposition pipeline delivers the forecasts to downstream consumers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ML-21294-1.png" alt="Decathlon’s forecasting pipeline on AWS: data prep, AutoGluon fine-tuning, MLflow model registry, and weekly batch inference" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: High-level architecture of Decathlon’s demand forecasting pipeline on AWS&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="infrastructure-and-deployment-pattern"&gt;Infrastructure and deployment pattern&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Component&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Details&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compute&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/m6i/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;m6i.8xlarge&lt;/a&gt; (CPU-based inference), &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/g5/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;g5.4xlarge&lt;/a&gt; (GPU-based fine-tuning)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Execution mode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Weekly batch inference&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inference runtime&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~40 seconds for 7,000 time series (LATAM), ~75 seconds for 15,000 time series (SEA)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Batch size&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~12,000 time series per execution&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Chronos-2 fine-tuned with LoRA through &lt;a href="https://auto.gluon.ai/stable/tutorials/timeseries/forecasting-chronos.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AutoGluon&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tuning cadence&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every 6 months&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regions served&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SEA, LATAM (Middle East and Africa planned)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EC2&lt;/a&gt; instances for batch inference, triggered by &lt;a href="https://docs.databricks.com/aws/en/jobs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Databricks&lt;/a&gt; jobs. Data pipelines are orchestrated through &lt;a href="https://airflow.apache.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Airflow&lt;/a&gt; on Decathlon’s existing data platform. The fine-tuning process uses &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.09685" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA)&lt;/a&gt; through the AutoGluon Chronos integration, allowing efficient adaptation to Decathlon’s domain without full model re-training. The model is fine-tuned automatically every 6 months on the latest data, and the different models are logged and versioned per supply zone with specific hyperparameters through &lt;a href="https://mlflow.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MLflow&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="chronos-2-technical-overview"&gt;Chronos-2 technical overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.amazon.science/blog/introducing-chronos-2-from-univariate-to-universal-forecasting" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Chronos-2&lt;/a&gt; is an encoder-only transformer closely following the T5 encoder design. It’s available in multiple variants, including the base model (&lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/amazon/chronos-2" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;amazon/chronos-2&lt;/a&gt;) with 120M parameters and a small model (&lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/autogluon/chronos-2-small" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;autogluon/chronos-2-small&lt;/a&gt;) with 28M parameters. Unlike the original Chronos, which quantized values into discrete tokens, Chronos-2 applies robust scaling to each series. It then splits each series into non-overlapping patches that are mapped to real-valued embeddings through a residual network. Forecasts are produced as continuous quantiles by a quantile head. The key architectural innovation is the alternating attention pattern. Each transformer block alternates between time attention (along the temporal axis within a single series) and group attention (across series within a group at each patch index).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates this design. Related time series and their covariates are grouped together. Information is exchanged both along time within each series and across the series in a group, allowing native multivariate forecasting with covariates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ML-21294-2.png" alt="Chronos-2 pipeline showing native covariate support" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Chronos-2 architecture for native multivariate forecasting with covariates&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="getting-started-with-chronos-2"&gt;Getting started with Chronos-2&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As of this writing, Chronos-2 models have been downloaded over 120 million times from &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/collections/amazon/chronos-models-and-datasets-65f1791d630a8d57cb718444" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face&lt;/a&gt; and are available for &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; customers through &lt;a href="https://auto.gluon.ai/cloud/stable/tutorials/foundation-model-timeseries.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AutoGluon-Cloud&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following code adapted from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/amazon-science/chronos-forecasting/tree/main/notebooks" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Chronos-2 quickstart notebook on GitHub&lt;/a&gt;, demonstrates how to run inference with covariate support. In production, Decathlon uses &lt;a href="https://auto.gluon.ai/stable/tutorials/timeseries/forecasting-chronos.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the AutoGluon Chronos integration&lt;/a&gt; for fine-tuning and inference orchestration with a slightly different API:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import pandas as pd
from chronos import BaseChronosPipeline, Chronos2Pipeline

# Load the Chronos-2 pipeline
pipeline: Chronos2Pipeline = BaseChronosPipeline.from_pretrained(
    "amazon/chronos-2",
    device_map="cpu"
)

# Historical data
df = pd.read_csv("sales.csv")  # columns: item_id, timestamp, sales, price, store_count

# Data available during the forecast horizon
future_df = pd.read_csv("future.csv")  # columns: item_id, timestamp, price, store_count

prediction_length = 52
forecast = pipeline.predict_df(df, future_df, target="sales", prediction_length=prediction_length)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fine-tuning-with-autogluon"&gt;Fine-tuning with AutoGluon&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon uses AutoGluon-TimeSeries to fine-tune and serve Chronos-2. With AutoGluon, you can streamline the end-to-end machine learning workflow, from data preparation to model training and deployment. Using its TimeSeries module’s high-level API, you can handle data formatting, covariate management, and fine-tuning with LoRA in a few lines of code:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# pip install autogluon.timeseries
from autogluon.timeseries import TimeSeriesDataFrame, TimeSeriesPredictor

tsdf = TimeSeriesDataFrame.from_data_frame(df, id_column="item_id", timestamp_column="timestamp")
future_tsdf = TimeSeriesDataFrame.from_data_frame(future_df, id_column="item_id", timestamp_column="timestamp")

predictor = TimeSeriesPredictor(
    prediction_length=52,  # forecast horizon
    target="sales",  # column to forecast
    known_covariates_names=["price", "store_count"],  # features known in the future
)

predictor.fit(
    tsdf,
    hyperparameters={
        "Chronos2": {
            "model_path": "amazon/chronos-2",
            "fine_tune": True,  # Turn on fine-tuning; if False the model is used in zero-shot mode
        }
    },
)

predictor.predict(tsdf, known_covariates=future_tsdf)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-and-business-impact"&gt;Results and business impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon measured the impact of the Chronos-2 deployment along three dimensions: forecast accuracy, business outcomes, and operational efficiency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="forecast-accuracy-improvements"&gt;Forecast accuracy improvements&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon’s deployment of Chronos-2 fine-tuned has delivered significant accuracy gains across both regions and horizons, in comparison with the previously used legacy forecasting tool:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Region&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Horizon&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Previous WAPE&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Chronos-2 WAPE&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;WAPE Reduction&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SEA&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;12 weeks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;39%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;28%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;11 pp&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LATAM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;12 weeks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;53%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;38%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;15 pp&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SEA&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;52 weeks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;44%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;38%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6 pp&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LATAM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;52 weeks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;55%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;46%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;9 pp&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;pp = percentage points&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="business-impact"&gt;Business impact&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each percentage point of WAPE improvement at the 12-week horizon translates to:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;0.3 days of inventory savings&lt;/strong&gt;, reducing working capital and warehousing costs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;0.3 points of product availability&lt;/strong&gt;, fewer stockouts on shelves.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;0.4 points of sales increase&lt;/strong&gt; for each point of availability gained, which is approximately 0.12 points of sales per WAPE point (average across all zones).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With 11–15 points of WAPE improvement at the replenishment horizon, the compounding effect on inventory efficiency, availability, and revenue is substantial.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="operational-efficiency"&gt;Operational efficiency&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond accuracy, the migration to Chronos-2 reduced operational overhead across several dimensions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before (TFT)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;After (Chronos-2)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deployment time new region&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~6 months (three people)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2–3 months (data scientist, ML engineer, data analyst)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inference runtime&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;10 min (10k series) to 15 min (25k series) including re-training&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~40s (7K series) to ~75s (15K series)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tuning frequency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Weekly&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every 6 months&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Engineering complexity&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;High (multi-seed training and inference)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Low (single model, LoRA fine-tuning, no GPU constraint)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reduction in deployment time from 6 months to 2–3 months is particularly impactful for Decathlon’s expansion into new markets. With Chronos-2, the team can deploy forecasting capabilities to a new region by running fine-tuning on local historical data with no architecture redesign required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="production-timeline"&gt;Production timeline&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Chronos-2 is already in production for the South East Asia and Latin America supply zones, which are the regions the preceding results are drawn from. Decathlon is now extending the solution across its remaining supply zones, with full multi-zone production rollout targeted for 2026 and the Middle East and Africa regions as the next expansion targets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion-and-lessons-learned"&gt;Conclusion and lessons learned&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Decathlon’s journey demonstrates that time series foundation models, and Chronos-2 in particular, are ready for production-grade retail demand forecasting. By combining a rigorous model evaluation process with efficient fine-tuning with LoRA, Decathlon achieved 11–15 points of WAPE improvement at the 12-week horizon. At the same time, the team reduced deployment time from 6 months to 2–3 months per region. For retailers evaluating foundation models for forecasting, Decathlon’s experience offers a practical blueprint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="lessons-learned"&gt;Lessons learned&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Benchmark on your own data&lt;/strong&gt;: Global leaderboards are useful for shortlisting candidates, but model rankings can differ significantly on domain-specific data. In Decathlon’s case, some models that ranked higher globally performed worse on retail distribution datasets. Testing across 25,000 products and 101 cutoffs also revealed that Chronos-2’s straightforward fine-tuning with AutoGluon was a decisive advantage, turning a strong zero-shot model into the clear leader.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fine-tuning unlocks the full potential&lt;/strong&gt;: Even infrequent fine-tuning (every 6 months) with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) measurably improves accuracy over zero-shot inference. The combination of a strong pre-trained foundation with domain adaptation is the winning formula.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Start with a focused approach, iterate&lt;/strong&gt;: Decathlon began with a single fine-tuned model in production and is progressively adding covariates. This pragmatic approach reduces risk while delivering value early.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Foundation models democratize forecasting&lt;/strong&gt;: Running Chronos-2 on a single m6i.8xlarge CPU instance with inference times of 40–75 seconds for 7,000–15,000 time series makes forecasting at this scale accessible without GPU infrastructure. At approximately $0.03 per weekly inference run, the compute cost is negligible.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Looking ahead, Decathlon plans to deploy a Mixture of Experts (MoE) ensembling approach in production. &lt;a href="https://medium.com/decathlondigital/enhancing-time-series-foundation-models-at-decathlon-from-covariates-to-robust-ensembling-6c65d11ecfdd" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Their benchmarks show&lt;/a&gt; that combining multiple TSFMs consistently outperforms the single best expert, with other models still winning on roughly 40 percent of products. The team is also expanding to Middle East and Africa regions and integrating external data such as price or weather as covariates. In addition, they are exploring Chronos-2’s cross-learning capabilities for cold-start products.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To explore Chronos-2 for your own forecasting use cases or to learn more about Chronos-2, visit the &lt;a href="https://www.amazon.science/blog/introducing-chronos-2-from-univariate-to-universal-forecasting" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Science blog post&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.15821" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;research paper&lt;/a&gt;, or try the &lt;a href="https://github.com/amazon-science/chronos-forecasting/tree/main/notebooks" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;quickstart notebook&lt;/a&gt;. For fine-tuning with AutoGluon, see the &lt;a href="https://auto.gluon.ai/stable/tutorials/timeseries/forecasting-chronos.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AutoGluon Chronos tutorial&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/footer&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Spreading the load: How Salesforce met Multi-AZ HA with SageMaker Inference Components</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/spreading-the-load-how-salesforce-met-multi-az-ha-with-sagemaker-inference-components/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rielah De Jesus]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 28 Aug 2026 16:20:40 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8dcb0dfaec8adf0cacbdfe39cb8d995c20776278</guid>

					<description>Learn how Salesforce used Amazon SageMaker AI Inference Component placement (the SchedulingConfig parameter) to distribute model copies across multiple Availability Zones, meeting their Multi-AZ high availability compliance requirements without sacrificing the cost efficiency of multi-model co-hosting.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;When Salesforce set out to make &lt;a href="https://www.salesforce.com/agentforce/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentforce&lt;/a&gt; (Salesforce’s AI foundation for agents) highly available (HA) across multiple Availability Zones (AZs), the team faced a gap. Amazon SageMaker AI Inference Components (ICs) could cut GPU costs, but their default placement didn’t guarantee the Multi-AZ resilience Salesforce’s compliance bar required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For Salesforce, the ICs delivered an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/optimizing-salesforces-model-endpoints-with-amazon-sagemaker-ai-inference-components/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;8x reduction in infrastructure costs&lt;/a&gt; by co-hosting multiple models on shared GPUs. However, this cost win introduced a new question: how do you make IC endpoints highly available across multiple AZs?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post explores how Salesforce used the new IC Placement capability (surfaced through the &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; parameter in the &lt;code&gt;CreateInferenceComponent&lt;/code&gt; API) to meet their Multi-AZ HA compliance requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-single-points-of-failure-in-ic-deployments"&gt;The challenge: Single points of failure in IC deployments&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By default, the SageMaker placement algorithm optimizes each IC deployment operation independently, distributing new copies evenly across instances without considering AZ balance. Even with a multi-AZ endpoint, this per-operation view means copies of a specific model can end up unevenly distributed across AZs, creating potential single points of failure:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instance-level failure:&lt;/strong&gt; A single instance crash takes down all copies of a model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AZ-level failure:&lt;/strong&gt; An AZ outage makes the entire model unavailable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compliance risk:&lt;/strong&gt; Salesforce mandates 2-AZ support for every production model. Default placement for ICs, optimized for cost alone, did not yet meet their internal 2-AZ compliance bar.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-solution-schedulingconfig"&gt;The solution: SchedulingConfig&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS introduced the &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; parameter in the &lt;code&gt;CreateInferenceComponent&lt;/code&gt; API. It gives customers fine-grained control over IC copy placement across instances and AZs. Two key sub-parameters drive the HA behavior:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AvailabilityZoneBalance:&lt;/strong&gt; Controls cross-AZ distribution, balancing copies evenly across Availability Zones with configurable imbalance tolerance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;PlacementStrategy (within each AZ):&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; distributes copies across as many instances as possible for fault isolation. &lt;code&gt;BINPACK&lt;/code&gt; packs copies onto fewer instances for utilization efficiency.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="code-example-deploying-an-ic-with-multi-az-ha-placement"&gt;Code example: Deploying an IC with Multi-AZ HA placement&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scenario:&lt;/strong&gt; Salesforce has a multi-AZ SageMaker endpoint with 4 instances distributed evenly across 2 Availability Zones (2 instances in AZ-1, 2 instances in AZ-2). The team wants to deploy a model with 4 IC copies so that they are spread across both AZs for high availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following &lt;code&gt;CreateInferenceComponent&lt;/code&gt; call deploys the model with &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; placement and AZ balancing:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.create_inference_component(
    InferenceComponentName='salesforce-llm-ic-ha',
    EndpointName='salesforce-multiaz-endpoint',
    VariantName='AllTraffic',
    Specification={
        'ModelName': 'salesforce-einstein-llm-v2',
        'ComputeResourceRequirements': {
            'NumberOfAcceleratorDevicesRequired': 1,
            'MinMemoryRequiredInMb': 65536
        },
        'DataCacheConfig': {'EnableCaching': True},
        'SchedulingConfig': {
            'PlacementStrategy': 'SPREAD',
            'AvailabilityZoneBalance': {
                'EnforcementMode': 'PERMISSIVE',
                'MaxImbalance': 1
            }
        }
    },
    RuntimeConfig={'CopyCount': 4}
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt;, SageMaker distributes 4 copies across 4 instances: 2 in AZ-1 and 2 in AZ-2. When you set &lt;code&gt;MaxImbalance&lt;/code&gt; to 1, you configure the system to tolerate at most a 1-copy difference between any two AZs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For lighter models needing only 2 copies, &lt;code&gt;MaxImbalance: 0&lt;/code&gt; enforces strict balance: exactly 1 copy per AZ:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Lighter model: strict 1-copy-per-AZ balance
'SchedulingConfig': {
    'PlacementStrategy': 'SPREAD',
    'AvailabilityZoneBalance': {
        'EnforcementMode': 'PERMISSIVE',
        'MaxImbalance': 0
    }
},
RuntimeConfig={'CopyCount': 2}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scaling-while-preserving-az-balance"&gt;Scaling while preserving AZ balance&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you perform scale-out and scale-in operations, SageMaker helps you maintain AZ balance through your configured &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; parameters. When you scale out, SageMaker places new copies to maintain even AZ distribution. When you reduce &lt;code&gt;CopyCount&lt;/code&gt;, SageMaker symmetrically removes copies across AZs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Important:&lt;/strong&gt; Never set &lt;code&gt;CopyCount&lt;/code&gt; to 1 for HA-critical models. A single copy can only reside in one AZ, which means you would immediately break your 2-AZ compliance requirements.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;update_response = client.update_inference_component(
    InferenceComponentName='salesforce-llm-ic-ha',
    RuntimeConfig={'CopyCount': 8}  # Scale out: 4 per AZ
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; governs the placement plan for each individual scale operation. For ongoing consolidation and rebalancing over time (for example, after repeated scale-in/scale-out cycles), configure the endpoint’s &lt;code&gt;ScaleInPolicy&lt;/code&gt; with the &lt;code&gt;CONSOLIDATION&lt;/code&gt; strategy. With this configuration, a background sweeper periodically consolidates IC copies and releases idle instances while honoring AZ balance constraints.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Step 1: Create an endpoint config with CONSOLIDATION ScaleInPolicy
client.create_endpoint_config(
    EndpointConfigName='salesforce-multiaz-endpoint-config-v2',
    ProductionVariants=[{
        'VariantName': 'AllTraffic',
        'InstanceType': 'ml.g5.xlarge',
        'InitialInstanceCount': 4,
        'ManagedInstanceScaling': {
            'Status': 'ENABLED',
            'MinInstanceCount': 2,
            'MaxInstanceCount': 8,
            'ScaleInPolicy': {
                'Strategy': 'CONSOLIDATION'
            }
        }
    }]
)

# Step 2: Update the endpoint to use the new config
client.update_endpoint(
    EndpointName='salesforce-multiaz-endpoint',
    EndpointConfigName='salesforce-multiaz-endpoint-config-v2'
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-three-pillars-of-the-placement-algorithm"&gt;The three pillars of the placement algorithm&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The new placement algorithm introduced three fundamental improvements. Each directly addressed Salesforce’s HA requirements:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Balanced final distribution:&lt;/strong&gt; The algorithm considers the balance of the final distribution rather than only immediate placement needs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Availability-aware distribution:&lt;/strong&gt; SageMaker evenly distributes copies across AZs on a best-effort basis. Endpoint and inference component update operations persist multi-AZ placement, so HA is preserved during model updates.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Within-AZ optimization:&lt;/strong&gt; Within each AZ, the &lt;code&gt;PlacementStrategy&lt;/code&gt; controls instance-level distribution. &lt;code&gt;BINPACK&lt;/code&gt; packs copies onto fewer instances to maximize GPU utilization. &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; distributes copies across as many instances as possible for maximum fault isolation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Salesforce chose &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; for Pillar 3, prioritizing fault isolation over packing density. This helps prevent a single instance failure from taking down multiple copies of the same model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="target-architecture-before-and-after"&gt;Target architecture: Before and after&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Continuing the preceding scenario: Salesforce’s endpoint has 4 instances across 2 AZs. Over time, the team deploys three ICs to this endpoint, each created in separate operations: IC1 (4 copies), IC2 (2 copies), and IC3 (2 copies).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Later, the team deploys IC3, a lighter model needing only 2 copies with strict AZ balance:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.create_inference_component(
    InferenceComponentName='salesforce-light-model-ic3',
    EndpointName='salesforce-multiaz-endpoint',
    VariantName='AllTraffic',
    Specification={
        'ModelName': 'salesforce-summarizer-v1',
        'ComputeResourceRequirements': {
            'NumberOfAcceleratorDevicesRequired': 1,
            'MinMemoryRequiredInMb': 16384
        },
        'SchedulingConfig': {
            'PlacementStrategy': 'SPREAD',
            'AvailabilityZoneBalance': {
                'EnforcementMode': 'PERMISSIVE',
                'MaxImbalance': 0
            }
        }
    },
    RuntimeConfig={'CopyCount': 2}
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;code&gt;MaxImbalance: 0&lt;/code&gt;, you configure the algorithm to target exactly 1 copy per AZ, which helps you keep IC3 available even if an entire AZ fails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates how the default placement and the new &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; placement differ when all three ICs are deployed to the same endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-20956-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-20956-1.png" alt="Default IC placement concentrating model copies in fewer Availability Zones compared to SchedulingConfig placement spreading copies evenly across two Availability Zones" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Default placement compared to SchedulingConfig placement across two Availability Zones&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; Models requiring multiple GPUs per copy (for example, large language models (LLMs) needing 4 accelerators) follow the same placement logic. &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; helps place each multi-GPU copy on a separate instance, and AZ balancing distributes them evenly across zones.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-considerations"&gt;Implementation considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections cover capacity planning, configuration, and monitoring for Multi-AZ HA deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="capacity-reservations"&gt;Capacity reservations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS strongly recommends On-Demand Capacity Reservations (ODCR) for capacity planning in AZ-constrained Regions. Salesforce pre-provisions reserved GPU capacity in each target AZ to help verify balanced IC placement. Without ODCR, on-demand capacity constraints may limit the distribution you want in high-demand Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The placement algorithm supports partial deployment. If capacity constraints prevent full AZ balance, SageMaker still places copies on available instances rather than failing the operation entirely. This means the feature is usable even without ODCR. However, balance may not be optimal.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="key-configuration-parameters"&gt;Key configuration parameters&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the recommended parameter values for Multi-AZ HA placement:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameter&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;PlacementStrategy&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;SPREAD&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Distribute copies across instances (not packed)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;EnforcementMode&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;PERMISSIVE&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Best-effort AZ balance. Places copies wherever available if balance cannot be achieved (currently the only enforcement mode)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MaxImbalance&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;0 or 1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Max copy count difference between any two AZs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CopyCount&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;≥ 2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Minimum 2 copies required for 2-AZ compliance&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ManagedInstanceScaling.MinInstanceCount&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;≥ 2&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Minimum 2 instances to span 2 AZs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;DataCacheConfig.EnableCaching&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;True&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Faster scale-out by caching model artifacts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;RoutingConfig.RoutingStrategy&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;LEAST_OUTSTANDING_REQUESTS&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Automatic failover routing across AZs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;DataCacheConfig&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;RoutingConfig&lt;/code&gt; are general endpoint/IC configuration features independent of the IC placement strategy. They are included in this table because they complement HA deployments, but they are not part of the &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; placement feature itself.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="monitoring-az-balance-with-sagemaker-ai-insights"&gt;Monitoring AZ balance with SageMaker AI Insights&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker AI Insights provides built-in observability for IC placement health. With detailed observability enabled, the following metrics help validate and maintain Multi-AZ HA:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AZ skew (Reliability tab):&lt;/strong&gt; Shows distribution imbalance percentage across your fleet. Use this to detect drift from balanced placement after scaling events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IC copy count per AZ:&lt;/strong&gt; Confirms each inference component maintains the expected copy distribution across Availability Zones.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rebalancing events and duration:&lt;/strong&gt; Tracks when SageMaker automatically rebalances copies and how long the operation takes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Insufficient Capacity Error (ICE) count per AZ:&lt;/strong&gt; Monitors ICE events by AZ and instance type. You can use this to help determine if ODCR capacity may need adjustment.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;You can access these metrics in the SageMaker AI Insights dashboard and through Amazon CloudWatch. A detailed walkthrough of observability for IC-based endpoints will be covered in an upcoming blog post.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows an example of the SageMaker AI Insights Reliability tab with AZ balance metrics:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-20956-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-20956-2.png" alt="SageMaker AI Insights Reliability tab showing AZ balance metrics and copy distribution across Availability Zones" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: SageMaker AI Insights Reliability tab with AZ balance metrics&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By using the IC Placement capability, Salesforce’s AI team achieved:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-AZ HA compliance:&lt;/strong&gt; Every model deployment in Salesforce’s fleet satisfies their 2-AZ support requirement.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Eliminated single points of failure:&lt;/strong&gt; No model can be fully taken offline by a single instance or AZ failure.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Preserved cost efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; Multi-model co-hosting continues to deliver infrastructure cost savings, while &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; placement maximizes fault isolation across instances.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resilient scaling:&lt;/strong&gt; Scale-up and scale-down operations preserve multi-AZ distribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Persistent HA through updates:&lt;/strong&gt; Model updates no longer risk breaking AZ balance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-takeaways-for-enterprise-ai-teams"&gt;Key takeaways for enterprise AI teams&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Salesforce’s journey to Multi-AZ HA with SageMaker Inference Components offers several lessons. Enterprise AI teams should consider the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Design HA at the IC level, not just the endpoint level. Even with a multi-AZ endpoint, IC copies can be concentrated in a single AZ without explicit placement controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use &lt;code&gt;SchedulingConfig&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;SPREAD&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;AvailabilityZoneBalance&lt;/code&gt; for workloads with high availability requirements. This is the recommended starting configuration for most models with HA requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Pre-provision capacity with ODCR. To achieve balanced AZ placement, you must provision available capacity in each target AZ. Don’t rely on on-demand capacity for HA-critical deployments.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set minimum &lt;code&gt;CopyCount&lt;/code&gt; to 2 and minimum instance count to 2 as the HA baseline.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Never scale to &lt;code&gt;CopyCount: 1&lt;/code&gt; for HA-critical models. A single copy can only reside in one AZ, which means you would immediately break your 2-AZ compliance requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Monitor IC distribution continuously using SageMaker AI Insights. Track AZ Skew, IC Copy Count per AZ, and Rebalancing Events on the Reliability tab to detect and remediate imbalance before it becomes a reliability issue.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;IC Placement gives enterprise AI teams the control they need to meet strict availability requirements without sacrificing cost efficiency. For Salesforce, this capability unlocked Multi-AZ HA compliance for their production Agentforce models. It also serves as a reference pattern for enterprises running AI workloads with strict availability requirements on SageMaker.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI workloads are increasingly business-critical with strict uptime requirements. The ability to control exactly how model copies are distributed across infrastructure is no longer a nice-to-have. It’s a fundamental requirement.&lt;/p&gt;
