		<title>Build agentic creative workflows with Amazon Quick and fal</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-agentic-creative-workflows-with-amazon-quick-and-fal/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[James Wu]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 27 Aug 2026 23:04:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3c57691eafa2d6c582d04d51debd371df1ea818e</guid>

					<description>Creative teams produce more assets than ever, but fragmented tools and manual context transfer slow production. This post shows how to build a reusable agent harness with Amazon Quick and fal, connected through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), using two hands-on workflows: an eight-panel storyboard and a music-video concept prototype.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Creative teams face growing demand for more assets, formats, and revisions, while their scripts, references, models, and outputs often remain fragmented across tools. Creators must repeatedly transfer context and assemble results manually. With &lt;a href="https://www.superside.com/blog/creative-bottlenecks" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;78% of creative leaders&lt;/a&gt; saying demand exceeds their teams’ capacity, faster generation alone does not solve the underlying workflow problem.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To address this problem, media enterprises need a reusable agent harness that preserves context, supports long-running media jobs, and introduces human review at key creative gates. The harness combines reusable workflow instructions, shared tool infrastructure, and orchestration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post demonstrates that approach through two workflows: creating an eight-panel storyboard and prototyping a music-video concept. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/quick/?trk=0ea79374-057c-4897-84f0-5fe792905a8f&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=EAIaIQobChMIgcWq-5mhlgMV-TqtBh2s3xnLEAAYASAAEgKF6PD_BwE&amp;amp;gads_camp=23532473728&amp;amp;gads_ag=195603221991&amp;amp;gads_ad=806967542617&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20quick&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9031958&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23532473728&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp9Wgb8wW2x247zAb3-6yKORv&amp;amp;gclid=EAIaIQobChMIgcWq-5mhlgMV-TqtBh2s3xnLEAAYASAAEgKF6PD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; serves as the agent workspace, fal provides production-ready generative media capabilities, and &lt;a href="https://modelcontextprotocol.io/docs/2026-07-28/getting-started/intro" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model Context Protocol&lt;/a&gt; (MCP) provides the standard interface connecting them. The following sections explain each component before the workflow walkthroughs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="understanding-the-agent-harness"&gt;Understanding the agent harness&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The media workflow harness has four reusable layers: Amazon Quick as the agent surface and orchestrator, Skills as standardized workflow instructions, MCP as the shared tool contract, and fal as the specialized generative media infrastructure. Together, these layers provide creators with one guided workflow that can be reused across campaigns, teams, and future agent experiences.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-quick"&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick is an agentic AI workspace for research, business insights, workflow automation, and no-code application building. In the workflows described in this post, Amazon Quick serves as the orchestration layer. It interprets the creator’s request, plans the work, and retains approved decisions. It then invokes the appropriate external tools and presents outputs for review.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="skills"&gt;Skills&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Creators can also capture repeatable processes as Skills. A Skill can encode instructions such as confirming the art direction before generation, creating character references before producing scenes, and pausing for approval at defined quality gates. This helps teams reuse a creative process instead of rebuilding it for each campaign.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fal.ai"&gt;fal&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;fal is a generative media platform for developers and enterprises, providing production-ready access to &lt;span&gt;&lt;a href="https://fal.ai/explore" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;more than 1,000 models&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/span&gt; for image, video, audio, 3D and other media-generation tasks. These models power creative workflows spanning character exploration, reference-guided image generation, audio production, and video creation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="model-context-protocol"&gt;Model Context Protocol&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that allows AI applications to connect to external tools and data sources through a consistent interface. fal makes its generative media capabilities available through an MCP server, and Amazon Quick uses its MCP client to discover and invoke those tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="benefits-for-creative-teams"&gt;Benefits for creative teams&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Connecting Amazon Quick and fal through MCP provides the following benefits:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A unified creative workspace&lt;/strong&gt;: Creators can plan, generate, compare, and refine assets without moving between separate generation tools for every modality.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Context retained across steps&lt;/strong&gt;: Approved style choices, character references, story beats, and format requirements can remain part of the working context as the project progresses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human judgment at creative gates&lt;/strong&gt;: The agent can pause after key stages so that creators can choose a direction before downstream assets are produced.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Access to multiple generative media models from fal&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams can use different production-ready models for image, audio, and video tasks through one MCP connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Repeatable production practices&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Quick Skills can encode the team’s preferred steps and approval criteria, allowing other team members to follow the same process.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Faster concept validation&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams can evaluate a storyboard, style direction, or video concept before committing to a full production cycle.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This architecture separates workflow orchestration from media generation. Amazon Quick serves as the agentic workspace and MCP client, while fal hosts the MCP server and provides generative media tools. Amazon Quick Skills can capture reusable workflow instructions, and MCP provides a consistent interface for discovering and invoking fal capabilities across similar media workflows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Creators describe the outcome they want in Amazon Quick, which invokes the appropriate tools through the configured fal MCP connector. fal processes each supported request and returns the generated asset for review. Creators can then approve the result, request revisions, or generate additional assets. Capabilities such as multi-stage planning, reference retention, and approval checkpoints depend on the Skill instructions, available conversational context, and tools exposed by the connector.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of Amazon Quick as MCP client connecting through MCP to the fal.ai MCP server and generation models" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Architecture and dataflow for an iterative creative workflow using Amazon Quick, MCP, and fal&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-the-integration"&gt;Set up the integration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow these steps to connect fal to Amazon Quick through MCP and verify the available media-generation tools before starting the example workflows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to Amazon Quick, with the Amazon Quick desktop application installed and signed in.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A fal account and an API key that you can use for the integration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permission to add and configure a remote Model Context Protocol (MCP) connector in Amazon Quick.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-obtain-a-fal.ai-api-key"&gt;Step 1: Obtain a fal API key&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sign in to your fal account and open the fal dashboard. Create a new API key for the integration or retrieve an existing one. Store the key securely, and don’t include it in screenshots, source files, or shared documents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-connect-the-fal.ai-mcp-server-to-amazon-quick"&gt;Step 2: Connect the fal MCP server to Amazon Quick&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the Amazon Quick desktop application and go to &lt;strong&gt;Settings&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Capabilities&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Connectors&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Add MCP Server: Remote&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;URL&lt;/strong&gt;, enter &lt;code&gt;https://mcp.fal.ai/mcp&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For the header, enter &lt;code&gt;Authorization: Key YOUR_FAL_API_KEY&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div class="mceTemp"&gt;&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_137901" style="width: 491px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-137901" loading="lazy" class="wp-image-137901 size-full" style="border: 1px solid #CCCCCC" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-2-1-1.png" alt="" width="481" height="800"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-137901" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: MCP setup configuration on Amazon Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-validate-the-fal.ai-tools"&gt;Step 3: Validate the fal tools&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Save the connector and confirm that Amazon Quick can discover the tools exposed by the fal MCP server. After the connection is active, start a new conversation in Amazon Quick.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img style="border: 1px solid #CCCCCC" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-3.png" alt="Amazon Quick conversation confirming the discovered fal.ai MCP tools after the server connects" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Validating the fal MCP server tools in Amazon Quick&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-workflow-1-storyboard-production"&gt;Example workflow 1: Storyboard production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A marketing team needs an eight-panel storyboard for a product launch. In a traditional process, this means a brief, a designer, three rounds of feedback, and a week of elapsed time. In Amazon Quick, the same work can happen in one interactive session as an agentic loop. Quick plans, generates, presents options, waits for approval, and continues. If something does not work, the creator responds and Quick iterates. There is no handoff and no context loss.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This walkthrough shows one possible pre-production flow. The models, number of iterations, and approval gates can vary based on the creator’s preferences.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Create an 8-panel storyboard for a futuristic racing-prototype launch. Use an anime style and comic-grid layout. The story follows a young racer from pre-test preparation and the prototype reveal through a high-speed test drive.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Follow this sequence:&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Lock the creative direction:&lt;/strong&gt; Confirm the art style, comic-grid format, aspect ratio, and restrictions.&lt;/em&gt; &lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Approve the story plan:&lt;/strong&gt; Draft eight story beats, a shot list, and the character description.&lt;/em&gt; &lt;em&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Lock the character design:&lt;/strong&gt; After approval, inspect the available fal models and generate two labeled character options for comparison. Once I select one, create its multi-view reference sheet and wait for approval before generating storyboard panels.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-1-lock-the-style-format-and-story-plan"&gt;Step 1: Lock the style, format, and story plan&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick confirms the anime style, comic-grid format, aspect ratio, and visual restrictions, then carries those constraints into later calls. It produces a written eight-beat outline, shot list, and character description.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;No images are generated at this stage. The creator can revise the story, pacing, character treatment, or visual direction before approving the plan.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-2-explore-and-lock-the-character-design"&gt;Step 2: Explore and lock the character design&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the written plan is approved, Quick inspects the available fal models and generates two alternative character designs for comparison. The creator selects the option that best matches the intended campaign.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-4.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-4.jpg" alt="First model’s character option for the young racer in anime style" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-5.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-5.jpg" alt="Second model’s character option for the young racer in anime style" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 4: A/B dual-model character generation, where the creator compares two model outputs and selects the reference that best matches their vision&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick then creates a reference package showing the selected character from the front and rear, in multiple poses with a helmet, and in a close-up facial view.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The creator reviews the hair, racing suit, facial appearance, colors, and accessories. No storyboard panel is generated until this reference is approved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-5.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-5.jpg" alt="Approved racer character shown from the front" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-7.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-7.jpg" alt="Racer character shown from the rear" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-8.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-8.jpg" alt="Close-up facial view of the racer character" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 5: Multi-angle character reference sheet passed as input to every panel generation to maintain visual identity&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-3-reference-guided-panel-generation"&gt;Step 3: Reference-guided panel generation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the character design is approved, Quick uses FLUX.1 Kontext to generate each panel, supplying the approved character references with every call to preserve visual identity across scenes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-9.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-9.jpg" alt="Storyboard panel showing the racer during pre-test preparation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-10.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-10.jpg" alt="Storyboard panel showing the prototype reveal" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-11.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-11.jpg" alt="Storyboard panel showing the high-speed test drive" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 6: Three reference-guided storyboard panels showing consistent character identity across scenes&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The creator reviews the sequence and requests any revisions in the same conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-4-rendering"&gt;Step 4: Rendering&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After all eight panels are approved, Quick arranges them in shot-list order and adds captions to create an interactive storyboard viewer. The creator can review and revise individual panels before presenting the storyboard as a comic grid, scroll, or slideshow. These layouts reuse the approved images without regenerating them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-5-convert-the-validated-workflow-into-a-reusable-skill"&gt;Step 5: Convert the validated workflow into a reusable Skill&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The creator validates the process and identifies storyboard production as a task the team will repeat. Then the creator can ask Quick to create an AI Storybuilding Skill from the conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Skill captures:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Creative-direction and format confirmation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Written story beats and shot planning.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Planning approval before image generation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A/B character-design comparison.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Multi-angle character-reference creation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Character approval before panel generation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reference-guided storyboard generation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Creator review and revision.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Skills in Amazon Quick are shareable workflows. One person captures the validated process, and other team members run it with the same quality gates. After the Skill is reviewed and saved, Quick can activate the AI Storybuilding Skill in a new conversation and collect missing information. Quick pauses at the planning and character-approval stages, but creators can still change the story, style, models, or number of iterations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="example-workflow-2-music-video-concept-prototyping"&gt;Example workflow 2: Music video concept prototyping&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The second workflow reuses a saved Skill to take a music-video concept from brief to lip-synced preview.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-1-activate-the-existing-skill"&gt;Step 1: Activate the existing Skill&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The team already has a shared &lt;strong&gt;Music Video Prototyping Skill&lt;/strong&gt; in Amazon Quick. The Skill encodes brief collection, shot planning, music generation, character references, creative approval gates, motion and lip-sync testing, model-substitution approval, and asset delivery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The creator starts with:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Use the Music Video Prototyping Skill to create a 60-second country music video concept. Generate the song, design the characters, and produce a lip-synced video preview.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick activates the Skill, collects any missing requirements, and follows the saved workflow and approval gates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-2-plan-the-production"&gt;Step 2: Plan the production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quick breaks the concept into individual shots. It identifies the performance close-ups that need lip-sync, the establishing shots and B-roll, the required character references, and the audio needed for each sequence. The creator reviews the shot plan before media generation begins.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-3-generate-music-and-visual-references"&gt;Step 3: Generate music and visual references&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After approval, Quick generates a country music track with audio models available through fal, creates character references for visual consistency, and produces the scene stills and audio snippets needed for the planned shots.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-4-produce-and-review-a-lip-sync-test"&gt;Step 4: Produce and review a lip-sync test&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before generating the complete video, Quick produces a short lip-synced performance clip. The creator can evaluate facial movement, timing, performance quality, and visual consistency before continuing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 640px;" class="wp-video"&gt;
 &lt;video class="wp-video-shortcode" id="video-137868-1" width="640" height="360" preload="metadata" controls="controls"&gt;
  &lt;source type="video/mp4" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/artifacts/DBSBlogs/ml-21711/video.mp4?_=1"&gt;
 &lt;/video&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Figure 7: A music-video production session in Amazon Quick showing the lip-sync test and options to storyboard the video, generate scene stills, test motion, or save the validated assets&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_137876" style="width: 295px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-137876" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-137876" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/25/ML-21711-13-1.png" alt="" width="285" height="500"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-137876" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 8: A frame from the generated lip-sync test showing the country performer singing into a microphone while playing guitar&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="step-5-continue-toward-the-complete-concept-preview"&gt;Step 5: Continue toward the complete concept preview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After validating the song, character direction, and lip-sync test, the creator can continue prototyping the video. Quick can storyboard the remaining sequence, generate scene stills, test an animated motion shot, and save approved assets. The creator can then assemble these assets into a longer concept preview.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="operational-considerations"&gt;Operational considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Prefer JPEG output when transparency and lossless quality aren’t required, because smaller assets can reduce data transferred through the MCP connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Monitor fal usage and costs during generation-intensive workflows. For long sessions, save approved assets externally and process work in manageable batches.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Treat the fal API key as a secret. Store it only in the connector configuration, restrict access, rotate it if exposed, and do not include it in prompts, files, screenshots, or logs. Because fal is a third-party service, send only approved content, follow your organization’s data-handling requirements, and review outputs before sharing.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon Quick and fal integration demonstrates one operator-facing agent harness pattern for media. With Amazon Quick, you can orchestrate the workflow and capture reusable logic in Skills. MCP provides a shared tool contract, while fal supplies image, audio, and video capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The value of the integration isn’t limited to producing an individual image or clip. It comes from coordinating the complete creative loop: establish direction, generate alternatives, preserve approved references, pause for human judgment, and assemble a prototype. The storyboard workflow demonstrates an approval-gated production loop, while the music-video workflow shows how teams can prototype and validate visual and audio assets before assembling the complete video.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To try this pattern, follow the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/mcp-integration.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick MCP integration guide&lt;/a&gt; to configure the fal connector, validate the available tools, and begin with one approval-gated creative workflow.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Introducing OpenAI models on Amazon Bedrock for in-country inferencing in India</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-openai-models-on-amazon-bedrock-for-in-country-inferencing-in-india/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sahil Verma]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 27 Aug 2026 18:36:08 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a396074812fe8a0090f88e23aed4ff7add4538d3</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock now supports the OpenAI GPT-5.6 models, Terra and Luna, in India with India geographic cross-Region inference. If you have local data processing requirements, you can now use these models at scale while Amazon Bedrock keeps inference requests and data within India.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.aboutamazon.in/news/aws/openai-gpt-terra-luna-available-india-bedrock" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock now supports the OpenAI GPT-5.6 models, Terra and Luna, in India,&lt;/a&gt; with India geographic cross-Region inference. If you have local data processing requirements in India, including in financial services, healthcare, and the public sector, you can now use these OpenAI models at scale. Amazon Bedrock processes inference requests and data within India.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both models offer a 1-million-token context window, accept text and image input, and produce text output. Your applications can process long documents, large code bases, and mixed text-and-image workloads in a single request. The processing never leaves the country.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through how India geographic cross-Region inference works from the Mumbai and Hyderabad Regions. We also show how to get started from the Amazon Bedrock console and with code, using the OpenAI Responses API, OpenAI Chat Completions API, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="india-geographic-cross-region-inference"&gt;India geographic cross-Region inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference automatically routes inference requests across multiple AWS Regions to help improve throughput, without you having to manage capacity in each Region yourself. It’s primarily a capacity mechanism. Instead of being bound to one Region’s capacity, your requests draw on a broader pool of compute. That helps you maintain throughput and consistent performance under load, which matters most during traffic peaks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With India geographic cross-Region inference, Amazon Bedrock routes requests only within the India geography across Regions such as Asia Pacific (Mumbai) Region (ap-south-1) and Asia Pacific (Hyderabad) Region (ap-south-2). You can scale to meet demand while keeping data processed within India to meet data residency requirements. You call the profile from either India Region as the source, and Amazon Bedrock routes the request to the destination India Region based on capacity. For the most current information about model availability in each Region, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Regional availability by models&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference works through inference profiles. You call a profile ID as the model, and it defines the model and the AWS Regions Amazon Bedrock can route your request to. The India geographic inference profiles keep that routing within India. There are two profiles:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra&lt;/code&gt; for GPT-5.6 Terra.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;in.openai.gpt-5.6-luna&lt;/code&gt; for GPT-5.6 Luna.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With inference profiles, billing and quota consumption are tracked against your account in the source Region, regardless of which backend Region handled the request. Amazon CloudWatch and AWS CloudTrail record log entries in the source Region only, so your monitoring stays in one place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Choosing between Amazon Bedrock Mantle and Runtime endpoints &lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For new applications, we recommend the bedrock-runtime endpoint. It supports the Bedrock-native&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-api.html"&gt;InvokeModel&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;and&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/conversation-inference.html"&gt;Converse&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;APIs, the OpenAI-compatible&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/bedrock-mantle.html#bedrock-mantle-responses"&gt;Responses&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;and&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-chat-completions-mantle.html"&gt;Chat Completions&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;APIs, and the Anthropic&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-messages-api.html"&gt;Messages&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;API, and it is where Amazon Bedrock features such as&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/guardrails.html"&gt;Guardrails&lt;/a&gt;,&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-routing.html"&gt;intelligent prompt routing&lt;/a&gt;, and&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference.html"&gt;cross-Region inference&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;are available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="data-residency"&gt;Data residency&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The India geographic profile keeps inference within India. Requests route only between ap-south-1 and ap-south-2. Your input prompts and output results might move between those two Regions. Data is encrypted in transit across the Amazon network. Amazon Bedrock uses a zero data retention (ZDR) data security model. This means that by default, Amazon Bedrock does not store model inputs or outputs. However, for certain models, including GPT-5.6, content flagged by the Amazon Bedrock automated abuse-detection classifiers is retained for offline abuse detection. Please see&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/abuse-detection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Abuse detection&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide for more details.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="global-cross-region-inference"&gt;Global cross-Region inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock also offers global cross-Region inference and global inference profiles (prefixed &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt;) in India that route to supported commercial AWS Regions worldwide for maximum capacity. You send your request to the India Region endpoint, either Asia Pacific (Mumbai) ap-south-1 or Asia Pacific (Hyderabad) ap-south-2, using the global profile ID as the model ID. Amazon Bedrock then decides which destination Region serves the request. Global cross-Region inference supports OpenAI GPT-5.6 models, including Sol, Terra, and Luna. However, if your workload has local data processing requirements, use the India (prefixed &lt;code&gt;in.&lt;/code&gt;) profiles instead, because they keep inference within the country. To read more about global cross-Region inference, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introduce-cross-region-inference-for-openai-gpt-5-6-models-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Introduce cross-Region inference for OpenAI GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="access-gpt-5.6-models-from-the-amazon-bedrock-console"&gt;Access GPT-5.6 models from the Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can try GPT-5.6 in the text playground in the Amazon Bedrock console, which requires no coding or SDK setup. You can send prompts, adjust inference parameters, and switch between variants to get a feel for each model before you integrate the API.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; in a Region where the models are available, such as Asia Pacific (Mumbai) ap-south-1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the navigation pane, under &lt;strong&gt;Test&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Playground&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Select model&lt;/strong&gt; in the middle of the page.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Search for OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra, select &lt;strong&gt;IN OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra&lt;/strong&gt;, and choose &lt;strong&gt;Apply&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a prompt and choose &lt;strong&gt;Run&lt;/strong&gt; to generate a response.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ML-21755-1.png" alt="Selecting the OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra model from the Amazon Bedrock Playground console" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: The OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra model selected in the Amazon Bedrock console playground&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="call-gpt-5.6-models-with-the-openai-responses-api"&gt;Call GPT-5.6 models with the OpenAI Responses API&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock natively support the OpenAI Responses API format. If your application already calls OpenAI models, you can point your existing OpenAI SDK client at the Amazon Bedrock endpoint in either India Region, Mumbai or Hyderabad, and pass an India geographic inference profile ID as the model parameter.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For authentication, Amazon Bedrock accepts either standard AWS credentials or an Amazon Bedrock API key. The API key path is the straightforward fit for the OpenAI SDK, which passes it as the bearer token. For production, generate short-term API keys programmatically with the &lt;code&gt;aws-bedrock-token-generator&lt;/code&gt; package. It derives a bearer token from your existing AWS credentials, so no static key needs to be stored.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example uses Asia Pacific (Mumbai) ap-south-1 endpoint and India geographic inference profile ID to call OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra on Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from aws_bedrock_token_generator import provide_token
from openai import OpenAI

# Point the OpenAI SDK at the Amazon Bedrock OpenAI-compatible
# endpoint in the Asia Pacific (Mumbai) Region.
client = OpenAI(
    base_url="https://bedrock-runtime.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com/openai/v1",
    api_key=provide_token(region="ap-south-1"),  # short-term Amazon Bedrock API key, valid up to 12 hours
)

# Geographic (India) inference profile ID for GPT-5.6 Terra.
model_id = "in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra"  # "in.openai.gpt-5.6-luna" for luna
response = client.responses.create(
    model=model_id,
    input="Extract the payment due date and total amount from the invoice text that follows, and return them as JSON. &amp;lt;invoice text&amp;gt;",
    max_output_tokens=512,
)
print(response.output_text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Responses API uses a single &lt;code&gt;input&lt;/code&gt; field and returns the generated text in &lt;code&gt;output_text&lt;/code&gt;, with the output limit set through &lt;code&gt;max_output_tokens&lt;/code&gt;. The same client also works with the Chat Completions API, useful if your application already uses this format.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="controlling-reasoning-depth"&gt;Controlling reasoning depth&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To control reasoning depth, set the optional &lt;code&gt;reasoning&lt;/code&gt; parameter, for example &lt;code&gt;reasoning={"effort": "low"}&lt;/code&gt;. GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock support the following reasoning effort levels: &lt;code&gt;none&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;low&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;medium&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;high&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;xhigh&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;max&lt;/code&gt;. For more information, see the related post &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/get-started-with-openai-gpt-5-6-sol-terra-and-luna-on-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Get started with OpenAI GPT-5.6 Sol, Terra, and Luna on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;. Omitting the parameter uses the model default.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    input="Extract the payment due date and total amount from the invoice text that follows, and return them as JSON. &amp;lt;invoice text&amp;gt;",
    reasoning={"effort": "high"},  # none | low | medium | high | xhigh | max
    max_output_tokens=512,
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the full list of supported parameters, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-cards-openai.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI model documentation in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="multi-turn-conversations-with-server-side-state"&gt;Multi-turn conversations with server-side state&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;store=True&lt;/code&gt; to have Amazon Bedrock retain the response server-side, then reference it on the next turn with &lt;code&gt;previous_response_id&lt;/code&gt;. You send only the new turn. You don’t resend the prior conversation history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;first = client.responses.create(
    model="in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    input="Remember this: my favorite number is 42. Reply with just 'stored'..",
    max_output_tokens=50,
    store=True,
)

second = client.responses.create(
    model="in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    previous_response_id=first.id,  # the model recalls the earlier turn
    input="what is my favourite number?",
    max_output_tokens=50,
    store=True,
)

print(second.output_text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you chain with &lt;code&gt;previous_response_id&lt;/code&gt;, the response you chain from must have been created with &lt;code&gt;store=True&lt;/code&gt;. Chaining from an unstored response returns an error.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="streaming-responses"&gt;Streaming responses&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For streaming, set &lt;code&gt;stream=True&lt;/code&gt; and iterate over the events:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;stream = client.responses.create(
    model="in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    input="Draft a short status update for a delayed shipment, in a polite and direct tone.",
    max_output_tokens=512,
    stream=True,
)

for event in stream:
    if event.type == "response.output_text.delta":
        print(event.delta, end="")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="call-gpt-5.6-models-with-the-converse-api"&gt;Call GPT-5.6 models with the Converse API&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you prefer the AWS SDK and SigV4 authentication over an API key, the Amazon Bedrock Converse API supports GPT-5.6 models with the same unified interface it provides for other models on Amazon Bedrock. &lt;code&gt;ConverseStream&lt;/code&gt; covers the streaming case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
  &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-python"&gt;import boto3

# Standard AWS credentials (SigV4), no API key needed.
client = boto3.client("bedrock-runtime", region_name="ap-south-1")
model_id = "in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra"  # India Geo inference profile
response = client.converse(
    modelId=model_id,
    messages=[
        {
            "role": "user",
            "content": [{"text": "Classify this support ticket as billing, technical, or account: &amp;lt;ticket text&amp;gt;"}],
        }
    ],
    inferenceConfig={"maxTokens": 512},
)
print(response["output"]["message"]["content"]
&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
 &lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prompt-caching"&gt;Prompt caching&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your prompts might share a long, stable prefix, such as a system instruction, a knowledge base excerpt, or a set of few-shot examples. When they do, GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock support prompt caching. Cached reads are billed at a 90 percent discount compared to uncached input tokens. This adds up quickly for Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) and agent workloads that repeat the same context across many turns. You can find details on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock prompt caching documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Prompt caching works with the India geographic inference profiles, so you keep the savings under the India data-residency boundary. Prompt caching runs in two modes, explicit and implicit. With implicit caching, Amazon Bedrock places the cache breakpoints for you automatically. Note that the minimum prefix length is 1,024 tokens. With explicit caching, you mark the cache boundary yourself for precise control, and the cached prefix stays warm for at least 30 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.responses.create(
    model="in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
    prompt_cache_key="ticket-agent-ver123",  # same key across all requests
    input=[
        {
            "type": "message",
            "role": "developer",
            "content": [{
                "type": "input_text",
                "text": SYSTEM_INSTRUCTIONS,  # long, static: guidelines, KB excerpts (&amp;gt;= 1,024 tokens)
                "prompt_cache_breakpoint": {"mode": "explicit"},
            }],
        },
        {
            "type": "message",
            "role": "user",
            "content": [{
                "type": "input_text",
                "text": user_question,  # changes on every request
            }],
        },
    ],
    extra_body={"prompt_cache_options": {"mode": "explicit"}},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every response tells you what the cache did, in &lt;code&gt;usage.input_tokens_details&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;details = response.usage.input_tokens_details
print(f"cached: {details.cached_tokens}, written: {details.cache_write_tokens}")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;On the first call, you will see &lt;code&gt;cache_write_tokens&lt;/code&gt; populated as the prefix is stored. On subsequent calls, the same tokens come back as &lt;code&gt;cached_tokens&lt;/code&gt;, billed at the cache-read rate. Because these counts are part of &lt;code&gt;input_tokens&lt;/code&gt; rather than added on top, a cached token is counted and charged once.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="iam-permissions-for-india-geographic-cross-region-inference"&gt;IAM permissions for India geographic cross-Region inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To allow an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role to invoke GPT-5.6 models through the India geographic inference profile, grant the role access to three resources. These are the India geographic inference profile itself, the foundation model (FM) in the source Region, and the foundation model in each destination Region listed in the profile (ap-south-1 and ap-south-2). You can use the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/aws-managed-policy/latest/reference/AmazonBedrockLimitedAccess.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AmazonBedrockLimitedAccess&lt;/a&gt; managed policy or create your own.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example grants permission to use the GPT-5.6 Terra model through the India geographic inference profile. The source Region is ap-south-1, and the destination Regions are ap-south-1 and ap-south-2. Replace &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with your account ID, and duplicate the resources for &lt;code&gt;in.openai.gpt-5.6-luna&lt;/code&gt; if the role needs both models. If you source from Hyderabad, use the ap-south-2 inference-profile ARN in the first statement. The foundation-model resources in the second statement stay the same, because they already list both India Regions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "GrantGeoCrisInferenceProfileAccess",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "bedrock:InvokeModel*"
            ],
            "Resource": [
                "arn:aws:bedrock:ap-south-1:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:inference-profile/in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
                "arn:aws:bedrock:ap-south-1:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:project/default"
            ]
        },
        {
            "Sid": "GrantGeoCrisModelAccess",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "bedrock:InvokeModel*"
            ],
            "Resource": [
                "arn:aws:bedrock:ap-south-1::foundation-model/openai.gpt-5.6-terra",
                "arn:aws:bedrock:ap-south-2::foundation-model/openai.gpt-5.6-terra"
            ],
            "Condition": {
                "StringEquals": {
                    "bedrock:InferenceProfileArn": "arn:aws:bedrock:ap-south-1:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT&amp;gt;:inference-profile/in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra"
                }
            }
        },
        {
            "Sid": "AllowBearerTokenAuth",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": ["bedrock:CallWithBearerToken"],
            "Resource": "*"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first statement grants access to the India geographic inference profile for requests originating from the source Region. The second grants access to the foundation model in the source Region and in every destination Region listed in the profile. This access is scoped to that one profile through the &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InferenceProfileArn&lt;/code&gt; condition. These policies grant &lt;code&gt;bedrock:CallWithBearerToken&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; on the &lt;code&gt;project/default&lt;/code&gt; resource. The OpenAI Responses and Chat Completions APIs use this resource to authenticate with an Amazon Bedrock API key and run inference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="service-control-policy-scp"&gt;Service Control Policy (SCP)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your organization uses Service Control Policies to block unused Regions, your Region-specific SCP conditions must allow every destination Region listed in the geographic inference profile.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For the India geographic inference profile, both &lt;code&gt;ap-south-1&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;ap-south-2&lt;/code&gt; must be permitted. Blocking either destination Region causes cross-Region inference to fail even though the source Region remains accessible.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;Setting up Codex with GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Codex can use the geographic inference profiles through Amazon Bedrock Runtime. Install the latest Codex CLI (codex-cli 0.149.1 or later) to use the Amazon Bedrock Runtime model provider. For organizations whose identity provider is Okta, Auth0, Microsoft Entra ID, Amazon Cognito, or AWS IAM Identity Center, the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-openai-on-aws/tree/main/aws-oidc-auth" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS OIDC Auth Helper repository&lt;/a&gt; provides a sample credential helper.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First,&amp;nbsp;follow&amp;nbsp;the&amp;nbsp;guide&amp;nbsp;to&amp;nbsp;configure your identity provider, the corresponding AWS federation resource, and an IAM role&amp;nbsp;carrying&amp;nbsp;the Bedrock permissions&amp;nbsp;shown earlier.&amp;nbsp;Then&amp;nbsp;add a named profile to&amp;nbsp;&lt;code&gt;~/.aws/config&lt;/code&gt;,&amp;nbsp;so the helper does not&amp;nbsp;replace&amp;nbsp;credentials&amp;nbsp;resolved by&amp;nbsp;the default&amp;nbsp;profile.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;[profile &amp;lt;AWS_OIDC_PROFILE&amp;gt;] 
credential_process = &amp;lt;ABSOLUTE_PATH_TO_CREDENTIAL_PROCESS&amp;gt; --profile &amp;lt;OIDC_HELPER_PROFILE&amp;gt; 
region = ap-south-1
output = json 
&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This&amp;nbsp;federation helper exchanges an OIDC token for temporary AWS credentials, and Codex&amp;nbsp;reads&amp;nbsp;through&amp;nbsp;the standard AWS&amp;nbsp;credential chain with no further configuration.&amp;nbsp;Next,&amp;nbsp;create or update&amp;nbsp;~/.codex/config.toml&amp;nbsp;and reference the AWS profile,&amp;nbsp;see the&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://developers.openai.com/codex/config-reference/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Codex configuration reference&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;for other supported settings:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;model = "in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra" 
model_provider = "amazon-bedrock-runtime" 
model_reasoning_effort = "high" 
 
[model_providers.amazon-bedrock-runtime.aws] 
profile = "&amp;lt;AWS_OIDC_PROFILE&amp;gt;" 
region = "ap-south-1" 
&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the helper does not have a valid cached session, it opens the configured sign-in page in your browser. After you authenticate, the helper returns temporary AWS credentials through&amp;nbsp;credential_process. Requests are signed with AWS SigV4.&amp;nbsp;When&amp;nbsp;the profile is backed by AWS IAM Identity Center,&amp;nbsp;the credentials are already short-term and rotate with the SSO session. To use&amp;nbsp;this in the Asia Pacific (Hyderabad)&amp;nbsp;Region, set the Region to&amp;nbsp;ap-south-2&amp;nbsp;in the AWS profile and Codex configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already have AWS credentials configured locally, Codex can use them directly. Point the Amazon Bedrock Runtime provider at an AWS profile and an India Region, and set the model to an India geographic inference profile ID.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create or update &lt;code&gt;~/.codex/config.toml&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="lang-code"&gt;  model = "in.openai.gpt-5.6-terra"
  model_provider = "amazon-bedrock-runtime"
  model_reasoning_effort = "high"

  [model_providers.amazon-bedrock-runtime.aws]
  profile = "default"
  region = "ap-south-1"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;The profile value is any profile in &lt;code&gt;~/.aws/config&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;~/.aws/credentials&lt;/code&gt;. In this case, it is shown as “default”, but you can use your own named profile if you keep multiple accounts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="monitoring-and-logging"&gt;Monitoring and logging&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The India geographic inference profiles work with the same account-level controls you already use for other models on Amazon Bedrock. Requests appear in &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model invocation logging&lt;/a&gt; the same way on-demand requests do. You can deliver those logs to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) or Amazon CloudWatch Logs. CloudWatch metrics for invocation count, token count, latency, throttles, and errors are published per inference profile. You can track GPT-5.6 models usage from Mumbai or Hyderabad without correlating logs across Regions yourself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To see which destination Region processed a given request, CloudTrail events include an &lt;code&gt;additionalEventData&lt;/code&gt; field with an &lt;code&gt;inferenceRegion&lt;/code&gt; key. For the India profile, this is always ap-&lt;code&gt;south-1&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;ap-south-2&lt;/code&gt;, which gives you an auditable record that inference stayed within India. Usage is itemized on AWS Cost Explorer and the AWS Cost and Usage Report. Spend attribution by model works the same way as for the rest of your Amazon Bedrock workloads.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock brings the OpenAI GPT-5.6 Terra and Luna models, with a 1-million-token context window, to customers building in India. The India geographic inference profiles are available on the bedrock-runtime&amp;nbsp; endpoint, which is the endpoint we recommend for new applications. With India geographic cross-Region inference, you can scale across the Mumbai and Hyderabad Regions while keeping inference within the country. You can call the models through the OpenAI Responses API, the OpenAI Chat Completions API, or the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. If you have an existing workload on the bedrock-mantle endpoint, it remains fully supported and no changes are required; to adopt the India geographic profiles, point that workload at the bedrock-runtime&amp;nbsp; endpoint in Mumbai or Hyderabad. Review the&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;&amp;nbsp;for current GPT-5.6 rates before sizing a production workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, review the model cards for GPT-5.6 Terra and Luna and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cross-region-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross-Region inference section&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock User Guide. Try a prompt in the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; playground in Mumbai or Hyderabad, then wire up the code examples shared earlier in this post. If you run into an issue or want to ask the community, &lt;a href="https://repost.aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS re:Post&lt;/a&gt; is a good place to search or post a question.&lt;/p&gt;
