			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Deepgram deepens Amazon SageMaker AI observability with Enhanced Metrics</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deepgram-deepens-amazon-sagemaker-ai-observability-with-enhanced-metrics/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Victor Wang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 27 Aug 2026 16:11:27 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3e8957b68753589eff32ed4d4989a283a55ca308</guid>

					<description>Self-hosted speech AI carries an observability trade-off: the numbers that drive capacity planning and cost management stay locked inside the vendor container. Deepgram closes that gap on Amazon SageMaker AI with two capabilities that land billing, usage, and per-GPU metrics directly in your own Amazon CloudWatch account.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Self-hosted speech AI has historically carried an observability trade-off. The service can tell you an endpoint is up and how many requests it served. The questions that actually drive capacity planning and cost management stay locked inside the vendor’s container: what you are billed for, which features your traffic uses, and what the inference engine is doing on each GPU.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you run Deepgram’s speech-to-text (STT) and text-to-speech (TTS) models on SageMaker AI, audio and transcripts stay inside your own AWS account. This can help support your data residency and compliance efforts without giving up a managed control plane for deployment, scaling, and monitoring. Your specific obligations depend on your own controls and assessments, so consult your compliance team and review the AWS shared responsibility model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram is closing the gap on billing, feature usage, and engine behavior with the following two innovations, available today on Deepgram SageMaker AI deployments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics&lt;/strong&gt;: Usage and billing metrics that the Deepgram container publishes directly into your Amazon CloudWatch account, with no agent, no sidecar, and no additional IAM permissions. These are the same consumed-unit values that drive AWS Marketplace metered billing, so you can reconcile your AWS bill against actual traffic down to the model and transport.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prometheus and OpenTelemetry support&lt;/strong&gt;: Engine-level Prometheus metrics scraped straight from the Deepgram container, and per-GPU accelerator and host metrics. Both are collected through SageMaker AI detailed observability and queryable with PromQL from CloudWatch, Grafana, or any Prometheus-compatible tool.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through both capabilities: how they work, what they show you that you couldn’t see before, and how to start using them on a Deepgram SageMaker AI endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deepgram-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai"&gt;Deepgram on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram’s speech models are available as model packages on AWS Marketplace and deploy as SageMaker AI real-time endpoints in your account. With SageMaker AI, you get baseline observability on your endpoints automatically: invocation metrics such as &lt;code&gt;ConcurrentRequestsPerModel&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;FirstChunkLatency&lt;/code&gt; in CloudWatch, container logs in Amazon CloudWatch Logs, and alarm-driven automatic scaling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Marketplace model packages run with network isolation. The container cannot make outbound connections, which is why security-conscious customers choose this deployment model. The same isolation, however, is what normally makes vendor-level telemetry hard. The container can’t push metrics to an external collector, and you can’t see inside the container. Both capabilities in this post work within that constraint: neither requires the container to open a network path, and both land the data in your own CloudWatch account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A note on terminology:&lt;/strong&gt; SageMaker AI has its own endpoint feature called &lt;em&gt;enhanced metrics&lt;/em&gt; (&lt;code&gt;EnableEnhancedMetrics&lt;/code&gt; in &lt;code&gt;MetricsConfig&lt;/code&gt;), which adds per-instance and per-GPU utilization dimensions to the standard endpoint metrics. The SageMaker AI feature is distinct from and complementary to &lt;strong&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics&lt;/strong&gt;, the Deepgram-published billing and usage metrics described in the following sections. This post covers the Deepgram capability. For the SageMaker AI capability, see &lt;a href="https://developers.deepgram.com/docs/observability-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deepgram’s SageMaker observability guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="innovation-1-billing-and-usage-transparency-with-deepgram-enhanced-metrics"&gt;Innovation 1: Billing and usage transparency with Deepgram Enhanced Metrics&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics answer two questions no standard endpoint metric can: exactly what you’re being billed for, and how your traffic actually uses Deepgram’s features.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-it-works-metrics-that-ride-the-logging-path"&gt;How it works: Metrics that ride the logging path&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Deepgram container writes &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonCloudWatch/latest/monitoring/CloudWatch_Embedded_Metric_Format_Specification.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CloudWatch Embedded Metric Format (EMF)&lt;/a&gt; records to container stdout. SageMaker AI already forwards container output to the endpoint’s CloudWatch log group, and CloudWatch Logs extracts EMF records into metrics automatically. The EMF path gives you the following benefits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No agent, sidecar, or collector to deploy.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No additional IAM permissions&lt;/strong&gt; beyond what the endpoint already has for logging.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Works under AWS Marketplace network isolation&lt;/strong&gt;, because metrics travel over the existing SageMaker-to-CloudWatch logging path rather than any outbound network connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Classic CloudWatch metrics&lt;/strong&gt;: They appear in &lt;code&gt;aws cloudwatch list-metrics&lt;/code&gt; and work with &lt;code&gt;get-metric-statistics&lt;/code&gt;, dashboards, alarms, and metric math, with nothing to enable on the endpoint configuration.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All dimensions are low-cardinality and contain no personally identifiable information (PII): no transcripts, TTS input, or per-request identifiers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics aggregate across the Deepgram endpoints in your AWS account and Region. The dimensions cover category, model, and transport, but not endpoint name or instance ID, so you cannot filter this stream to a single endpoint or instance. For per-endpoint, per-instance, or per-GPU breakdowns, use the Prometheus and OpenTelemetry metrics in the next section.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="reconcile-your-aws-marketplace-bill-the-deepgramsagemakerinference-namespace"&gt;Reconcile your AWS Marketplace bill: The Deepgram/SageMakerInference namespace&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The billing namespace emits one record per completed request, covering each streaming session, pre-recorded request, and TTS request. The &lt;code&gt;ConsumedUnits&lt;/code&gt; metric carries the same billable-unit values that drive AWS Marketplace metered billing. The following table lists the metrics this namespace publishes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unit&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ConsumedUnits&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Billable inference units for the request. Sum over a period is the total billed volume.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AudioDurationSeconds&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Seconds&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Duration of audio processed (speech-to-text).&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;CharCount&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Count&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Characters synthesized (text-to-speech).&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dimensions are published at three granularities (&lt;code&gt;[Category]&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;[Category, Model]&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;[Category, Model, Transport]&lt;/code&gt;), so you can answer &lt;em&gt;what did streaming STT cost this month&lt;/em&gt; and &lt;em&gt;how much of that was nova-3&lt;/em&gt; with the same namespace. For example, total consumed units per hour for streaming speech-to-text:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws cloudwatch get-metric-statistics \
--namespace Deepgram/SageMakerInference \
--metric-name ConsumedUnits \
--dimensions Name=Category,Value=stt_streaming \
--start-time $(date -u -d '24 hours ago' +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S) \
--end-time $(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S) \
--period 3600 \
--statistics Sum SampleCount \
--region YOUR_AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;SampleCount&lt;/code&gt; is the number of billed requests. Because these are ordinary CloudWatch metrics, you can build a finance-facing billing dashboard or set a budget alarm on consumed units.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="understand-how-your-endpoint-is-used-the-deepgramselfhosted-namespace"&gt;Understand how your endpoint is used: The Deepgram/SelfHosted namespace&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A second stream, emitted by the Deepgram API server itself, breaks raw usage down by method, model tier, and enabled feature, independent of billing. It answers product-level questions: how much traffic is streaming versus pre-recorded, which model tiers process the most audio, and which features (diarization, smart formatting, redaction, keyterm prompting) your applications actually enable. The following table lists the metrics in this namespace and the dimensions each one carries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimensions&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it tells you&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AudioMs&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Requests&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Deployment&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Method&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Volume by streaming compared to pre-recorded&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;TierAudioMs&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Deployment&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Tier&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Audio processed per model tier (for example, &lt;code&gt;nova-3&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;flux&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;FeatureAudioMs&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;FeatureTokens&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Deployment&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Feature&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Utilization per enabled feature (for example, &lt;code&gt;diarize&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;smart_format&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;redact&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;TtsCharacters&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Tokens&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;VoiceAgentMs&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;Deployment&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Method&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TTS, intelligence-feature, and voice-agent volume&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For example, how much audio ran with diarization enabled in the last 24 hours:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws cloudwatch get-metric-statistics \
--namespace Deepgram/SelfHosted \
--metric-name FeatureAudioMs \
--dimensions Name=Deployment,Value=sagemaker Name=Feature,Value=diarize \
--start-time $(date -u -d '24 hours ago' +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S) \
--end-time $(date -u +%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S) \
--period 3600 \
--statistics Sum \
--region YOUR_AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The usage stream is on by default and can be disabled with a single environment variable override in the endpoint configuration (&lt;code&gt;DEEPGRAM_API_01: emf.enabled=false&lt;/code&gt;). The billing stream cannot be disabled. It is part of the metering pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="innovation-2-engine-level-and-per-gpu-visibility-with-prometheus-and-opentelemetry"&gt;Innovation 2: Engine-level and per-GPU visibility with Prometheus and OpenTelemetry&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram containers serve a Prometheus metrics endpoint, and SageMaker AI &lt;strong&gt;detailed observability&lt;/strong&gt; runs an AWS managed &lt;a href="https://opentelemetry.io/docs/collector/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenTelemetry Collector&lt;/a&gt; on each instance backing the endpoint. The collector scrapes the container and exports the results to CloudWatch, and it exports per-GPU and host-level metrics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With detailed observability enabled, three metric sources publish to the CloudWatch OTel-compatible metric store. The following table describes each source and why it matters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Source&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example metrics&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why it matters&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;GPU (DCGM exporter)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;DCGM_FI_DEV_FB_USED&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-GPU series. On multi-GPU instances, each GPU reports separately, so a saturated device can’t hide behind a summed or averaged utilization number.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Host (node exporter)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;node_cpu_seconds_total&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;node_memory_MemTotal_bytes&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Standard Prometheus node-exporter metrics for each instance.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deepgram engine&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;engine_active_requests{kind="stream"}&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;engine_estimated_stream_capacity&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The collector scrapes the Deepgram container’s Prometheus endpoint directly. These are the same API and Engine metrics Deepgram’s self-hosted customers use for capacity planning, now available on SageMaker AI without running any collector yourself. See Deepgram’s &lt;a href="https://developers.deepgram.com/docs/metrics-guide" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Metrics Guide&lt;/a&gt; for the complete reference.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;engine_estimated_stream_capacity&lt;/code&gt; is the Deepgram engine’s own estimate of how many concurrent streams the instance can sustain. Comparing it against &lt;code&gt;engine_active_requests&lt;/code&gt; gives you an engine-reported headroom signal for scaling decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every series carries SageMaker resource labels, including &lt;code&gt;aws.sagemaker.endpoint.name&lt;/code&gt;, the variant name, and the instance ID. You can filter to a single endpoint, isolate one instance in a scaled-out fleet, or compare GPUs within an instance. Because the collector runs on the host, outside the model container, this works under AWS Marketplace network isolation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="enabling-it"&gt;Enabling it&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Detailed observability is on by default for newly created endpoints, publishing every 60 seconds. To set it explicitly, for example on an endpoint created before the feature launched, or to change the publish frequency, use &lt;code&gt;MetricsConfig&lt;/code&gt; on the endpoint configuration:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws sagemaker create-endpoint-config \
--endpoint-config-name YOUR_CONFIG_NAME \
--production-variants file://production-variants.json \
--metrics-config '{"EnableDetailedObservability": true, "MetricPublishFrequencyInSeconds": 60}' \
--region YOUR_AWS_REGION&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For an endpoint already serving traffic, create a new endpoint configuration and run &lt;code&gt;update-endpoint&lt;/code&gt;. The update is a blue/green deployment, and the endpoint stays in service.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="query-with-promql-from-cloudwatch-grafana-or-anything-prometheus-compatible"&gt;Query with PromQL from CloudWatch, Grafana, or anything Prometheus-compatible&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The metrics land in the CloudWatch OTel metric store and are queried with PromQL. In the CloudWatch console’s PromQL editor, filter per-GPU utilization to one endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-promql"&gt;DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_UTIL{"aws.sagemaker.endpoint.name"="YOUR_ENDPOINT_NAME"}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;CloudWatch also exposes a standard Prometheus-compatible HTTP API for these metrics, authenticated with SigV4:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;awscurl --service monitoring --region YOUR_AWS_REGION \
"https://monitoring.YOUR_AWS_REGION.amazonaws.com/api/v1/query?query=engine_active_requests"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you’ve standardized on Grafana or another Prometheus-native observability stack, you can point your existing tooling at your SageMaker AI fleet: no parallel monitoring stack, no export pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choosing-the-right-metric-stream"&gt;Choosing the right metric stream&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two innovations complement the built-in metrics of SageMaker AI rather than replacing them. The following table maps common questions to the metric stream that answers them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Question&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Where to look&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Is the endpoint healthy? Are requests erroring or slow to start?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Standard SageMaker metrics (&lt;code&gt;ConcurrentRequestsPerModel&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;FirstChunkLatency&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;Invocation5XXErrors&lt;/code&gt;)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What is my Marketplace bill going to be, and which models drove it?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;Deepgram/SageMakerInference&lt;/code&gt;, account-level)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Which features and model tiers does my traffic actually use?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics&lt;/strong&gt; (&lt;code&gt;Deepgram/SelfHosted&lt;/code&gt;, account-level)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;How saturated is each GPU? How much stream capacity remains on each instance of a specific endpoint?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prometheus/OTel through detailed observability&lt;/strong&gt; (DCGM and Deepgram engine metrics, per endpoint/instance/GPU)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="get-started"&gt;Get started&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics require no setup: they flow into your CloudWatch account from the moment a Deepgram SageMaker AI endpoint serves traffic. Detailed observability is on by default for new endpoints and takes one endpoint-configuration change to add to existing ones. To get started, use the following resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy Deepgram from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/marketplace/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Marketplace&lt;/a&gt; and follow &lt;a href="https://developers.deepgram.com/docs/deploy-amazon-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deploy Deepgram on Amazon SageMaker&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reference: &lt;a href="https://developers.deepgram.com/docs/enhanced-metrics-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deepgram Enhanced Metrics&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://developers.deepgram.com/docs/prometheus-otel-sagemaker" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prometheus &amp;amp; OpenTelemetry Metrics&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://developers.deepgram.com/docs/metrics-guide" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Metrics Guide&lt;/a&gt; for the complete list of Deepgram API and Engine metrics.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS documentation: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/monitoring-cloudwatch-detailed-observability.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Detailed observability for SageMaker AI endpoints&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Self-hosting speech AI no longer leaves a gap between the service’s request counters and the vendor’s invoice. Deepgram Enhanced Metrics land the exact units that drive AWS Marketplace billing in your own CloudWatch account, with zero infrastructure. The feature-level usage behind those units lands there too. With Prometheus and OpenTelemetry support through SageMaker AI detailed observability, the Deepgram engine’s own capacity and load metrics and per-GPU utilization are one PromQL query away in the tools your team already uses.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These capabilities are Deepgram’s latest investment in SageMaker AI as a preferred deployment option for self-hosted speech AI, following &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deepgram-enhances-amazon-sagemaker-ai-support-with-aws-iam-temporary-delegation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM Temporary Delegation&lt;/a&gt; for support access. They continue the commitment of making the day-two experience match the day-one experience. Deepgram models currently listed on SageMaker AI, including Nova, Flux, and Aura-2, come with a 14-day trial at no additional cost. Your team can stand up a real deployment in your own AWS account before committing. Running Deepgram models on SageMaker AI incurs charges for endpoint hosting, including GPU instances. It also incurs charges for Amazon CloudWatch logs and metrics and associated networking resources. While the Deepgram trial is offered at no additional cost, AWS infrastructure costs apply from the start of deployment. Review the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker AI pricing&lt;/a&gt; and use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-explorer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/a&gt; to monitor your spending.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Reduce ASR inference costs by 75% with NVIDIA MPS on Amazon EC2</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-asr-inference-costs-by-75-with-nvidia-mps-on-amazon-ec2/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Iman Abbasnejad]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 27 Aug 2026 16:05:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon EC2]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">61ebfd4bb75d20f4c4d64b731c1e86a4ebed0031</guid>

					<description>Serving automatic speech recognition (ASR) models at scale is costly when each request uses only a fraction of a GPU. Learn how NVIDIA CUDA Multi-Process Service (MPS) with NVIDIA Triton Inference Server on Amazon EC2 GPU instances cuts GPU infrastructure by 75% while holding sub-second latency at 92.1 requests per second per GPU.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is a collaboration between AWS, NVIDIA and&amp;nbsp;&lt;a href="https://www.heidihealth.com/"&gt;Heidi&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Reducing automatic speech recognition (ASR) inference costs on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2)&lt;/a&gt; becomes critical when GPU utilization per request is low but latency requirements are strict. A single ASR inference request typically uses only 15–20 percent of a GPU’s compute capacity, yet the default time-slicing behavior in NVIDIA CUDA® forces sequential access, leaving 80 percent of the hardware idle. Heidi Health is an AI Care Partner that processes over 2.4 million clinical consultations per week across 190 countries. To sustain sub-second transcription latency at peak traffic, this inefficiency forces the company to run 16 GPU instances.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fine-tuning-nvidia-nemotron-speech-asr-on-amazon-ec2-for-domain-adaptation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;a previous post&lt;/a&gt;, you learned how to fine-tune a Nemotron speech model, NVIDIA Parakeet TDT 0.6B V2 for clinical speech recognition. In this post, we focus on what comes after fine-tuning: serving that model efficiently. We demonstrate how NVIDIA CUDA Multi-Process Service (MPS), combined with NVIDIA Triton Inference Server&lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2122.png" alt="™" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt; on Amazon EC2 GPU instances, reduces GPU infrastructure requirements by 75 percent (from 16 instances to 4). This setup maintains sub-second latency at 92.1 requests per second (RPS) per GPU.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section covers the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The GPU utilization challenge.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The three available sharing mechanisms.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model-level optimizations with ONNX and TensorRT&lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2122.png" alt="™" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Request scheduling with Triton.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;How these components integrate on Amazon EC2.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-gpu-utilization-problem"&gt;The GPU utilization problem&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A single ASR inference request on the Parakeet TDT 0.6B V2 model uses roughly 15–20 percent of an NVIDIA L40S GPU’s 142 streaming multiprocessors (SMs). The remaining 80 percent sits idle during each forward pass. CUDA’s default time-slicing behavior compounds this waste by giving each process exclusive GPU access. Processes take turns, context switching adds overhead between them, and no concurrent execution occurs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The result: a single GPU handles only approximately 62 RPS at acceptable latency (mean &amp;lt; 650 ms, p99 &amp;lt; 1,000 ms). This requires 16 GPUs in Heidi’s current production deployment to handle peak traffic with sufficient headroom for latency service-level agreements (SLAs).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To address this utilization gap, we evaluated three GPU sharing mechanisms available on NVIDIA hardware, each with different tradeoffs between isolation, concurrency, and operational complexity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram compares default GPU time-slicing behavior with CUDA MPS concurrent execution, showing how MPS eliminates idle SM capacity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-21209-1.png" alt="Diagram comparing default GPU time-slicing, where each request uses about 20% of SMs and 80% sits idle across 16 GPUs, with CUDA MPS running four concurrent 25% SM instances on 4 GPUs" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: GPU time-slicing versus CUDA MPS. Left: default time-slicing where each request uses only ~20% of SMs with ~80% idle and context-switch overhead, requiring 16 GPUs. Right: CUDA MPS partitions the GPU into 4 concurrent instances at 25% SM each, achieving 92.1 RPS with only 4 GPUs (75% reduction)&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="understanding-gpu-sharing-time-slicing-mig-and-mps"&gt;Understanding GPU sharing: time-slicing, MIG, and MPS&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA GPUs offer three mechanisms for multi-tenant sharing, each with different tradeoffs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mechanism&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Isolation&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concurrent Execution&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Best For&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Time-slicing (default)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Full context switch&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No — sequential&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Few large models&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MIG (Multi-Instance GPU)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hard physical partition&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes — fixed partitions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-tenant isolation&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MPS (Multi-Process Service)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Shared context, soft SM limits&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes — concurrent kernels&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Many small models on one GPU&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NVIDIA CUDA MPS&lt;/strong&gt; is a binary-compatible alternative implementation of the CUDA API. It allows multiple processes to share a GPU concurrently without code changes. Unlike time-slicing (where processes rotate access) or Multi-Instance GPU (MIG, which creates hard physical partitions with dedicated memory controllers), MPS funnels all CUDA work through a single GPU context managed by an MPS daemon process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Key advantages for our workload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Removes context-switching overhead: all processes share one set of GPU scheduling resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Supports concurrent kernel execution, where kernels from different processes run simultaneously on different SMs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configurable partition size through the CUDA_MPS_ACTIVE_THREAD_PERCENTAGE environment variable.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Works without code changes. Existing CUDA applications run unmodified.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Memory protection between clients through separate address spaces.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For this workload, deploy two separate MPS configurations on dedicated GPU instances. Transcription instances use 25 percent SM allocation with four concurrent processes (each using approximately 2.5 GB of the 48 GB VRAM). Diarization instances use 12 percent SM with eight concurrent processes (approximately 1.8 GB each).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Although MPS addresses GPU utilization, we can further reduce per-request compute time through model-level optimizations. The next layer in our optimization stack converts the model’s compute-heavy encoder to a hardware-optimized format.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="onnx-runtime-with-tensorrt"&gt;ONNX Runtime with TensorRT&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ONNX Runtime is a high-performance inference engine that runs Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) models using hardware-specific Execution Providers. The TensorRT Execution Provider routes ONNX graph nodes to NVIDIA TensorRT, which applies kernel fusion, precision calibration (FP16/INT8), and memory optimization to produce a hardware-tuned engine.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For our workload, the pipeline uses a hybrid approach. The compute-heavy Conformer encoder (24 layers, 1024 hidden dimensions) runs through ONNX Runtime with TensorRT EP, benefiting from operator fusion and FP16 precision calibration. The RNN-T Token-and-Duration Transducer (TDT) decoder runs natively in PyTorch CUDA, where variable-length token generation with CUDA graph caching is more flexible than a static TensorRT engine.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="nvidia-triton-inference-server"&gt;NVIDIA Triton Inference Server&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVIDIA Triton Inference Server handles request scheduling and batching for production inference. The pipeline uses two batching strategies:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dynamic batching (transcription):&lt;/strong&gt; accumulates requests for a configurable delay (50 ms), then dispatches them as a batch. Preferred batch sizes [4, 8, 16] allow the scheduler to form optimal groups.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sequence batching (diarization):&lt;/strong&gt; maintains per-recording streaming state server-side. Each client sends 15-second audio chunks with a correlation ID, and Triton routes them to the correct model instance. Sessions auto-expire after 600 seconds of inactivity (max_idle_timeout).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each Triton model instance maps to one MPS partition, providing natural integration between the batching scheduler and the GPU partitioning layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These three technologies compose into a unified inference pipeline on Amazon EC2, described in the following section.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="inference-pipeline-architecture"&gt;Inference pipeline architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The inference pipeline runs on Amazon EC2 g6e.4xlarge and g7e.4xlarge instances (NVIDIA L40S, 48 GB) with three containerized components orchestrated with Docker Compose:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the three-layer inference architecture deployed on a single Amazon EC2 GPU instance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-21209-2.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing the FastAPI gateway decoding audio and routing requests over gRPC to Triton Inference Server, which dispatches across MPS-partitioned GPU model instances on Amazon EC2" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Solution architecture on Amazon EC2. The FastAPI gateway decodes audio and routes requests through gRPC to Triton Inference Server, which dispatches across MPS-partitioned model instances&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The three layers are:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;FastAPI gateway:&lt;/strong&gt; OpenAI Whisper-compatible REST API that decodes uploaded audio into raw 16 kHz mono float32 tensors before forwarding to Triton through gRPC. Runs on 4 uvicorn workers on port 8002.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;NVIDIA Triton Inference Server:&lt;/strong&gt; Manages dynamic batching (preferred batch sizes [4, 8, 16], max_queue_delay_microseconds: 50000) for transcription, and sequence batching for streaming diarization. Dispatches requests across model instances.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CUDA MPS daemon:&lt;/strong&gt; Starts inside the Triton container before the inference server. Partitions the GPU into concurrent execution contexts (four instances at 25 percent SM for transcription, or eight at 12 percent SM for diarization).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Supporting AWS services:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecr/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)&lt;/a&gt;: Stores the Triton and gateway container images (nvcr.io/nvidia/tritonserver:26.03-py3 base).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ebs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS)&lt;/a&gt;: Model checkpoints, TensorRT engine cache, and ONNX exports.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt;: Prometheus metrics ingestion, log aggregation, and p50/p90/p95/p99 latency dashboards.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;: Model artifact archive and checkpoint storage.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following prerequisites apply to the accompanying repository. With them in place, you can follow the deployment steps in the following sections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to Amazon EC2 g6e.4xlarge or g7e.4xlarge instances (NVIDIA L40S, 48 GB).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;NVIDIA drivers 535+ with CUDA 12.x.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Docker with NVIDIA Container Toolkit.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;NVIDIA Triton Inference Server container (nvcr.io/nvidia/tritonserver:26.03-py3).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;NVIDIA NeMo&lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/2122.png" alt="™" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt; Toolkit 2.7+ and a Parakeet TDT 0.6B V2 model checkpoint (.nemo format).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;torchcodec for audio decoding (pip install torchcodec).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;tritonclient[grpc] for gateway-to-Triton communication.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="key-implementation-details"&gt;Key implementation details&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An open source repository accompanying this post provides the complete implementation. The following sections walk through deployment, configuration, and the key design decisions behind stable operation under CUDA MPS. The repository contains everything needed to build and deploy the inference pipeline: Dockerfiles, the Triton model backend, the FastAPI gateway, and orchestration configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Clone the accompanying repository and deploy with three commands:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# 1. Build (provide your fine-tuned .nemo checkpoint)
docker build -f Dockerfile.single \
  --build-arg LOCAL_NEMO_FILENAME=your_model.nemo \
  -t parakeet-mps:latest .

# 2. Run with 4 concurrent instances on one GPU
docker run --gpus all --shm-size=2g \
  -e MPS_INSTANCE_COUNT=4 \
  -p 8002:8002 \
  parakeet-mps:latest

# 3. Transcribe (OpenAI Whisper-compatible API)
curl -X POST http://localhost:8002/v1/audio/transcriptions \
  -F file=@audio.wav -F model=parakeet-tdt&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The container handles MPS daemon startup, model loading, and health monitoring automatically. The service is ready when /health returns 200 (typically 90-120 seconds after start). Refer to the repository README for the full quick-start guide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="explore-the-repository"&gt;Explore the repository&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository supports two deployment modes: a single all-in-one container (Dockerfile.single) or a two-container split through Docker Compose for independent scaling of the GPU inference and CPU gateway layers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;├── Dockerfile.single           # All-in-one: MPS daemon + Triton + gateway
├── Dockerfile.triton           # Triton-only (GPU container)
├── Dockerfile.gateway          # Gateway-only (CPU container)
├── docker-compose.yml          # Two-container orchestration
├── start-single-container.sh   # Single-container entrypoint
├── auto_config.py              # MPS instance count -&amp;gt; Triton config
├── server.py                   # FastAPI gateway (OpenAI-compatible API)
└── triton_model_repo/
    └── parakeet_asr/
        ├── config.pbtxt        # Dynamic batching configuration
        └── 1/model.py          # Python backend - direct forward pass&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="build-and-run-the-container"&gt;Build and run the container&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Build the container image with your fine-tuned .nemo checkpoint as a build argument, then run with the MPS instance count you want. The Dockerfile bakes the checkpoint into the image and applies local attention optimization during the build step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Build the all-in-one image
docker build -f Dockerfile.single \
  --build-arg LOCAL_NEMO_FILENAME=your_model.nemo \
  -t parakeet-mps:latest .

# Run with 4 MPS instances (25% SM each) on a single GPU
docker run --gpus all --shm-size=2g \
  -e MPS_INSTANCE_COUNT=4 \
  -p 8002:8002 \
  parakeet-mps:latest&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The container startup sequence is: (1) start the CUDA MPS daemon, (2) run auto_config.py to set the Triton instance count and SM percentage, (3) launch tritonserver. Refer to the repository for complete build and run instructions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="configure-the-deployment"&gt;Configure the deployment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Environment variables (MPS_INSTANCE_COUNT, GATEWAY_WORKERS, TRITON_URL, CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES) control all runtime behavior. The same container image works across GPU types by changing MPS_INSTANCE_COUNT, which sets both the Triton instance group count and the SM percentage per instance. Refer to the repository README for the full configuration reference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="understand-the-key-design-decisions"&gt;Understand the key design decisions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The implementation makes several important design choices that are critical for stable operation under CUDA MPS. We highlight the most important ones in this section.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Direct forward pass.&lt;/strong&gt; The Triton backend calls model.forward() directly instead of calling model.transcribe() through NeMo, removing approximately 50 ms of framework overhead per request. Combined with bfloat16 autocast and a dedicated CUDA stream per instance, a single instance processes 45-second audio in approximately 160 ms.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Serialized model loading.&lt;/strong&gt; Loading a 600M-parameter model from four processes simultaneously would exceed GPU memory. The backend serializes initialization using a file lock (fcntl.flock), with each instance loading, moving to GPU, freezing weights, and performing CUDA graph warmup before releasing the lock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CUDA graph warmup envelope.&lt;/strong&gt; The TDT decoder uses CUDA graphs to eliminate kernel launch overhead. During initialization, the backend pre-warms all expected production shapes (5, 15, 30, 45, and 60 seconds at batch size 1, plus batch size 2 at 61 seconds). Shapes within this envelope replay cached graphs at approximately 165 ms. Shapes exceeding it fall back to eager execution (approximately 500 ms) for that call only.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MPS-safe CUDA graph fallback.&lt;/strong&gt; Under MPS, when NeMo’s decoder encounters a new tensor shape, it attempts to recapture the CUDA graph. During the 1.5–2.5 second recapture window, sibling MPS instances corrupt the capture, causing cudaErrorIllegalAddress and crashing the process.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Wedge sentinel health monitoring.&lt;/strong&gt; Under sustained load, CUDA errors in one MPS instance can leave it unrecoverable. When the backend detects a wedged instance (through a CUDA stream probe failure), it writes a sentinel file to tmpfs (/tmp/parakeet_wedged).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dynamic batching.&lt;/strong&gt; Triton accumulates requests up to batch size 16 (preferred sizes 4, 8, 16) with a 50 ms max queue delay, balancing latency against throughput.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Gateway-side audio decoding.&lt;/strong&gt; The FastAPI gateway handles audio decoding (WAV, WebM/Opus, MP3, M4A, FLAC) using torchcodec, keeping the Triton input as raw float32 tensors so the gateway can run on CPU-only nodes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="streaming-diarization"&gt;Streaming diarization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The speaker diarization model (NVIDIA Streaming Sortformer 4-speaker v2) uses eight MPS instances at 12 percent SM each with sequence batching for per-recording state. Each recording gets a unique correlation ID for chunk routing, with sessions auto-expiring after 600 seconds. The model runs as a TensorRT + ONNX engine with warmup optimization at container start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="api-endpoints"&gt;API endpoints&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway exposes an OpenAI Whisper-compatible API (POST /v1/audio/transcriptions), making it a drop-in replacement for existing integrations. Additional endpoints include /health (liveness + wedge sentinel check) and /metrics (Prometheus-format latency quantiles). Response formats include json, verbose_json, text, srt, and vtt. Refer to the repository for the full API reference.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The benchmark sweeps concurrency from 1 to 100 on each configuration, averaging 5 rounds of measurements. Audio samples are representative clinical consultation segments. The SLA threshold is: mean latency &amp;lt; 650 ms AND p99 &amp;lt; 1,000 ms.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;h3 id="configuration-2-triton-mps-g7e.4xlarge---selected-production-path"&gt;Configuration 2: Triton + MPS (g7e.4xlarge) – Selected production path&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conc&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;RPS&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;RPM&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p50 (ms)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mean (ms)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p99 (ms)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SLA&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;8.3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;498&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;121.0&lt;/td&gt;

&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result: 4 GPUs recommended (compared to 16 today), a 75 percent reduction.&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;div id="attachment_137931" style="width: 1315px" class="wp-caption alignleft"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-137931" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-137931" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/26/ML-21209-Figure-3.png" alt="" width="1305" height="963"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-137931" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Throughput scaling with concurrency for all configurations. The dashed region indicates SLA violation (mean &amp;gt; 650 ms or p99 &amp;gt; 1,000 ms). The selected production path (Triton + MPS on g7e) achieves 92.1 RPS per GPU with 75% infrastructure savings&lt;/p&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;-23%&lt;/td&gt;

&lt;p&gt;The warmup optimization also reduced standard deviation from 12.62 ms to 7.13 ms (under 44 percent), indicating significantly more predictable inference latency. The model processes 60-second recordings in four chunks of 15 seconds each, all well within the overall pipeline budget.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In operational terms, diarization processes a 60-second consultation in four chunks of 15 seconds at 238 ms mean latency per chunk (total under 1 second, real-time factor 0.016x). The eight diarization instances run on a separate MPS partition from transcription without contention.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid incurring ongoing charges after testing, clean up the resources you created while following this post:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Stop and terminate the Amazon EC2 GPU instances (g6e.4xlarge or g7e.4xlarge).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete attached Amazon EBS volumes (model checkpoints, TensorRT cache).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove Docker images from Amazon ECR if pushed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete any Amazon CloudWatch log groups created during testing.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how NVIDIA CUDA MPS on Amazon EC2 reduces ASR inference infrastructure by 75 percent (from 16 GPUs to 4) while maintaining sub-second latency SLAs (mean &amp;lt; 650 ms, p99 &amp;lt; 1,000 ms). On g7e.4xlarge, MPS achieves 92.1 RPS per GPU at 352 ms mean latency. The TensorRT + ONNX + MPS optimization pushes further to 111.6 RPS (88 percent reduction) for workloads where ONNX re-export on each fine-tuning cycle is acceptable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These optimizations are model-agnostic: the MPS architecture, direct forward-pass pattern, CUDA graph safety mechanism, and wedge sentinel apply to any encoder-decoder model served through Triton on NVIDIA GPUs. The same approach has been validated with NVIDIA Canary and OpenAI Whisper large-v3 checkpoints. The pattern extends to any workload where individual requests use a small fraction of available GPU compute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For production deployments, start with four MPS instances on g7e.4xlarge and monitor GPU SM utilization with nvidia-smi. If p99 latency has headroom, increase MPS_INSTANCE_COUNT incrementally. The TensorRT + ONNX + MPS path delivers an additional 21 percent throughput gain (111.6 compared to 92.1 RPS). The tradeoff is a longer deployment pipeline that requires weekly ONNX re-export.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/genai-ml-platform-examples/tree/main/infrastructure/nvidia-parakeet-model-mps" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; contains the complete implementation: Dockerfiles, Triton model configurations, the FastAPI gateway, CUDA graph safety patch, health monitoring, and benchmark scripts, ready to deploy on any EC2 GPU instance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, explore the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/genai-ml-platform-examples/tree/main/infrastructure/nvidia-parakeet-model-mps" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accompanying GitHub repository with complete implementation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/instance-types/g6e/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon EC2 G6e and G7e instances&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/triton-inference-server/user-guide/docs/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA Triton Inference Server documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fine-tuning-nvidia-nemotron-speech-asr-on-amazon-ec2-for-domain-adaptation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Part 1: Fine-tuning NVIDIA NeMoTron Speech ASR on Amazon EC2 for domain adaptation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;
