		<guid isPermaLink="false">1e93ba86b7ae1669fbe51dab04bbc4c453221247</guid>

					<description>The advanced side of supervised fine-tuning data prep. This second post in a two-part series covers evaluating data readiness with learning curves, selecting high-value data subsets, augmenting data with synthetic and distilled examples, and mixing data sources to prevent catastrophic forgetting.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Data preparation for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) doesn’t end when your dataset is clean and correctly formatted. The harder questions come next. How much data do you actually need? Should you collect more, or select a better subset of what you have? How do you generate high-quality examples when human annotation doesn’t scale? And how do you specialize a model without erasing its general capabilities? This post assumes you have prepared a quality-checked, schema-compliant SFT dataset and are ready to optimize it for training. The first post in this series, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/preparing-data-for-supervised-fine-tuning-part-1-formatting-and-quality/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Preparing data for supervised fine-tuning Part 1: Formatting and quality&lt;/a&gt;, covers that groundwork: the conversational format Amazon Nova expects, the quality checks that catch malformed and low-signal examples, and how to split train and evaluation data without leakage. If your dataset hasn’t been through those steps, start there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This second post covers four advanced strategies: evaluating data readiness with learning curve analysis, data subset selection and filtering, data augmentation, and data mixing. We reference Amazon Nova customization findings throughout, and the guidance applies to any model you choose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-readiness-evaluation"&gt;Data readiness evaluation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After your data is cleaned and formatted, assess whether you have enough signal to train effectively.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="volume-and-distribution-assessment"&gt;Volume and distribution assessment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As a general starting point, plan for roughly 2,000 high-quality training samples for a typical SFT task. Treat this as a ballpark estimate: the right size varies with task difficulty and how far the model’s current behavior is from your goal. A simple format or style change can work with as few as 500 samples, while a complex multi-step reasoning task might require 10,000 or more. Every task and domain has different saturation characteristics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before running those experiments, one prerequisite matters more than anything else: a clearly defined evaluation benchmark. This means an evaluation set that’s representative of production traffic, with metrics that reflect what good means for your use case. Most teams find this harder than preparing the training data itself. If you don’t yet have one, build it first. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/evaluations/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock evaluations&lt;/a&gt; supports evaluating models against your own datasets and metrics, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/evaluate-large-language-models-for-quality-and-responsibility/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Evaluate large language models for quality and responsibility&lt;/a&gt; on the AWS Machine Learning Blog walks through building an evaluation pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the benchmark in place, determine your dataset size empirically through a learning curve analysis:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Train once, evaluate checkpoints.&lt;/strong&gt; Run a single training job on your full dataset and save intermediate checkpoints, for example every 10–20 percent of training. Evaluate each checkpoint on your held-out evaluation set. During the first epoch, a checkpoint at step N has seen roughly N/total_steps of your unique data, so this approximates a data scaling curve without multiple training runs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Plot performance against data scale.&lt;/strong&gt; Chart your downstream metric against training tokens consumed. You’re looking for the saturation point, where doubling data yields less than 1–2 percent improvement on your primary metric.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stop when gains diminish.&lt;/strong&gt; If the curve is flattening, more data of the same type won’t help. Either stop training early and save compute, or add data that’s qualitatively different, specifically targeting failure cases the model still gets wrong. Figure 1 shows how successive checkpoint gains distinguish a flattening curve from one that is still improving.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20903P2-1.png" alt="Learning curve showing model performance flattening as training data scale increases, marking the saturation point" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Read the gain from each doubling, not only the final score. When doubling the data improves the primary metric by less than 1 to 2 percentage points, more data of the same type is unlikely to help. Values are illustrative&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="why-more-data-doesnt-always-mean-better-performance"&gt;Why more data doesn’t always mean better performance&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SFT doesn’t follow the same monotonic power-law scaling as pretraining. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2509.06463v2" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SFT scaling research&lt;/a&gt; shows that data volume alone doesn’t guarantee proportional gains. What matters is coverage and depth of the instruction set. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11149" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Data Repetition Beats Scaling&lt;/a&gt; demonstrated this strikingly. Under a fixed compute budget, 128 epochs on 400 reasoning examples beat single-epoch training on 51,200 examples by 12–26 percentage points on AIME and GPQA. A smaller, high-quality dataset trained to full memorization can outperform a larger dataset the model sees only once. Training token accuracy serves as a practical stopping criterion, because gains plateau after the model achieves near-perfect training accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-subset-selection-and-filtering"&gt;Data subset selection and filtering&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When diminishing returns set in, intelligent data selection can outperform training on the full set. Methods like &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.15685" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DEITA&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.04425" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DELIFT&lt;/a&gt;, and coreset selection identify the smallest subset that covers your task space while maintaining quality and diversity. These methods score candidates on quality, diversity, and how much each example actually teaches the model. Two benefits stand out:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Matching or exceeding full-data performance with fewer samples.&lt;/strong&gt; Removing redundant or low-quality examples provides cleaner gradient signal. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.08701" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AlpaGasus&lt;/a&gt; showed that filtering to the top 20 percent by quality trained faster and scored higher than the full set. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.14115" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Rethinking Data Selection&lt;/a&gt; demonstrates that subset selection can outperform full-dataset training.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reduced catastrophic forgetting.&lt;/strong&gt; Fewer, more targeted samples mean fewer gradient updates pulling the model away from its pretrained capabilities. This makes subset selection a natural complement to data mixing: you get task specialization with less forgetting, potentially reducing the need for mixing at all.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A practical workflow ties readiness evaluation and selection together. The learning curve is a diagnostic (one training run with saved checkpoints), so you reach these decisions without repeated full-scale training runs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run the learning-curve diagnostic on your full dataset.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Identify the saturation point.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If it saturates early, the tail of your dataset is adding little signal. Curate a high-value subset (quality and diversity filtering) and make that your training set going forward. This is not repeating the diagnostic run for the same result: a curated subset trains faster on every future retraining cycle, and quality filtering often scores higher than the full set by removing low-value examples that add noise to the gradient (the AlpaGasus result cited earlier in this section).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If it’s still improving at 100 percent, data volume is the bottleneck. Collect more data targeting failure cases from error analysis, not more of the same distribution.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If targeted data is expensive, consider reinforcement fine-tuning with programmatic verification as a more efficient path.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-augmentation"&gt;Data augmentation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When your dataset is too small or too narrow, augmentation can expand it without proportional annotation cost. The most impactful form of augmentation for modern SFT is generating reasoning traces and synthetic demonstrations. In practice, most teams source augmented data one of three ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Distill from a stronger model.&lt;/strong&gt; Generate traces or responses from a capable teacher such as &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.12948" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DeepSeek-R1&lt;/a&gt; or a frontier commercial model, verify the final answer, and keep the outputs on correct samples. This is how most open reasoning datasets are built.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Self-generate and filter.&lt;/strong&gt; Have the base model you’re fine-tuning produce multiple candidate responses per problem at high temperature, then keep only those whose final answers are correct or whose reasoning is self-consistent across samples. This is the core idea behind &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.14465" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;STaR&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amplify human-authored data for high-stakes domains.&lt;/strong&gt; When correctness matters more than volume, in medical, legal, or safety-critical settings, expert-written examples remain the gold standard. You can amplify them by using a large language model (LLM) to paraphrase expert reasoning into multiple styles while preserving the logical steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond reasoning traces, classic augmentation techniques expand instruction datasets directly. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.10560" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Self-Instruct&lt;/a&gt; bootstraps new instruction-response pairs from a small seed set, and &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.12244" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Evol-Instruct&lt;/a&gt; evolves existing instructions along controlled dimensions such as added constraints or deeper reasoning requirements. Simpler transformations also help: paraphrasing prompts to cover phrasing variation, and reformatting responses to match your target output style. These techniques directly address the coverage and depth properties that predict SFT generalization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two quality principles govern augmented data. First, diversity in style matters, not just content. Two correct but differently structured solutions are more useful than two copies of the same solution, a pattern &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.05653" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MAmmoTH&lt;/a&gt; exploits. Second, verification is non-negotiable. Every augmented example should pass the same quality bar as human-curated data, because synthetic generation is one of the most common sources of duplicates and subtle errors. Apply the deduplication and filtering steps from the first post to augmented data before it enters your training set.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-mixing-patterns"&gt;Data mixing patterns&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you fine-tune a model for a specific task, you risk degrading its performance on other capabilities, a phenomenon known as catastrophic forgetting. Data mixing addresses this by blending your target-task data with samples that preserve the model’s existing strengths. For Amazon Nova customization, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/nova/forge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova Forge&lt;/a&gt; supports this pattern, so you can blend proprietary data with Amazon-curated training data at each stage of customization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An important nuance: Data mixing doesn’t always help your target task. Its primary purpose is to retain general capabilities alongside specialization, and mixing in general data often comes at a direct cost to your domain metrics, because every general-data token displaces a domain-data token. Positive transfer does occur when data sources share underlying reasoning patterns: &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.12186" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Qwen2.5-Coder&lt;/a&gt; found that a 70:20:10 ratio of code, text, and math outperformed training on 100 percent code, even on coding benchmarks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Conversely, mixing can hurt when token-length imbalance lets general data dominate the loss: even a 5 percent general-data mix by sample count can represent over 80 percent of gradient signal by token count if its sequences are much longer. Monitor token-level ratios, not just sample-level ratios.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The practical implication: Treat data mixing as an insurance policy for general capabilities, not as a performance booster for your target task.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-data-mixing-works"&gt;How data mixing works&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Instead of training exclusively on task-specific data, you compose each training batch from multiple data sources with controlled proportions. A typical mix might be 70 percent target task, 20 percent general instruction-following, and 10 percent safety data. Mixing happens at the batch level, so the model sees a consistent distribution throughout training.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few empirical findings from the SFT-mixing literature are worth knowing before you start experimenting. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14908" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dong et al.&lt;/a&gt; find that the absolute amount of data per skill drives performance more than the precise ratio between categories, so if a skill is weak, add data for it before rebalancing. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.14316" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dual-stage Mixed Fine-Tuning&lt;/a&gt; shows that training on specialty data first, then on general data mixed with a small fraction of specialty data, outperforms both sequential training (which forgets) and flat mixtures (which interfere). And &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.09837" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cao et al.&lt;/a&gt; show that optimal mixtures shift with model size and data budget, so there’s no universal ratio and you should plan to experiment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="automated-mixture-optimization"&gt;Automated mixture optimization&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you have many candidate data sources and can’t afford to sweep all combinations manually, automated methods find near-optimal weights with a fraction of the compute. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.10429" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DoReMi&lt;/a&gt; trains a small proxy model and up-weights the domains with the highest excess loss, and the resulting weights transfer reliably to full-scale runs. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.01492" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;RegMix&lt;/a&gt; fits a regression that predicts performance from mixture weights across many small training runs, which is useful when you care about specific downstream benchmarks. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14908" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dynamic Data Mixing&lt;/a&gt; adjusts weights during training based on how much each source is still contributing, and typically outperforms the best static mixture by 2–5 percent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scenario&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended approach&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2–3 data sources, clear priority&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Manual: try 3 fixed ratios, pick the best&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Over 5 sources, unclear interactions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;RegMix: fit a regression from 50 small runs&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Large-scale training, budget for proxy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;DoReMi: train a small proxy to find weights&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Repeated training runs, evolving data&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Dynamic: adjust weights during each run&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For most practitioners starting out, the manual approach is sufficient: pick 3–5 candidate mixtures reflecting different priorities, train each for a short run, evaluate, and scale the winner up. Graduate to automated methods when you have more than five data sources or run regular retraining cycles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="choosing-your-mix-ratio"&gt;Choosing your mix ratio&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There’s no universal optimal ratio. As a rule of thumb, the more specialized the task, the more target-heavy the mix: highly specialized tasks such as medical coding can run 80 percent target data, while light adaptations such as tone changes benefit from closer to an even split, with 5 to 10 percent safety data in either case.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Experiments with Amazon Nova models show a 50/50 split provides a strong balance between specialization and capability retention. Three other findings worth carrying into your own experiments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Include the reasoning-instruction-following category. It significantly improves general benchmark performance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Watch the learning rate. SFT with data mixing is sensitive to it, so use the defaults of 1e-5 for Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and 5e-6 for full-rank as starting points.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For multimodal datasets, keep video above 20 percent. This helps maintain general benchmark performance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="decision-framework-mix-or-dont-mix"&gt;Decision framework: mix or don’t mix?&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most customers fine-tune an instruction-tuned model (such as Amazon Nova Lite 2.0 or any off-the-shelf chat model), which has already been post-trained to follow instructions, hold conversations, and respond safely. This framework assumes that starting point. The rare exception is starting from a base checkpoint that has only been pre-trained on next-token prediction. In that case, always mix, because there’s no instruction-following or safety behavior to preserve. From an instruction-tuned starting point, train briefly without mixing and evaluate checkpoints. The decision comes down to two questions, both answered by that single run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Does the no-mix run overfit your target data?&lt;/strong&gt; Watch the signals: training loss keeps falling while validation loss stalls or rises, or target-task validation performance peaks at an intermediate checkpoint and then degrades. Small datasets of a few thousand examples raise this risk, but there is no hard threshold. It depends on epochs, learning rate, and whether you train LoRA or full-rank. Respond with training-configuration fixes first: fewer epochs, earlier stopping, a lower learning rate. If overfitting persists, mixing in general data adds gradient diversity and can act as a further regularizer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Did the same run regress the general skills you need?&lt;/strong&gt; Evaluate the capabilities your application needs in production, such as instruction following, conversation, or math and coding. If they held steady, ship without mixing and save the training cost. If they regressed beyond your tolerance, add mixing and scale the general-data ratio to the severity of the regression.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dataset size and diversity alone don’t settle either question. A 20,000-example medical dataset, however diverse within its domain, can still erode math or coding skills if your application depends on them. That’s why the framework measures instead of assumes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Data mixing isn’t the only way to fight forgetting. If you update the model repeatedly over time, algorithmic approaches such as &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.01858" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OSFT&lt;/a&gt; restrict each training run to directions in weight space that don’t disturb prior knowledge. OSFT is available as a drop-in option in Hugging Face PEFT.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The advanced strategies covered here share a common thread: measure before you act. Learning curve analysis tells you whether more data will help. Subset selection tells you which data matters. Augmentation fills gaps that collection can’t reach economically. And a controlled mixing experiment tells you whether capability retention is worth the training cost. Teams that treat these as empirical questions, rather than applying defaults, consistently get better models for less compute.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you haven’t read it yet, the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/preparing-data-for-supervised-fine-tuning-part-1-formatting-and-quality/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;first post&lt;/a&gt; in this series covers the foundations: data quality checks, formatting requirements, and train/evaluation splits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, visit the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; to explore model customization options. Learn more about &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova&lt;/a&gt; on the service detail page, review the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova documentation&lt;/a&gt; for customization guides, and explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/sagemaker-hyperpod-recipes" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod recipes repository&lt;/a&gt; for ready-to-run training configurations. For related reading, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/customize-amazon-nova-models-with-amazon-bedrock-fine-tuning/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Customize Amazon Nova models with Amazon Bedrock fine-tuning&lt;/a&gt; on the AWS Machine Learning Blog.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Preparing data for supervised fine-tuning Part 1: Formatting and quality</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/preparing-data-for-supervised-fine-tuning-part-1-formatting-and-quality/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Elyse Zhang]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 26 Aug 2026 16:24:02 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Nova]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">754286664acbb27c986bdae9cedda3b54d89bae4</guid>

					<description>Data preparation determines the ceiling of any supervised fine-tuning project. This first post in a two-part series covers the foundations of SFT data prep: quality checks, conversational (JSONL) formatting, reasoning and tool-calling schemas, and a representative train/evaluation split.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Data preparation determines the ceiling of any supervised fine-tuning (SFT) project. You’ve evaluated your foundation model (FM), and out-of-the-box performance isn’t meeting your production requirements. Maybe the model doesn’t follow your output schema reliably, struggles with your domain’s classification taxonomy, or can’t maintain the tone your application demands. The question isn’t whether to customize, it’s how. This post assumes you have decided to fine-tune a foundation model and are evaluating how to prepare data for that work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Post-training customization provides three distinct levers. Each addresses a different gap between what the model can do today and what you need it to do. &lt;em&gt;Continued pre-training (CPT)&lt;/em&gt; ingests large volumes of unstructured domain text to expand the model’s knowledge base. Use CPT when the model lacks familiarity with your domain’s terminology, concepts, or data patterns. &lt;em&gt;Supervised fine-tuning (SFT)&lt;/em&gt; trains on curated input-output pairs to reshape the model’s behavior. SFT teaches the model how to respond: following instructions, adhering to schemas, adopting a specific tone, or producing structured outputs. It doesn’t inject new knowledge. It teaches the model to apply what it already knows in the way that you need, an idea sometimes called the &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.00752" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Superficial Alignment Hypothesis&lt;/a&gt;. &lt;em&gt;Reinforcement fine-tuning (RFT)&lt;/em&gt; optimizes behavior through reward signals rather than explicit demonstrations. RFT works when you can programmatically evaluate output quality but can’t easily demonstrate the reasoning path at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These techniques aren’t mutually exclusive. A production pattern is CPT, then SFT, then RFT: first expand knowledge, then shape behavior, then optimize through feedback. In practice, CPT is used less often and only required when the base model lacks critical domain vocabulary or knowledge your task requires. Foundation models like Amazon Nova are already pre-trained on a broad corpus, so SFT followed by RFT is usually sufficient.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post, the first of a two-part series, covers the foundations of SFT data preparation: quality checks, formatting requirements, and train/evaluation splits. We use code snippets from &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/nova-2-sft-data-prep.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock documentation&lt;/a&gt; to illustrate key concepts while keeping the guidance applicable to any model you choose. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/preparing-data-for-supervised-fine-tuning-part-2-advanced-data-strategies/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;second post&lt;/a&gt; covers advanced strategies: readiness evaluation, data subset selection and filtering, data augmentation, and data mixing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-quality-checks"&gt;Data quality checks&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you invest in formatting or training infrastructure, audit your raw data. Catching problems early saves substantial time and compute cost downstream.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="accuracy-and-correctness"&gt;Accuracy and correctness&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every response in your dataset should be a gold-standard answer you would be comfortable deploying to production. Incorrect examples can teach the model a persistent bad habit that’s difficult to unlearn. This risk is especially acute in SFT, where the model isn’t learning new facts so much as learning which patterns to imitate. A wrong demonstration gets imitated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The practical upshot is that quality beats quantity by a wide margin. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.11206" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LIMA&lt;/a&gt; showed that 1,000 carefully curated examples can match models trained on orders of magnitude more data. &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.08701" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AlpaGasus&lt;/a&gt; showed that filtering an instruction set down to its cleanest 20 percent can train faster and score higher than the full set. If you’re working with human-annotated data, implement a multi-review process before examples enter your training set.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="diversity-of-examples"&gt;Diversity of examples&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Dataset diversity is one of the strongest predictors of SFT success. Research on &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.14854" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;supervised fine-tuning scalability&lt;/a&gt; identifies two properties that govern how well fine-tuning generalizes. The first is semantic coverage, the breadth of task domains and prompt phrasings represented. The second is information depth, the richness of individual examples. A dataset that covers only a narrow slice of your production traffic will produce a model that performs well on that slice and poorly everywhere else.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Audit your dataset for prompt variation first. Your examples should include the different ways users phrase the same intent, because a model trained only on “Summarize this document” won’t reliably handle “Give me the key takeaways.” Check domain and topic breadth next. If your task spans multiple domains, verify that each is represented proportionally to its production frequency. Then look at difficulty range: include straightforward cases alongside complex, multi-step problems, weighted toward the complexity profile of real traffic. Finally, explicitly include edge cases such as ambiguous inputs, incomplete information, and out-of-scope requests, paired with the responses you want the model to produce in those situations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A practical approach is to cluster your examples by embedding similarity and inspect the resulting clusters for gaps. Sparse or missing clusters indicate areas where the model will lack training signal. For example, a customer-support dataset might cluster into password resets, billing questions, and shipping updates. If refunds are a common request in production but no cluster covers them, that empty region is your gap. The model will have little signal for refund conversations, so collect or write refund examples before training. The second post in this series shows how to automate this idea with data subset selection methods.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="consistency-within-similar-tasks"&gt;Consistency within similar tasks&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While diversity across your dataset is critical, examples that handle the same type of situation should be internally consistent. Inconsistency within the same task type sends contradictory signals about correct behavior. For example, if similar prompts produce both bullet-point and paragraph-form answers, the model won’t learn a reliable default for response structure. If you need concise, two-sentence answers, don’t include paragraph-length responses in your training data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Target behavior&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Two inconsistent examples (avoid)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Two consistent examples (use)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Return one lowercase label from a fixed set&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;Positive&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;This one reads as fairly negative.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;positive&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;negative&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Summarize in two sentences, no preamble&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; 
    &lt;ul&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Login fails after reset&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;iOS 17&lt;/li&gt; 
     &lt;li&gt;Escalated to Tier 2&lt;/li&gt; 
    &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt;Sure! Here’s a quick summary: the customer reports that…&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;Customer cannot log in after resetting their password on iOS 17. The ticket is escalated to Tier 2.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;Customer’s card was declined three times at checkout. Billing confirmed the issuer blocked the transaction.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decline out-of-scope requests with one fixed sentence&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;I can’t help with that.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;I’m really sorry, but that falls outside what I’m able to assist with today. However, I’d be happy to…&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt; &lt;p&gt;That request is outside the scope of this assistant.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;That request is outside the scope of this assistant.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deduplication"&gt;Deduplication&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Duplicate or near-duplicate examples cause the model to overfit on those patterns, inflating their importance relative to the rest of your dataset. Duplication is less critical in SFT than in pre-training, but it can creep in when you merge data from multiple annotators, combine datasets across projects, or use synthetic data generation. In those cases, apply both exact-match and semantic deduplication before training.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="toxicity-and-safety-screening"&gt;Toxicity and safety screening&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Scan your dataset for harmful, biased, or inappropriate content. Even if your use case is narrow, the model can internalize patterns from problematic examples and surface them in unexpected contexts. Use automated classifiers, such as the open &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.06674" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Llama Guard&lt;/a&gt;, to flag content for human review, and establish clear guidelines for what constitutes acceptable training data in your domain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="data-formatting"&gt;Data formatting&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With quality checks complete, you can structure your data for training. Formatting isn’t just about syntax. How you structure examples shapes the model’s learned behavior, because SFT teaches the model to respond to a specific shape of input, not just its content.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="system-prompt-in-the-sample"&gt;System prompt in the sample&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Include a system prompt in your training examples when you plan to use one during inference. The system prompt establishes context, persona, and constraints that shape model behavior. If training data lacks system prompts but inference includes them, you create a distribution mismatch that can degrade behavior.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="conversational-format-jsonl"&gt;Conversational format (JSONL)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most modern SFT pipelines, including Amazon Nova recipes, use a conversational JSONL format where each line is a self-contained JSON object representing a conversation. The Amazon Nova 2.0 models use the Converse API format:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "schemaVersion": "bedrock-conversation-2024",
  "system": [{"text": "You are a helpful coding assistant."}],
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [{"text": "Write a Python function to check if a string is a palindrome."}]
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",
      "content": [
        {"text": "def is_palindrome(s):\n    cleaned = s.lower().replace(' ', '')\n    return cleaned == cleaned[::-1]"}
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Key formatting rules to follow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;One JSON object per line, with no pretty-printing across multiple lines.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Validate every line parses as valid JSON before uploading.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Maintain strict role alternation between user and assistant turns.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Include system messages when your production setup uses them.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="reasoning-traces"&gt;Reasoning traces&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For models with reasoning capabilities (such as Amazon Nova 2.0 with &lt;code&gt;reasoning_enabled: true&lt;/code&gt;), include intermediate thinking steps using the &lt;code&gt;reasoningContent&lt;/code&gt; field in assistant turns. Training on reasoning traces is what transfers the behavior popularized as chain-of-thought from a prompting trick into the model itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "schemaVersion": "bedrock-conversation-2024",
  "system": [{"text": "You are a financial analyst. Provide data-driven answers with supporting calculations."}],
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [{"text": "Calculate YoY revenue growth. 2024: $4.2M, 2025: $5.1M"}]
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",
      "content": [
        {
          "reasoningContent": {
            "reasoningText": {
              "text": "The user asks for year-over-year revenue growth. I need to calculate the percentage change: (new - old) / old x 100. That gives (5.1 - 4.2) / 4.2 x 100 = 21.43%."
            }
          }
        },
        {"text": "YoY revenue growth is approximately 21.4 percent: ($5.1M - $4.2M) / $4.2M x 100."}
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hardest part of using reasoning traces isn’t the format. It’s getting traces that actually teach the model something. Useful traces are faithful to the answer, meaning the reasoning actually leads to the final response. They’re proportional to difficulty: short problems get short traces and long problems get long ones, a trade-off that &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.12012" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;difficulty-aware trace compression&lt;/a&gt; makes explicit. They’re complete, with no thought leaps, because expert-written rationales often skip intermediate steps the model hasn’t yet learned. And quality matters more than quantity: &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.19393" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;s1&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.03387" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LIMO&lt;/a&gt; both show that under 1,000 carefully curated reasoning demonstrations can elicit strong reasoning in a capable base model. The second post in this series covers how to source these traces at scale through distillation and self-generation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use plain text for reasoning content and keep it directly relevant to the problem-solving process. When reasoning is enabled during training, it should also be enabled during inference for consistent behavior. Keep in mind that training on a non-reasoning dataset with &lt;code&gt;reasoning_enabled: true&lt;/code&gt; can cause the model to lose its reasoning capabilities, because it learns to generate responses without applying reasoning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tool-calling-and-multimodal-formats"&gt;Tool calling and multimodal formats&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SFT supports training models for tool use (function calling) and multimodal understanding (documents, images, video). For tool calling, &lt;code&gt;toolUse&lt;/code&gt; blocks appear in assistant turns and &lt;code&gt;toolResult&lt;/code&gt; blocks in user turns, each referencing a unique &lt;code&gt;toolUseId&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "schemaVersion": "bedrock-conversation-2024",
  "system": [{"text": "You are an expert in composing function calls."}],
  "toolConfig": {
    "tools": [
      {
        "toolSpec": {
          "name": "getItemCost",
          "description": "Retrieve the cost of an item from the catalog",
          "inputSchema": {
            "json": {
              "type": "object",
              "properties": {
                "item_id": {
                  "type": "string",
                  "description": "The ASIN of item to retrieve cost for"
                }
              },
              "required": ["item_id"]
            }
          }
        }
      }
    ]
  },
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [{"text": "How much does item id-456 cost?"}]
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",
      "content": [
        {
          "toolUse": {
            "toolUseId": "getItemCost_0",
            "name": "getItemCost",
            "input": {"item_id": "id-456"}
          }
        }
      ]
    },
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [
        {
          "toolResult": {
            "toolUseId": "getItemCost_0",
            "content": [
              {"text": "{"name": "getItemCost", "results": {"cost": "$29.99"}}"}
            ]
          }
        }
      ]
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",
      "content": [
        {"text": "Item id-456 costs $29.99."}
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Key constraints for tool calling data:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;toolUse&lt;/code&gt; must appear only in assistant turns, and &lt;code&gt;toolResult&lt;/code&gt; only in user turns.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Each &lt;code&gt;toolResult&lt;/code&gt; must reference a valid &lt;code&gt;toolUseId&lt;/code&gt; from a preceding assistant turn, used exactly once per conversation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;toolResult&lt;/code&gt; content should be text or JSON only.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The &lt;code&gt;inputSchema&lt;/code&gt; within &lt;code&gt;toolSpec&lt;/code&gt; must be a valid JSON Schema object.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For multimodal training, document and image content blocks appear in user turns alongside text, with sources referencing Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) locations:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "schemaVersion": "bedrock-conversation-2024",
  "system": [{"text": "You are a document analysis assistant."}],
  "messages": [
    {
      "role": "user",
      "content": [
        {
          "document": {
            "format": "pdf",
            "name": "quarterly_report",
            "source": {"s3Location": {"uri": "s3://&amp;lt;your-bucket-name&amp;gt;/report.pdf"}}
          }
        },
        {"text": "Summarize the key findings from this quarterly report."}
      ]
    },
    {
      "role": "assistant",
      "content": [
        {"text": "The quarterly report highlights three key findings..."}
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="staying-close-to-the-original-models-chat-template"&gt;Staying close to the original model’s chat template&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every foundation model is post-trained with a specific chat template: the exact token sequences that delimit system prompts, user turns, and assistant turns. Your training data should match this template exactly. Deviating from it, even subtly, forces the model to learn a new input format alongside the new behavior, splitting its learning budget across two objectives. In the worst case, template mismatch causes the model to ignore its system prompt or produce malformed outputs at inference time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Practical guidelines for template alignment:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use the model provider’s official formatting utilities.&lt;/strong&gt; For Amazon Nova, use the Converse API schema. For open source models, use the tokenizer’s &lt;code&gt;apply_chat_template()&lt;/code&gt; method from Hugging Face Transformers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Never hand-roll delimiters.&lt;/strong&gt; If you’re converting data from another format such as ShareGPT or Alpaca, run it through the target model’s chat template function rather than manually inserting role marker strings.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Validate tokenization round-trips.&lt;/strong&gt; After formatting, tokenize a sample of your data and decode it back to text. Confirm that role boundaries, special tokens, and content are preserved exactly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Match inference-time structure during training.&lt;/strong&gt; If your production pipeline injects a system prompt, tool definitions, or retrieval context at specific positions, your training data should include those elements in the same positions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="trainevaluation-split"&gt;Train/evaluation split&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hold out 10–20 percent of your data as an evaluation set. This isn’t optional. Without it, you can’t distinguish genuine learning from overfitting. The evaluation set should be representative of your production distribution, not a random slice that might under-represent rare but important categories.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For small datasets (under 1,000 samples), use stratified splitting so each task category appears in both training and evaluation sets. For larger datasets, random splitting usually suffices, but verify the distribution afterward. Before training, establish baseline performance on your evaluation set using the unmodified model. This gives you a concrete target to beat and helps you quantify the value of supervised fine-tuning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Data preparation is the most impactful activity in any SFT workflow. The patterns covered here, including systematic quality checks, schema-compliant formatting, and a representative train/evaluation split, apply whether you’re fine-tuning for a narrow classification task or a complex multi-turn dialogue system. Invest the time upfront, and your training jobs will converge faster, generalize better, and avoid the costly cycle of debugging model behavior that traces back to data problems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/preparing-data-for-supervised-fine-tuning-part-2-advanced-data-strategies/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;second post&lt;/a&gt; of this series, we cover the advanced side of SFT data preparation: evaluating data readiness with learning curves, selecting high-value subsets, augmenting your data, and mixing data sources to prevent catastrophic forgetting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, visit the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock console&lt;/a&gt; to explore model customization options. Learn more about &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova&lt;/a&gt; on the service detail page, review the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova documentation&lt;/a&gt; for customization guides, and explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/sagemaker-hyperpod-recipes" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod recipes repository&lt;/a&gt; for ready-to-run training configurations. For related reading, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/customize-amazon-nova-models-with-amazon-bedrock-fine-tuning/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Customize Amazon Nova models with Amazon Bedrock fine-tuning&lt;/a&gt; on the AWS Machine Learning Blog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

		<title>Connect Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to cross-account knowledge bases</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/connect-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-to-cross-account-knowledge-bases/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kunal Ghosh]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 26 Aug 2026 15:48:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">72a57f49ae4a30c9171124efb14f5f05b1febd89</guid>

					<description>Learn how Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agents in one account can generate answers from an Amazon Bedrock knowledge base backed by Amazon Redshift Serverless in another account, without copying source data. This post covers the architecture, security boundary, and two orchestration models: a code-based Strands agent and a declarative AgentCore harness.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Organizations often deploy agents using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. These agents may access governed knowledge bases hosted in separate AWS accounts. This cross-account separation helps maintain clear workload boundaries but can introduce integration challenges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post explains how AgentCore agents in one account can generate answers from a knowledge base (using Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, the fully managed Retrieval Augmented Generation capability) backed by Amazon Redshift Serverless in another account without copying source data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post covers the architecture, security boundary, request flow, and selection criteria for two generally available &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; orchestration models. The linked &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub sample&lt;/a&gt; provides the deployment procedures and implementation details for both variants:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A code-based &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands agent&lt;/a&gt; on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agents-tools-runtime.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A declarative &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/harness.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore harness, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations that build AI agents using Amazon Bedrock can maintain structured data in Amazon Redshift Serverless. These data repositories can reside in separate AWS accounts from their AI agents. Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases resource policies support &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;GetDocumentContent&lt;/code&gt; for cross-account operations. The supported resource policy actions do not include &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt;. Because this solution needs the generated answer returned by &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt;, the tool assumes a narrowly scoped AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role in the Knowledge Base account before calling the API.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This creates a challenge for enterprises with multi-account architectures who want to:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Generate natural-language answers over structured data in Amazon Redshift Serverless.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Maintain account boundaries between agent workloads and data workloads.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Avoid copying governed source data into the agent account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Grant least-privilege access through a dedicated cross-account IAM role.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution implements the cross-account query pattern in two ways. Variant 1 deploys a Strands agent to AgentCore runtime. Variant 2 uses a declarative &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/harness.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore harness&lt;/a&gt;. Both implementations use the same data access boundary. The model-controlled tool assumes the Knowledge Base access role with AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS), calls &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt;, and returns the generated answer and citations to the orchestration layer. The variants differ in who owns the agent loop and how the tool is hosted. Figure 1 compares the two orchestration paths and their shared cross-account data access boundary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img title="Figure 1. Side-by-side AgentCore implementation paths" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/21/ML-20558-1.png" alt="Two AgentCore orchestration paths share a cross-account Knowledge Base access role: a code-based Strands agent on AgentCore runtime and a declarative AgentCore harness using AgentCore Gateway and AWS Lambda." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Side-by-side AgentCore implementation paths&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="request-flow"&gt;Request flow&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The request follows this sequence:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A user submits a natural-language question through Streamlit UI or an AgentCore API.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova&lt;/a&gt; Pro decides to call the &lt;code&gt;query_knowledge_base&lt;/code&gt; tool.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In Variant 1, the tool runs in a local Model Context Protocol (MCP) subprocess packaged with AgentCore runtime. In Variant 2, the AgentCore harness calls an AWS Lambda tool through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Gateway, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The tool assumes the &lt;code&gt;bedrock_kb_access_role&lt;/code&gt; in the Knowledge Base account by using AWS STS.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The assumed-role session calls &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt; with Claude Haiku 4.5. The Knowledge Base translates the question into a structured query against Amazon Redshift Serverless.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The generated answer returns through the selected orchestration path to the user.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 2 shows the request path for the code-based Strands agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img title="Figure 2. Code-based Strands agent architecture" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/21/ML-20558-2.png" alt="A code-based Strands agent on AgentCore runtime invokes a local MCP subprocess, assumes a cross-account IAM role, and queries an Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base backed by Amazon Redshift Serverless." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Code-based Strands agent architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3 shows the corresponding managed path for the declarative AgentCore harness.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img title="Figure 3. Declarative AgentCore harness architecture" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/21/ML-20558-3.png" alt="A declarative AgentCore harness invokes an AWS Lambda tool through AgentCore Gateway, assumes a cross-account IAM role, and queries an Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base backed by Amazon Redshift Serverless." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Declarative AgentCore harness architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="choose-the-simplest-sufficient-pattern"&gt;Choose the simplest sufficient pattern&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before selecting an agent implementation, decide whether the workload needs an agent at all. This keeps the design proportional to the required behavior.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If the application only needs retrieved content, evaluate native cross-account Retrieve with a Knowledge Base resource policy.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If every request deterministically needs one generated answer, first confirm that the workload does not need tool selection, multi-step reasoning, or conversational state. If it does not, assume the Knowledge Base account role and call &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt; directly from the application or an AWS Lambda function.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use an agent when the model must decide when or how to query the Knowledge Base as part of a broader conversation or tool-using workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="when-to-use-each-agentcore-variant"&gt;When to use each AgentCore variant&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cross-account requirement does not favor one variant. Choose according to how much of the orchestration loop your team needs to customize and operate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decision area&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Code-based Strands agent&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Declarative AgentCore harness&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Best fit&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Teams that require custom orchestration, hooks, middleware, retry behavior, instrumentation, or direct control of tools and streaming.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Teams whose use case fits the managed loop and who prefer a configuration-first lifecycle.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent loop&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Your Python code runs the loop with the Strands Agents SDK.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore runs the loop from the harness definition.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tool path&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore runtime to local MCP subprocess to Knowledge Base.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore harness to AgentCore Gateway to AWS Lambda to Knowledge Base.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cross-account principal&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore runtime execution role.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Lambda execution role behind AgentCore Gateway.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What you maintain&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;agent.py&lt;/code&gt;, dependencies, MCP server, tool logic, and runtime configuration.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AgentCore harness configuration, system prompt, AWS Lambda tool, IAM, and application behavior.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Invocation API&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;InvokeAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;InvokeHarness&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The decision is custom control versus managed orchestration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, confirm that you have the following prerequisites:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Two AWS accounts: an &lt;strong&gt;&lt;em&gt;agent&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; account and an &lt;strong&gt;&lt;em&gt;agent-kb&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) v2.24.22 or later with credentials for both accounts.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10 or later and &lt;code&gt;jq&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A structured Amazon Bedrock knowledge base connected to Amazon Redshift Serverless.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model access in US West (Oregon): &lt;code&gt;us.amazon.nova-pro-v1:0&lt;/code&gt; in the agent account and &lt;code&gt;us.anthropic.claude-haiku-4-5-20251001-v1:0&lt;/code&gt; in the &lt;strong&gt;&lt;em&gt;agent-kb&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/strong&gt; account. For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For the harness variant, install the current &lt;code&gt;@aws/agentcore&lt;/code&gt; Node.js CLI in addition to the Python &lt;code&gt;bedrock-agentcore&lt;/code&gt; CLI used by the code-based variant.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="assumptions"&gt;Assumptions&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The examples use the following local profile aliases and placeholder account IDs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Profile&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Example account&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Purpose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;agent&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;111122223333&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hosts AgentCore resources.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;agent-kb&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;999999999999&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hosts the Knowledge Base and Redshift Serverless.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The examples use the US West (Oregon) Region (&lt;code&gt;us-west-2&lt;/code&gt;). Profile names are local aliases, so replace them with your configured profile names and discover account IDs instead of hardcoding them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;AGENT_PROFILE=agent
AGENT_KB_PROFILE=agent-kb
REGION=us-west-2

AGENT_ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity \
--profile "$AGENT_PROFILE" --query Account --output text)
AGENT_KB_ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity \
--profile "$AGENT_KB_PROFILE" --query Account --output text)
aws bedrock-agent list-knowledge-bases \
--profile "$AGENT_KB_PROFILE" --region "$REGION"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-walkthrough"&gt;Implementation walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post focuses on architecture and decision guidance. Deployment procedures are maintained in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;public GitHub sample&lt;/a&gt;. Use the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb#implementation-walkthrough" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Implementation walkthrough&lt;/a&gt; to prepare the structured Knowledge Base, cross-account IAM roles, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;. Then follow &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb#running-the-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Running the agent&lt;/a&gt; for the code-based Strands variant or &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb#variant-2--declarative-harness-managed-agent-loop" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Variant 2 – Declarative harness&lt;/a&gt; for the managed agent loop. The sample also includes the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb/tree/main/streamlit_app" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Streamlit end-to-end client&lt;/a&gt; for the Local, AgentCore, and Harness UI modes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validation"&gt;Validation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use the same question, Knowledge Base ID, model, retrieval count, and fresh session when comparing variants. The prompt contract tells both agents to pass the user’s question to the tool exactly as written, but &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt; remains generative. For row-level questions, specify a row count, date range, and sort order. Unbounded generated SQL can exceed result-handling limits. Validate business facts against the direct tool response and the underlying structured data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the Streamlit client, select the invocation mode before comparing results. For AgentCore or Harness mode, confirm that the corresponding deployed resource Amazon Resource Name (ARN) is populated. Start a new conversation for each independent factual comparison.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img title="Figure 4. Streamlit invocation mode" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/21/ML-20558-4.png" alt="Streamlit sidebar showing Local, AgentCore, and Harness invocation mode options, with Local selected." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Select Local, AgentCore, or Harness in the Streamlit sidebar&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 5 shows a successful bounded query in Harness mode.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img title="Figure 5. Successful Harness validation" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/21/ML-20558-5.png" alt="Streamlit Harness mode showing the deployed Harness ARN, sample queries, New conversation control, and a successful bounded query for urgent orders from the fourth quarter of 1997." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Harness mode with the deployed ARN and a successful bounded query&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Sample questions for the TPC-H dataset include:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Who are the top 5 customers in Saudi Arabia?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Who are the top parts suppliers in the United States by volume?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;What is the total revenue by region for 1998?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Which products generated the highest revenue after discounts?&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Show the 100 highest-value orders marked 1-URGENT from October 1 through December 31, 1997.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="recommended-practices"&gt;Recommended practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Apply these practices when deploying and validating either variant:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use fresh session IDs for independent factual comparisons.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Log the exact tool query, Knowledge Base ID, model ID, and retrieval result count.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Define derived business metrics with approved curated SQL and column descriptions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Bound row-level questions with an explicit row count, date range, and sort order to avoid oversized SQL result sets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Keep the cross-account role scoped to the required Knowledge Base and model resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use refreshable STS credentials in long-lived runtimes and warm AWS Lambda environments.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Test system prompts, tool descriptions, IAM, and application behavior for both variants.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Apply Amazon Bedrock Guardrails to evaluate user questions and generated answers for harmful content, denied topics, and sensitive information. Guardrails complement, but do not replace, least-privilege access, source data governance, citation-based verification, and human review for consequential decisions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow the sample’s &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb#cleanup" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cleanup section&lt;/a&gt; for the current commands and required order. When both variants are deployed, complete &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-for-strands-agentcore-connect-cross-account-kb#harness-cleanup" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;harness cleanup&lt;/a&gt; first so that its AWS Lambda execution-role trust grant is removed from the shared Knowledge Base access role. Then remove the code-based runtime and shared IAM resources. The scripts do not delete the project-managed AgentCore memory, Knowledge Base, or Redshift Serverless resources. Delete those separately when they are no longer needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="summary"&gt;Summary&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post describes how AgentCore agents can return generated answers from a structured Amazon Bedrock knowledge base across AWS account boundaries. Both implementations use a least-privilege role in the data account. The architectural choice is who owns the agent loop and how the tool is hosted. Start with native &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; or a direct &lt;code&gt;RetrieveAndGenerate&lt;/code&gt; call when those patterns satisfy the workload. When model-controlled tools or conversational orchestration are required, choose the code-based Strands variant for custom loop behavior and direct control. Choose the AgentCore harness when its managed loop fits and your team prefers a configuration-first operating model. Both are valid, generally available paths. Here is the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Documentation&lt;/a&gt; where you can &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/agentcore-get-started-cli.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;get started with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; with &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;code samples&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
