		<item>
		<title>Agentic observability with Amazon OpenSearch Service MCP Apps</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/agentic-observability-with-amazon-opensearch-service-mcp-apps/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Hang Zuo]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 25 Aug 2026 19:00:09 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon OpenSearch Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">bcd03430b15f5c1675997d3f2fcc6d165ab30085</guid>

					<description>Amazon OpenSearch Service now supports MCP Apps, which return interactive visualizations alongside your AI agent's text responses. Learn how a single, locally run MCP server lets your agent move from alert to trace to logs to root cause in one conversation, and how you can verify every step inline without leaving your IDE.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Observability agents are fast. They query alerts, correlate logs with traces, and produce a root cause hypothesis in minutes. The part that still takes time is verification. You read the agent’s text summary, open your observability tools in a browser, navigate to the trace waterfall, check the service map to scope impact, and cross-reference what the agent told you against what you see on screen. The agent saved you the query time. It did not save you the tab-switching, context-carrying, manual-verification time. That is still your job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Service MCP Apps close that gap. MCP Apps extend the Model Context Protocol so that each tool call responds with an interactive visualization — a trace waterfall, a service topology, a log pattern view — rendered directly in your AI assistant’s chat window alongside the text response. You ask the agent to investigate. The agent queries Amazon OpenSearch Service. The response arrives with both a text explanation and the relevant dashboard widget. You verify in the same thread where you asked the question, without opening a separate browser tab or re-running a query.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explain how MCP Apps change your observability workflow and walk through setup step by step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-verification-still-requires-leaving-the-agent-loop"&gt;The problem: Verification still requires leaving the agent loop&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The typical investigation loop proceeds as follows. First, the engineer asks the agent and gets a text-based root cause hypothesis. Next, they leave the IDE to open a browser and log in to a separate observability UI. They then re-run queries manually to reproduce what the agent found, in a different tool. After verifying visually by comparing the agent’s text output against actual dashboards, they return to the agent and resume the conversation, having lost their place in the investigation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agent generates a response in seconds, but you must leave the agent’s environment to verify. This means logging in to a separate observability experience and navigating dashboards manually. This external verification loop is the bottleneck. It forces you into a tool-switching role that undermines the speed advantage of agentic automation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations running agentic observability locally chose control and cost efficiency over vendor-provided AI. But this choice has historically come with a trade-off: local agentic setups sacrifice ease of use and sometimes agent performance compared to vendor-hosted solutions that tightly couple AI with their services. For these teams, the verification gap is the primary operational burden. They optimized for autonomy, yet verification still happens at human speed, in a separate observability tool.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-solution-mcp-apps-bring-observability-ui-into-your-ide"&gt;The solution: MCP Apps bring an observability UI into your IDE&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Service now supports MCP Apps, a capability that extends MCP with a &lt;em&gt;dual response&lt;/em&gt; pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When your AI agent calls an MCP App tool, the response contains two parts. The first is a &lt;em&gt;text summary &lt;/em&gt;with concise, structured data. The second is an &lt;em&gt;interactive visualization&lt;/em&gt; rendered in the same conversation thread for you to review. OpenSearch MCP App generates the visualization by executing code against the same data sources that power your dashboards. Because of this, the results are &lt;em&gt;deterministic&lt;/em&gt;. You’re not trusting the AI’s interpretation. You’re seeing the actual query result rendered as an interactive chart, trace waterfall, or service map.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-21227-1-2.png" alt="MCP App observability report showing an error-count-by-service bar chart, a root cause table, and an analysis summary." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: MCP App rendering an observability report inside the agentic IDE, showing error counts by service alongside AI-generated root cause analysis&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works"&gt;How it works&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP Apps capability consists of a local MCP server, your IDE, and your OpenSearch UI application working together. This section explains the architecture, the protocol extension mechanism, and the end-to-end flow of a single tool call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;em&gt;local MCP server &lt;/em&gt;runs on your machine. It acts as a secure bridge between your agentic IDE and your OpenSearch UI application. The server exposes observability tools that your AI agent can call. Each tool call goes through the MCP server to your OpenSearch UI endpoint, executes the query, and returns the dual response back to your IDE. OpenSearch UI is the serverless interface for unified observability that works with OpenSearch domains, serverless collections, CloudWatch, and Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus (learn more about OpenSearch UI).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the request flow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;Your IDE or AI desktop client (Claude, VS Code, Cursor, etc.)
  ↓ tool call
Local MCP server (runs on your machine)
  ↓ authenticated query
OpenSearch UI application (connected with your data sources)
  ↓ dual response
Your IDE ← text summary + interactive MCP App visualization&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;You maintain full control.&lt;/strong&gt; The MCP server runs locally. Your data stays in your AWS account. Your credentials, your policies, your domains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-mcp-apps-extend-the-mcp-protocol"&gt;How MCP Apps extend the MCP protocol&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Standard MCP tool calls return text-only responses. The agent sends a JSON-RPC request specifying the tool name and parameters, and the server returns a text result that the agent incorporates into its reasoning. MCP Apps extend this pattern by adding a second response channel: a visualization payload that the IDE renders as an interactive widget alongside the text.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the local MCP server receives a tool call, it authenticates using your configured AWS credentials and forwards the request to your OpenSearch UI application endpoint as an HTTP API call. OpenSearch UI executes the query against your connected data sources and returns both a structured text summary and a rendered visualization artifact. Supported data sources include OpenSearch domains, serverless collections, and Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus. The MCP server packages these into a single MCP response containing the text content for the agent and the visualization content for the IDE host to render.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The IDE host detects the visualization payload and renders it as an interactive widget in the conversation thread. OpenSearch MCP App generates the visualization server-side by executing code against your actual data. Therefore, the rendered output is deterministic and matches what you would see in your OpenSearch dashboards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="a-tool-call-end-to-end"&gt;A tool call end-to-end&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To illustrate the dual response pattern in practice, consider a trace investigation. The following walkthrough shows what happens when your agent calls the trace investigation MCP App tool.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the agent sends.&lt;/strong&gt; Your agent issues a tool call to the trace investigation MCP App, passing parameters such as the trace ID or a filter like service name and time range. This call travels from your IDE to the local MCP server over the standard MCP protocol.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How the server executes it.&lt;/strong&gt; The local MCP server receives the tool call, authenticates against your AWS credentials, and forwards the request to your OpenSearch UI application endpoint. OpenSearch UI executes the trace query against your connected data sources, retrieves the matching spans, and assembles the response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the dual response contains.&lt;/strong&gt; The MCP server returns two outputs in a single response. The text portion contains a structured summary. It includes the trace ID, total duration, span count, the critical path, and an analysis of where the failure originated. The visualization portion contains an interactive trace waterfall rendered as an MCP App inside your IDE, showing the span hierarchy, timing, and error annotations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How the agent and human each consume it.&lt;/strong&gt; From the text summary, your agent extracts context for its next reasoning step, for example correlating the failing span with related log entries. Meanwhile, you see the interactive trace waterfall in the same conversation thread. You can expand individual spans, inspect attributes, and confirm the root cause visually, without opening a separate browser tab.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="available-mcp-apps"&gt;Available MCP Apps&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP Apps support observability investigation across the lifecycle, with tools that chain together across investigation stages.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="core-investigation-tools"&gt;Core investigation tools&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A typical investigation begins with triage and response tools, which surface active alerts, correlate related alerts across data sources, and present severity breakdowns so your agent can prioritize the issue. After the agent identifies the affected service, log investigation tools search for error patterns and cluster similar log entries to isolate the failure signature. From there, trace investigation tools locate the specific distributed trace, display the span hierarchy and latency breakdown, and pinpoint where the failure originated.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="context-and-visualization-tools"&gt;Context and visualization tools&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To quantify the impact, metric investigation tools execute PromQL queries and perform threshold analysis, while service performance tools provide RED metrics (rate, errors, duration) at the service level. Topology tools render the service map as a dependency graph. The graph shows call volume and error rates across edges so you can scope the impact. Throughout the investigation, dynamic visualization tools generate line, bar, area, and metric charts from queries you specify, and datasets and correlations tools support cross-signal joins and data summaries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="specialized-tools"&gt;Specialized tools&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Specialized tools address emerging needs. AI and agent observability tools trace large language model (LLM) calls and render agent trace maps for teams building their own AI workflows. Stack health tools report cluster status and shard allocation. Instrumentation scoring tools detect telemetry quality gaps so teams can improve their observability coverage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-21227-2-2.png" alt="MCP App trace investigation showing a span hierarchy timeline with span details and AI analysis." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Trace investigation MCP App showing span hierarchy, timeline, and failure origin analysis inside the IDE&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="revisiting-the-on-call-scenario"&gt;Revisiting the on-call scenario&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With MCP Apps, the same on-call investigation now looks like this.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engineer asks the agent: “What’s causing the spike in checkout errors?” The agent investigates by querying logs, correlating with traces, and checking the service map. A dual response arrives containing both a text summary and interactive visualizations (alert view, trace waterfall, and service map) rendered in the same thread. The engineer reviews inline by scrolling through the MCP App visualizations and selecting span details to confirm the scope of impact, without leaving the IDE. Finally, they instruct the agent to draft the issue summary or trigger a remediation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The engineer never leaves the IDE. Investigation, verification, and resolution happen in a single conversation thread. For on-call engineers, this means faster resolution and a more straightforward experience to collaborate with AI agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-21227-3-2.png" alt="MCP App service map showing a call graph with per-service error rates and dependency edges." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Service map MCP App showing dependency graph with error rate color coding and call volume edge widths&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-set-up-the-mcp-server"&gt;Getting started: Set up the MCP server&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Follow these steps to connect your agentic IDE to your OpenSearch UI application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you begin, check that you have the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An OpenSearch UI application with an Observability workspace connected to at least one data source (Amazon OpenSearch Service domains, serverless collections, or Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A compatible agentic IDE (Claude Desktop, VS Code GitHub Copilot, Goose, ChatGPT, or Cursor).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Node.js 22 or later installed locally.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS credentials configured with &lt;code&gt;es:ESHttpGet&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;es:ESHttpPost&lt;/code&gt; permissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-by-step-setup"&gt;Step-by-step setup&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following procedure walks through downloading the server, configuring your IDE, and verifying the connection.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 1: Download and extract the MCP server&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Download and prepare the MCP server package:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to the OpenSearch observability MCP server &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/latest/developerguide/opensearch-observability-mcp-app.html#opensearch-observability-mcp-app-setup" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;download page.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Download the MCP server .zip file.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Extract the archive. The extracted directory contains a &lt;code&gt;server/server.js&lt;/code&gt; file. Note the full path to this file.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 2: Add the MCP server to your IDE&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each supported IDE has an MCP configuration file. The following list shows where to find it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Claude Desktop&lt;/strong&gt;: Settings → Developer → Edit Config.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;VS Code GitHub Copilot&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;code&gt;.vscode/mcp.json&lt;/code&gt; in your workspace, or User Settings → MCP Servers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cursor&lt;/strong&gt;: Settings → MCP → Add Server.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Goose&lt;/strong&gt;: &lt;code&gt;~/.config/goose/mcp.json&lt;/code&gt; (through extensions).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ChatGPT&lt;/strong&gt;: Settings → MCP Plugins → Add.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Open the configuration for your IDE and add the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "mcpServers": {
    "opensearch-observability-stack-mcp": {
      "command": "node",
      "args": ["/path/to/opensearch-observability-stack-mcp/server/server.js"],
      "env": {
        "OS_UI_ENDPOINT": "application-foo-bar.us-west-2.opensearch.amazonaws.com",
        "AWS_REGION": "us-west-2",
        "AWS_PROFILE": "my-profile"
      }
    }
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replace the placeholder values with your OpenSearch UI endpoint, AWS Region, and profile.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To find your OpenSearch UI endpoint:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the Amazon OpenSearch Service console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;Applications&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select your OpenSearch UI application.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Copy the Application URL (for example, &lt;code&gt;application-abc123.us-west-2.opensearch.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step 3: Verify the connection&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After saving the configuration, restart your IDE or reload the MCP server list. Then enter the following prompt in your IDE:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“List available observability data sources”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the agent returns your connected data sources (Amazon OpenSearch Service domains, serverless collections, or Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus workspaces), the MCP server is configured correctly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you receive an error, check that your AWS credentials are active and that your AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policy includes the &lt;code&gt;es:ESHttpGet&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;es:ESHttpPost&lt;/code&gt; actions for your OpenSearch UI application ARN.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tip:&lt;/strong&gt; To test without production data, deploy the OpenTelemetry Demo application to generate sample traces, logs, and metrics in your Amazon OpenSearch Service domain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To remove the MCP server configuration, open your IDE’s MCP settings and delete the &lt;code&gt;opensearch-observability-stack-mcp&lt;/code&gt; entry. Then delete the extracted MCP server directory from your local machine. This setup provisions no cloud resources, so you don’t need AWS side cleanup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-this-matters"&gt;Why this matters&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes how MCP Apps change the on-call workflow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Without MCP Apps&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With MCP Apps&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent returns text → open browser → log in → navigate → verify manually&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent returns text + interactive visualization → review inline&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;You cannot see the underlying data in AI output&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;MCP App results are deterministic (OpenSearch MCP App executes code)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Context-switching between IDE and dashboard tabs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Single conversation thread in your IDE&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent reasons only on its own output&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agent reads MCP App results as additional structured context&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Human verification takes minutes across external platforms&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Verification compressed to seconds, inline with the agent&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With MCP Apps, Amazon OpenSearch Service closes the verification gap in agentic observability. Your AI agent investigates, and the interactive proof arrives in the same thread: no context-switching, no separate logins, no re-running queries. For on-call engineers, this can mean faster resolution. For organizations running agentic observability locally, this provides the operational simplicity you wanted without sacrificing accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Get started today:&lt;/strong&gt; For setup instructions, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/opensearch-service/latest/developerguide/opensearch-observability-mcp-app.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentic observability with MCP Apps&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon OpenSearch Service Developer Guide.&lt;/p&gt;

		<title>Governed reports with Amazon Quick Desktop and Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/governed-reports-with-amazon-quick-desktop-and-amazon-fsx-for-netapp-ontap/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ebbey Thomas]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 25 Aug 2026 16:35:01 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Quick Suite]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">d19c9a80a6c9cff4fad87f27b9628f0939c589c7</guid>

					<description>Build a governed weekly reporting workflow with Amazon Quick Desktop and Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP. An Amazon S3 access point exposes an approved folder to a Quick knowledge base, and a custom skill drafts cited weekly reports and Slack summaries with human review before anything is shared.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/amazon-quick-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick Desktop&lt;/a&gt; brings governed, AI-assisted reporting to the files your team already manages on &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/what-is-fsx-ontap.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP&lt;/a&gt; (FSx for ONTAP), cutting weekly report preparation from hours to minutes. Today, producing those reports takes hours of manual effort each week. Teams re-read the same documents, reformat metrics, and copy summaries into Slack. Leaders need those updates quickly, but they also need to trust the source behind each metric, risk, and recommendation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you build a governed weekly reporting workflow with Amazon Quick Desktop, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-and-agents-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick skills&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/accessing-data-via-s3-access-points.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) access points&lt;/a&gt;, and Slack. Amazon Quick Desktop is a native desktop application that extends Amazon Quick from the browser to the user’s computer. It provides the AI-powered capabilities of the web experience together with desktop-specific capabilities such as&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Local file access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Background processing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A personal knowledge graph.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In Amazon Quick Desktop, you create a skill called &lt;strong&gt;Weekly Business Reporting Assistant&lt;/strong&gt;. A skill is a reusable workflow that gives Amazon Quick task-specific instructions, so users can start the same reporting process each week with a trigger phrase.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow connects a governed set of business files to a guided reporting experience. It keeps source documents under existing storage and access controls, gives Amazon Quick access only to the selected reporting content, and keeps human review in place before artifacts or Slack summaries are shared. The examples use fictitious business data, but the pattern applies to operating reviews, forecast updates, risk reporting, executive briefings, and weekly business reviews.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution makes Amazon Quick Desktop the main workspace for weekly reporting. Business users open Quick Desktop, activate the &lt;strong&gt;Weekly Business Reporting Assistant&lt;/strong&gt; skill, and ask questions such as “What changed since last week’s operating review?” or “Create a one-page weekly report for leadership.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The skill makes the workflow repeatable. It gives Amazon Quick task-specific instructions that cover the source collection, output formatting, citation behavior, and when to ask for clarification. This lets users start from a guided reporting process instead of a blank conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The source files remain in FSx for ONTAP. In this walkthrough, FSx for ONTAP stores the documents that support weekly reporting, such as final weekly reviews, operating plans, forecast summaries, risk registers, and executive templates. Keeping the files in FSx for ONTAP lets storage and business teams continue using their existing file access and governance processes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You choose one reporting folder for the assistant to use. This folder contains the files business owners approve for the workflow. Drafts, outdated files, restricted documents, and unrelated content stay outside this folder.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The S3 access point gives Amazon Quick controlled read access to that selected folder. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; permissions limit what Amazon Quick can list and read.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick uses the selected files to create a searchable source collection called a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/knowledge-base-integrations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;knowledge base&lt;/a&gt;. In this walkthrough, the knowledge base is named &lt;strong&gt;Business Reporting Archive&lt;/strong&gt;. The Weekly Business Reporting Assistant skill uses it to answer questions, cite source documents, create reports, generate decks and PDFs, build visuals, and draft Slack-ready summaries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the report is ready to share, you use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/slack-integration.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Slack integration in Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt; to draft a channel summary. You review and approve the message before posting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 1 shows the workflow. Business files stay in FSx for ONTAP. The S3 access point gives Amazon Quick controlled access to the selected reporting folder. Amazon Quick prepares that content as the Business Reporting Archive knowledge base. Users work in Quick Desktop with the Weekly Business Reporting Assistant skill to create cited reporting artifacts and Slack summaries for approval.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-1.png" alt="Diagram of the governed reporting workflow from Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP through an S3 access point and Amazon Quick to a Slack summary" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Governed weekly reporting workflow with Amazon Quick Desktop, Amazon Quick skills, Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP, Amazon S3 access points, and Slack&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Quick Desktop also builds a personal knowledge graph in &lt;strong&gt;My context&lt;/strong&gt;, connecting people, projects, events, actions, and documents. For example, while preparing a weekly report, a user can identify related people, action items, documents, and Slack channels from connected sources. The Business Reporting Archive remains the governed source for report facts and citations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-2.png" alt="Personal knowledge graph in the My context view of Amazon Quick Desktop connecting people, projects, events, actions, and documents" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Example personal knowledge graph in the My context view of Amazon Quick Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This walkthrough assumes your storage and identity foundation already exists. You configure the access path, Amazon Quick knowledge base, reporting assistant, Slack action connector, and validation workflow. You don’t create the FSx for ONTAP file system itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS account and Amazon Quick requirements&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with access to a Region where Amazon Quick, Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP, and Amazon S3 access points for FSx for ONTAP are supported.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon Quick Enterprise subscription with permissions to create Amazon S3 integrations, knowledge bases, and spaces.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Amazon Quick desktop application installed for the users who run the reporting workflow. Review enterprise setup requirements before a production rollout.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;FSx for ONTAP requirements&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An FSx for ONTAP file system running NetApp ONTAP 9.17.1 or later.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An FSx for ONTAP volume that is mounted and has a junction path, such as /business-reporting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An S3 access point attached to the FSx for ONTAP volume, or permissions to create one. The S3 access point and the FSx for ONTAP volume must be in the same AWS Region and owned by the same AWS account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions to grant Amazon Quick least-privilege read access to the S3 access point and approved object prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An Amazon S3 path in the same AWS Region as your Amazon Quick application.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Slack and testing requirements&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Slack workspace, an approved channel for reviewed summaries, and permissions to configure the Slack action connector.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A focused set of fictitious or approved business files for testing, such as weekly reviews, operating plans, forecast summaries, risk registers, and executive templates.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation"&gt;Implementation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The implementation has seven steps. The following sections walk through each one: prepare the source folder, create the S3 access point, grant Amazon Quick read access, create the Amazon S3 integration and knowledge base, configure Slack, create the reporting assistant, and test the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-prepare-source-content"&gt;Step 1: Prepare source content&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with a narrow folder scope so business users can verify citations against a known set of source files. For this walkthrough, use a mounted FSx for ONTAP volume and create or choose an approved folder such as /business-reporting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Within that folder, organize files around the reporting process. Add the last 8–12 weekly business reviews, the current operating plan, the current forecast summary, and the current risk register. Also add the standard report template that guides the output style. Remove draft, obsolete, archived, and restricted files before indexing, and confirm that file owners agree with the source scope.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;/business-reporting/
    weekly-reviews/
    operating-plans/
    forecasts/
    risk-registers/
    executive-templates/&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 366px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-3.png" alt="The terminal output shows a mounted /mnt/fsx path with folders for forecasts, operating plans, risks, templates, and weekly reviews. The example files include a forecast summary CSV file, an operating plan document, a risk register spreadsheet, a weekly business report template, and two weekly business review documents." width="356"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Example approved reporting folder structure on a mounted FSx for ONTAP volume&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-create-an-s3-access-point"&gt;Step 2: Create an S3 access point&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Attach an S3 access point to the FSx for ONTAP volume. Amazon Quick can then read the approved content through an S3-compatible path. Work with your storage or cloud administrator if you don’t own the FSx for ONTAP configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon FSx&lt;/strong&gt; console in the Region that contains the FSx for ONTAP volume.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the navigation pane, choose &lt;strong&gt;Volumes&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select the mounted FSx for ONTAP volume that contains the approved reporting folder.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;From &lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Create S3 access point&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter an access point name that follows your naming standard, such as business-reporting-ap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure the access point user and network settings according to your governance model. Select a virtual private cloud restriction if your workload requires private network access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create the access point, and then record the access point alias and Amazon Resource Name (ARN). Use the alias in the S3 URI and the ARN in IAM policies.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-4.png" alt="The Amazon S3 console page shows an access point named fsx-ontap-blog-ap. The overview identifies the data source type as FSx for ONTAP and shows the AWS Region, creation date, network origin, S3 URI, ARN, and access point alias. Account-specific values are masked." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: S3 access point overview for an FSx for ONTAP data source&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-grant-amazon-quick-read-access"&gt;Step 3: Grant Amazon Quick read access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Grant only the access Amazon Quick requires to list and read the approved prefix. The following policy is a starting point. Replace the placeholders with your Region, account ID, access point name, and prefix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "ListApprovedReportingPrefix",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": ["s3:ListBucket", "s3:GetBucketLocation"],
            "Resource": "&amp;lt;access-point-arn&amp;gt;"
        },
        {
            "Sid": "ReadApprovedReportingObjects",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": "s3:GetObject",
            "Resource": "&amp;lt;approved-prefix-object-arn&amp;gt;"
        }
    ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;access-point-arn&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;arn:aws:s3:&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;account-id&amp;gt;:accesspoint/&amp;lt;access-point-name&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;. Set &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;approved-prefix-object-arn&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;arn:aws:s3:&amp;lt;region&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;account-id&amp;gt;:accesspoint/&amp;lt;access-point-name&amp;gt;/object/business-reporting/*&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Identify the IAM role or service role that Amazon Quick uses for the Amazon S3 integration in your account. Attach the policy to that role, and grant read access to document-level access control list (ACL) or metadata files that the Amazon S3 integration requires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before you configure the Amazon S3 integration, validate the access point alias and prefix. Verify that the role cannot read folders outside the approved reporting prefix.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 634px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-5.png" alt="The IAM policy editor shows a policy named FSxS3AccessPointAccess. The policy grants GetObject, ListBucket, and GetBucketLocation actions on an S3 access point. The account-specific ARN values are masked." width="624"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Example IAM policy editor with S3 access point permissions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-create-the-amazon-s3-integration"&gt;Step 4: Create the Amazon S3 integration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create an Amazon S3 integration in Amazon Quick and use it to create a knowledge base from the approved prefix. The console might label the path as a bucket URL even when you use an S3 access point alias. Validate the alias path in your account before you publish the assistant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/strong&gt; console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Knowledge&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose an existing Amazon S3 data source, or choose &lt;strong&gt;+ Add account&lt;/strong&gt; to connect a new source.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For &lt;strong&gt;Name&lt;/strong&gt;, enter &lt;strong&gt;FSx ONTAP Business Reports&lt;/strong&gt;. For the S3 location, enter the access point alias path, such as s3:///amzn-s3-demo-business-reporting/.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the knowledge base page, enter &lt;strong&gt;FSx ONTAP Business Reports&lt;/strong&gt; as the knowledge base name. Select only the approved prefix.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After the knowledge base sync, open Spaces in the Amazon Quick console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a new space called &lt;strong&gt;Business Reporting Archive&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add the &lt;strong&gt;FSx ONTAP Business Reports&lt;/strong&gt; knowledge base to this space.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your reporting files require per-user or per-group permissions, configure document-level ACL and metadata settings first. Amazon Quick requires you to make the ACL decision during knowledge base creation. After you choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt;, wait for the first sync to finish.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-6.png" alt="The Amazon Quick knowledge base page shows the FSx ONTAP Business Reports knowledge base. The sync status is completed, the content section lists 53 included S3 prefixes, and the integration section shows an S3 bucket URL that uses the access point alias. Account-specific values are masked." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: Amazon Quick knowledge base created from the approved S3 access point path&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-configure-slack"&gt;Step 5: Configure Slack&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Configure Slack so the assistant can stage business summaries for review and posting to the approved channel. Choose the authentication method that matches your organization’s security requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Amazon Quick console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Connectors&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Amazon Quick desktop application, choose &lt;strong&gt;Settings&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Capabilities&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Connections&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;Browse Connections&lt;/strong&gt;. This opens the Amazon Quick web console to complete the connection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the &lt;strong&gt;Available&lt;/strong&gt; tab, find &lt;strong&gt;Slack&lt;/strong&gt; and choose &lt;strong&gt;Connect&lt;/strong&gt;. For a team-managed app or token-based setup, use the &lt;strong&gt;Create for your team&lt;/strong&gt; tab.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Complete the Slack sign-in or token flow and grant only the required permissions. Use the minimum required Slack OAuth scopes (for example, &lt;code&gt;chat:write&lt;/code&gt; scoped to the approved channel), and rotate credentials on a defined schedule.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If your organization requires connector approval, work with your Amazon Quick administrator to publish the Slack connector to your user or group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm the approved Slack channel, such as #finance-weekly, and document who can approve messages before posting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Return to the desktop application and test with a draft message in a non-production channel before using Slack for weekly reporting.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-6-create-the-reporting-assistant"&gt;Step 6: Create the reporting assistant&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a custom skill in the Amazon Quick desktop application that loads the Business Reporting Archive space and applies reporting guardrails. The skill provides a reusable assistant that users activate with a trigger phrase. For more on skill authoring, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-and-agents-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick skill authoring documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Quick&lt;/strong&gt; desktop application, open a conversation and enter a condensed skill specification like the following. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;Save this as a skill

Skill: "Weekly Business Reporting Assistant"

Trigger: weekly business reviews, operating plans, financial reports, WBR summaries, trend analysis, business reporting deliverables from the Business Reporting Archive

Depends on: quick_suite__spaces

Workflow:

1. [Deterministic] Load Space Context --- search_spaces("Business Reporting Archive") -&amp;gt; list_space_documents(space_id) to get full document list. On failure: "I couldn't load the Business Reporting Archive. Please check that the space is connected and accessible."
2. [Agentic] Greet and Suggest --- Show greeting with 4 example questions:
   - Top 5 changes since last week's operating review
   - Risks repeating across last 4 WBRs
   - One-page weekly report (highlights, risks, action items, asks, key metrics, decisions needed)
   - Six-slide PowerPoint deck for leadership from latest review + operating plan
3. [Agentic] Answer Questions --- Use query_topic or search_relevant_content to find relevant content. Respond using structured format: Executive Summary -&amp;gt; Key Highlights -&amp;gt; Risks/Issues -&amp;gt; Action Items/Asks -&amp;gt; Sources (with doc names + dates cited).
4. [Agentic] Generate Deliverables (if requested) --- Reports structured as: business unit, period covered, key metrics/highlights, risks/blockers, mitigation plans, requests/escalations.

Response tone: Professional business. Always cite specific document name, section, and date.

Safety: Only reference content from the archive. Never fabricate metrics. State gaps clearly.

Lessons Learned:
- Always load space context before greeting
- Always cite doc name + date
- Don't extrapolate metrics not in source docs
- Ask user when period is ambiguous, business unit unclear, or audience/format not specified
- If a metric isn't tracked, name what IS available&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Name the skill &lt;strong&gt;Weekly Business Reporting Assistant&lt;/strong&gt; and set a trigger phrase, such as weekly business report.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Save the skill and confirm that it loads the &lt;strong&gt;Business Reporting Archive&lt;/strong&gt; space.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-7.png" alt="The Weekly Business Reporting Assistant skill specification entered in the Amazon Quick desktop application" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 7: Entering the reporting assistant skill specification in Amazon Quick Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-8.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows a proposed skill configuration. It suggests the skill name weekly-business-reporting, display name Weekly Business Reporting Assistant, trigger phrases, and notes that the skill uses the Business Reporting Archive space." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: Amazon Quick suggests skill defaults before saving the assistant&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-7-use-amazon-quick-desktop-as-the-reporting-workspace"&gt;Step 7: Use Amazon Quick Desktop as the reporting workspace&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you create the skill, activate it from a conversation in the Amazon Quick desktop application. The following example shows a business user moving from a question to a cited report and reviewed Slack summary.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start a new conversation and enter the trigger phrase, such as I need help with business reporting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The skill loads the Business Reporting Archive space and greets you with example questions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-9.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows the assistant greeting. It says the Business Reporting Archive is loaded and offers example questions about weekly changes, repeated risks, a one-page report, and a six-slide PowerPoint deck." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 9: Reporting assistant activated in Amazon Quick Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="3" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter a reporting request, such as What changed since the prior operating review?&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-10.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows a response to a question about what changed since the prior operating review. The response compares March 22 and March 29 weekly business reviews and displays a table of improvements such as digital sales, mobile checkout transactions, customer satisfaction, inventory accuracy, net promoter score, and return rate." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 10: Week-over-week change analysis generated from the Business Reporting Archive&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Review the cited findings before you use the response. Amazon Quick Desktop shows source details and response steps so you can check the documents and dates behind the response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-11.png" alt="The Amazon Quick response lists key developments and open action items. Example developments include mobile checkout scaling, inventory corrections, the Northeast region exceeding the revenue target, and electronics supply timing remaining a high-severity risk. Example action items include alternate supplier contracts, staffing plans, budget approval, and publishing an operating model playbook." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 11: Cited key developments and action items from the weekly reporting response&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-12.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows the response steps and source details behind the response. It displays an activity area with instructions, search activity, and the Business Reporting Archive source used to retrieve information. Sensitive identifiers are masked or synthetic." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 12: Response steps and source details for review to review before using the response&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="4" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ask Amazon Quick to create a weekly report, PowerPoint deck, PDF, or executive visual from the cited insights.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review the proposed plan, suggest changes if needed, and choose &lt;strong&gt;Approve &amp;amp; Build&lt;/strong&gt; when the plan is ready.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-13.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows a plan for a one-page weekly report. The plan includes sections for highlights, key metrics, risks and issues, action items, asks or decisions needed, and sources. The user can suggest changes or choose Approve &amp;amp; Build." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 13: Report creation plan staged for review before Amazon Quick builds the artifact&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="6" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After you approve the plan, Amazon Quick creates the asset in the requested format.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-14.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows a completed weekly report named Retail Business Unit - Weekly Report. The report includes highlights, a key metrics table, risks and issues, action items, asks or decisions needed, and source documents. The response pane offers follow-up options such as changing the report period, editing a section, exporting as Word, or confirming that no changes are needed." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 14: Generated weekly report artifact in Amazon Quick Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ol start="7" type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ask Amazon Quick Desktop to draft a Slack summary for the approved channel.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Review the draft in the conversation and confirm only when it is ready to post.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-15.png" alt="The Amazon Quick desktop application shows a six-bullet Slack summary for #finance-weekly. The draft includes weekly highlights, metrics, a risk, and a decision request. The approval card asks whether to post as-is, edit the message first, or copy without posting." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 15: Slack-ready reporting summary staged for approval in Amazon Quick Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 783px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20626-16.png" alt="The Slack test channel #finance-weekly shows the approved weekly reporting summary with synthetic metrics and source-derived business highlights. User names, workspace identifiers, and internal values are masked." width="773"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 16: Approved weekly reporting summary posted to the Slack test channel&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="test-the-workflow-with-business-prompts"&gt;Test the workflow with business prompts&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After you save the skill, validate the end-to-end workflow with prompts that match real weekly reporting work. Each response must cite specific source documents, include a source list in generated artifacts, and ask for your confirmation before posting to Slack. The following table shows representative prompts and what to look for in the response.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Test area&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prompt&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Expected result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Research&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What are the top five changes since the prior operating review?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A cited summary with source documents and dates.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Risk analysis&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;What risks repeat across the last four weekly business reviews?&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Grouped risks with citations and suggested owners when the source documents include them.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Report creation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create a one-page weekly report with highlights, risks, action items, asks, key metrics, and decisions needed.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A structured report with cited claims and a source list.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Deck creation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create a six-slide PowerPoint deck for leadership from the latest review and operating plan.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A deck or deck outline with title, summary, highlights, risks, decisions, and sources.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Slack distribution&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create a six-bullet Slack summary and ask me before posting.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A Slack-ready message staged for approval before posting.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-governance-best-practices"&gt;Security and governance best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Move from the sample workflow to a shared reporting workflow in stages. Start with one approved folder path and expand after business users validate citation quality. Keep source file permissions aligned with your existing FSx for ONTAP governance model, and use least-privilege IAM permissions for the role that Amazon Quick uses to read the S3 access point.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Use document-level ACLs in the Amazon S3 integration when the knowledge base requires per-user or per-group access controls. Use a dedicated Slack channel for reporting summaries, define who can approve posts, and require human review for board materials, regulatory content, investor communications, or sensitive financial reporting. Review indexed source files on a schedule and remove obsolete content from the approved folder path.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="troubleshooting"&gt;Troubleshooting&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Refer to the following table to diagnose common setup and runtime issues.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Issue&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What to check&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Quick cannot read the source path&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Confirm the S3 access point alias, same-Region requirement, IAM policy resource scope, and Amazon Quick service role permissions.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Knowledge base sync fails&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Confirm that the approved prefix exists, the access point is attached to a mounted FSx for ONTAP volume, and the file system is running NetApp ONTAP 9.17.1 or later.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Responses do not include citations&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Confirm that the Business Reporting Archive space is connected in Amazon Quick Desktop and that the knowledge base files synced successfully.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Responses refer to old files&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Confirm the knowledge base sync status and remove obsolete files from the approved folder path.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Slack posting fails&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Confirm the Slack connector authorization, channel access, required scopes, and whether the connector was published to the user or group.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid future charges or unused access paths, remove only the resources that you created or changed for this walkthrough. These steps reverse the Amazon Quick knowledge base, Slack action connector, S3 access point, and IAM policy changes from the preceding sections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the &lt;strong&gt;Weekly Business Reporting Assistant&lt;/strong&gt; skill from the Amazon Quick desktop application if you no longer need it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the &lt;strong&gt;Business Reporting Archive&lt;/strong&gt; knowledge base if it was created only for this post.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove the Amazon S3 integration from Amazon Quick if no other knowledge bases use it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the S3 access point if it was created only for this workflow and no other workload uses it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove IAM policy statements and access point policies that were added only for this walkthrough.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Remove sample files from the approved folder path if they were used only for testing.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You now have a governed weekly reporting workflow that creates cited business artifacts from file content stored on Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP. The S3 access point exposes only the approved folder path, and Amazon Quick indexes that content through the Amazon S3 integration. Business users can research reporting questions, generate reports and visuals, and prepare Slack summaries from one workspace without moving the source files out of the managed storage workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For executives and business stakeholders, this pattern improves trust in recurring updates. Users review citations before they use the output and approve Slack posts before distribution. For builders, it keeps the first implementation narrow enough to validate governance, access, and reporting quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with one approved folder, one reporting use case, and one reviewed distribution channel. After users trust the citations and output quality, expand the knowledge base to more business units, add document-level ACLs if required, and refine the skill instructions for your internal reporting standards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="for-more-information"&gt;For more information&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, identify one approved reporting folder on your FSx for ONTAP volume and work with your storage team to create an S3 access point for that path. Then create the Amazon Quick knowledge base and reporting assistant skill, and validate the workflow with your first weekly update.&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/amazon-quick-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick on desktop&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/skills-and-agents-desktop.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Quick skill authoring documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/s3-integration.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon S3 integration in Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/quick/latest/userguide/document-and-visual-creation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Document and visual creation with Amazon Quick&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
