		<item>
		<title>Introducing new Ray capabilities on SageMaker HyperPod</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-new-ray-capabilities-on-sagemaker-hyperpod/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Nilesh PS]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 24 Aug 2026 19:32:14 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker Studio]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Foundational (100)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">1869206df2363735c9e3340493ba659c7c99406b</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod now offers managed Ray support on Amazon EKS. Create and monitor Ray clusters, connect JupyterLab and Code Editor notebooks to live clusters, get out-of-the-box observability, and run resilient distributed training and accelerated inference from SageMaker Studio, all with open-source KubeRay and standard Ray APIs.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Today, we are announcing new Ray capabilities on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt; that integrate Ray with the HyperPod purpose-built infrastructure for foundation model training and serving.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray&lt;/a&gt; is an open-source framework that data scientists use to scale distributed Python workloads across clusters of GPUs, from distributed training with Ray Train to model serving with Ray Serve. On Kubernetes, Ray clusters are managed by &lt;a href="https://github.com/ray-project/kuberay" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KubeRay&lt;/a&gt;, an open-source operator that handles cluster lifecycle through custom resources (RayCluster, RayJob, RayService). SageMaker HyperPod provides purpose-built infrastructure for large-scale machine learning (ML) on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon EKS) with built-in node health monitoring and automatic recovery. Until now, running Ray on Kubernetes required data scientists to write YAML manifests, manage Docker image rebuilds for every dependency change, set up kubectl port-forward to access their Ray Dashboard, and configure Prometheus and Grafana manually for observability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this launch, data scientists can create Ray clusters, open the Ray Dashboard and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/implementing-logging-monitoring-cloudwatch/amg-dashboarding-visualization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Grafana&lt;/a&gt; observability dashboards, connect a JupyterLab or Code Editor workspace to their cluster, submit distributed jobs, and configure hung job detection, all from &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-updated.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt;. At the application level, Ray training jobs gain automatic fault tolerance through HyperPod node health monitoring and recovery, plus tiered checkpointing for faster resume through HyperPod distributed tiered storage. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/studio-jumpstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt; integration loads model weights directly into Ray Serve endpoints, with KV cache offloading to tiered storage for serving long-context requests. These capabilities work with open-source &lt;a href="https://github.com/ray-project/kuberay" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;KubeRay&lt;/a&gt; and standard Ray APIs, so existing scripts and workflows run without modification.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through how to get started with each of these capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along with this post, you need an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod cluster with Amazon EKS orchestration&lt;/a&gt; and the following components installed on your cluster:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SageMaker Spaces EKS add-on&lt;/strong&gt;, which enables JupyterLab and Code Editor workspaces in Amazon SageMaker HyperPod that attach to Ray clusters for interactive development.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;HyperPod Observability EKS add-on&lt;/strong&gt;, which collects metrics from Ray workloads and provisions Grafana dashboards in Amazon Managed Grafana.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;KubeRay operator&lt;/strong&gt;, which manages RayCluster, RayJob, and RayService as native Kubernetes resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;HyperPod Ray Endpoint Operator&lt;/strong&gt; (Helm chart), which generates authenticated public endpoints for dashboard access and remote job submission.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You also need a SageMaker Studio domain, which provides the console interface for creating Ray clusters, viewing workloads, opening dashboards, and managing HyperPod Spaces. For full setup instructions, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray on HyperPod getting started guide.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="purpose-built-data-scientist-experience"&gt;Purpose-built data scientist experience&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker Studio now provides a complete Ray development environment. Data scientists can create, manage, and monitor Ray clusters directly from the console without writing Kubernetes manifests or running kubectl commands. You can check out the full experience &lt;a href="https://d1dpyy0tl92esj.cloudfront.net/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;in this interactive demo.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, navigate to SageMaker Studio and choose &lt;strong&gt;HyperPod&lt;/strong&gt;. Select your HyperPod cluster, then go to the &lt;strong&gt;Tasks&lt;/strong&gt; tab. From the task type list, choose &lt;strong&gt;RayCluster&lt;/strong&gt;. The console displays your Ray clusters with their status, instance types, and available actions. To create a new cluster, choose &lt;strong&gt;Create Ray Cluster&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137823" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-2.-create-cluster.png" alt="SageMaker Studio HyperPod Tasks tab listing Ray clusters with a Create Ray Cluster button" width="2166" height="1322"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The creation form prompts you for a cluster name, head and worker instance types, worker count, and container image. By default, clusters use the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/notebooks-available-images.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker Distribution&lt;/a&gt; image, which comes with Ray pre-installed and is managed by AWS with regular vulnerability patching and software upgrades. You can also specify a custom container image if your workload requires additional dependencies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For customers who prefer kubectl or need advanced customization, an inline YAML editor in Studio exposes the full Kubernetes manifest. The KubeRay operator also integrates with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks-operate-console-ui-governance.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod task governance&lt;/a&gt;, so administrators can set compute quotas and scheduling priorities for Ray workloads alongside other training jobs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;During creation, enable remote endpoints so that you can access the Ray Dashboard, submit jobs, and retrieve logs from anywhere with internet access, securely and without local kubectl port-forwarding. To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-dashboard.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Dashboard access.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137824" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-3.-enable-endpoints.png" alt="Ray cluster creation form with remote endpoints enabled" width="2172" height="1316"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the create cluster form is submitted, go back to the tasks tab and it will show the cluster like in the screenshot below. From the &lt;strong&gt;Actions&lt;/strong&gt; menu, you can open the Ray Dashboard, view Grafana metrics, edit the cluster configuration, or delete the cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137825" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-4.-List-clusters.png" alt="Tasks tab showing a running Ray cluster with the Actions menu open" width="2172" height="1326"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the cluster reaches Running status, choose &lt;strong&gt;Open Ray Dashboard&lt;/strong&gt; from the Actions menu. The system generates a short-lived, IAM-authenticated URL scoped to the cluster creator. The Ray Dashboard opens in a new tab, showing cluster health, running jobs, and node status.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137826" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-5-ray-dashboard.png" alt="Ray Dashboard showing cluster health, running jobs, and node status" width="2172" height="1320"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;figure&gt;&lt;/figure&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="remote-job-submission"&gt;Remote job submission&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For production workloads, you can submit jobs remotely to Ray clusters from Studio, your laptop, or continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines using the &lt;code&gt;toolkit-for-ray-on-sagemaker-ai&lt;/code&gt; &lt;a href="https://pypi.org/project/toolkit-for-ray-on-sagemaker-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Python package&lt;/a&gt;. The package handles endpoint resolution and EKS API credential generation through IAM authentication, so you use standard Ray job submission APIs with a SageMaker-aware address resolver:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;$ aws eks update-kubeconfig --name &amp;lt;eks-cluster-name&amp;gt; --region &amp;lt;region&amp;gt;
$ pip install toolkit-for-ray-on-sagemaker-ai
$ ray job submit --address sagemaker_ray://&amp;lt;ray-cluster-name&amp;gt;/&amp;lt;namespace&amp;gt; \
     --working-dir &amp;lt;your-code-directory&amp;gt; \
     --python your-code.py
# To list ray jobs
$ ray job list --address sagemaker_ray://&amp;lt;ray-cluster-name&amp;gt;/&amp;lt;namespace&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="interactive-development-with-sagemaker-spaces"&gt;Interactive development with SageMaker Spaces&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Data scientists can attach a Ray cluster to a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks-cluster-ide.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod JupyterLab or Code Editor space&lt;/a&gt; from Studio. The space joins the cluster as a zero-compute worker node, giving the notebook full native Ray driver access. You select your cluster from a list during space creation and begin working immediately.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After creating a HyperPod JupyterLab or Code Editor space, a Ray cluster integration option appears in the configuration panel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137827" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-6.-Select-RayCluster-in-spaces.png" alt="HyperPod space configuration panel showing the Ray cluster integration option" width="2176" height="1324"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Select the Ray cluster you want your workspace to connect to.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137828" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-7.-attach-ray-cluster.png" alt="Selecting the Ray cluster to attach to the workspace during space creation" width="2166" height="1320"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Attaching a Ray cluster restarts the space. Once it restarts, open JupyterLab or Code Editor in your browser and call &lt;code&gt;ray.init(address="auto")&lt;/code&gt;. You are connected to your Ray cluster and can run distributed workloads as if you were on the head node. Ray’s &lt;code&gt;runtime_env&lt;/code&gt; parameter lets you inject Python dependencies at runtime without rebuilding container images, and you can scale workers up or down without recreating the cluster. For example, a data scientist training a model can start prototyping in a notebook with a single worker, then scale to four GPU workers by changing one line in &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/train/api/doc/ray.train.ScalingConfig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ScalingConfig&lt;/a&gt;. The training runs distributed across the attached Ray cluster while the notebook remains interactive for monitoring progress, adjusting hyperparameters, or inspecting intermediate results. The entire workflow stays within the notebook.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137829" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-8-Screenshot-2026-08-17-at-2.54.45 PM.png" alt="JupyterLab notebook connected to a Ray cluster running a distributed training job" width="3142" height="868"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;figure&gt;
 &lt;figcaption aria-hidden="true"&gt;&lt;/figcaption&gt;
&lt;/figure&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-ide-notebooks.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IDEs and Notebooks with Ray.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="out-of-the-box-observability"&gt;Out-of-the-box observability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Setting up Ray observability on Kubernetes with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prometheus/latest/userguide/what-is-Amazon-Managed-Service-Prometheus.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Service for Prometheus&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/prescriptive-guidance/latest/implementing-logging-monitoring-cloudwatch/amg-dashboarding-visualization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Managed Grafana&lt;/a&gt; is a multi-step process that involves installing Helm charts, creating PodMonitors and ServiceMonitors, configuring IAM roles for SigV4 signing, and manually importing dashboard JSON files.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The HyperPod Observability EKS add-on now handles all of this. It automatically discovers Ray head and worker pods, scrapes their metrics endpoints, and provisions four pre-built Grafana dashboards in Amazon Managed Grafana: Ray Core, Ray Data, Ray Train, and Ray Serve. You do not need to create PodMonitors, configure scrape targets, or import dashboard JSON files.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All four dashboards are organized under a &lt;strong&gt;Ray&lt;/strong&gt; folder in Amazon Managed Grafana and support filtering metrics by specific Ray cluster. The &lt;strong&gt;Open Grafana&lt;/strong&gt; action from the cluster list (shown in the first screenshot) takes you directly to the metrics for your specific cluster in one click. The dashboards appear alongside existing HyperPod infrastructure dashboards (GPU, EFA, task governance), so operations teams see Ray workload metrics and cluster health in one place.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignleft size-full wp-image-137830" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/24/ml-21715-9-grafana.png" alt="Amazon Managed Grafana Ray dashboard showing Ray workload metrics" width="2982" height="1508"&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="resilient-training"&gt;Resilient training&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker HyperPod provides three layers of resilience for Ray training workloads: automatic node recovery when hardware fails, hung job detection when training stalls, and tiered checkpointing for fast recovery after either event.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="automatic-node-recovery"&gt;Automatic node recovery&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker HyperPod continuously monitors node health and can &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks-resiliency-node-recovery.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;automatically replace faulty nodes&lt;/a&gt; so your Ray training jobs can run for as long as needed without manual restarts. When a node is replaced, Ray reschedules worker pods onto the new healthy node. If your training code saves checkpoints periodically and includes logic to resume from the latest checkpoint, the job picks up where it left off. This requires no changes to your existing Ray training code. You only need to configure a sufficient number of retries in your &lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/train/api/doc/ray.train.FailureConfig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;RayJob’s FailureConfig&lt;/a&gt; so that jobs recover automatically rather than failing permanently on the first interruption. To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-node-recovery.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Automatic node recovery with Ray.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="hung-job-detection"&gt;Hung job detection&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Distributed training jobs can hang without producing errors. A single pod fails (because of a network partition, storage mount issue, or hardware fault), and every other pod blocks at the next collective operation, waiting indefinitely. GPUs stay allocated with memory loaded but produce no useful compute. Because there is no error message or crash, data scientists often discover the problem hours later when they check job progress manually. At scale, a few hours of undetected hang time across dozens of GPUs represents significant wasted cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker HyperPod EKS now includes a per-node Job Monitoring Agent that detects these conditions for Ray Train workloads automatically, with no changes to your code. The agent monitors multiple node-level and job-level signals to determine when a training job has stalled, and notifies the user through the cluster’s Amazon CloudWatch log group and the Ray Train Grafana dashboard provisioned by the HyperPod Observability add-on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For custom detection rules, data scientists can use the &lt;code&gt;toolkit-for-ray-on-sagemaker-ai&lt;/code&gt; library to define log patterns and timeout thresholds. When the configured action is &lt;code&gt;cancel&lt;/code&gt;, HyperPod terminates the hung worker process and Ray Train’s built-in FailureConfig restarts workers from the last checkpoint. To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-hung-job-detection.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod Hung Job Detection on Ray.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tiered-checkpointing"&gt;Tiered checkpointing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fast checkpoint recovery is critical for both node replacement and hung job restart scenarios. The &lt;code&gt;amzn-sagemaker-checkpointing&lt;/code&gt; library integrates with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/managed-tier-checkpointing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod managed tiered checkpointing&lt;/a&gt;, which writes checkpoints to local disk and asynchronously uploads them to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3). When a job restarts, the library checks HyperPod Tiered Storage first. If the checkpoint is still available there, recovery is faster than restoring from Amazon S3. For large models, this can reduce recovery time compared to restoring directly from Amazon S3. To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-tiered-storage.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HyperPod Tiered Storage on Ray.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="accelerated-inference"&gt;Accelerated inference&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.ray.io/en/latest/serve/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray Serve&lt;/a&gt; is Ray’s framework for deploying ML models as scalable, production-ready endpoints. It supports multi-model composition, autoscaling, and works with serving engines like vLLM. SageMaker HyperPod supports Ray Serve workloads on EKS, so you can deploy and scale inference endpoints on your HyperPod cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/jumpstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SageMaker JumpStart&lt;/a&gt; provides a catalog of pre-trained models. With this launch, the &lt;code&gt;toolkit-for-ray-on-sagemaker-ai&lt;/code&gt; library now includes a JumpStart model loader that downloads model weights from the JumpStart catalog and deploys them directly into Ray Serve on HyperPod without manual weight download, model configuration, or container setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One common challenge with large language model (LLM) serving is that inference latency grows with context length. Each new token requires recalculating attention over all previous tokens, making long documents and multi-turn conversations slow and expensive. SageMaker HyperPod addresses this with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/managed-tiered-kv-cache-and-intelligent-routing-for-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managed Tiered KV Cache&lt;/a&gt;. The tiered cache stores attention key-value vectors in CPU memory on each node (L1) and on HyperPod Tiered Storage for cross-instance sharing (L2). Your Ray Serve deployments can take advantage of the Tiered Storage KV caching capabilities in SageMaker HyperPod with minimal code changes, reducing time-to-first-token for multi-turn conversations and long-document workloads. To learn more, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray-accelerated-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accelerated Ray Inference on SageMaker HyperPod.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To free up compute capacity on your cluster, delete any Ray clusters you created during this walkthrough. From the SageMaker Studio Tasks tab, select your Ray cluster and choose &lt;strong&gt;Delete&lt;/strong&gt; from the Actions menu. If you created a HyperPod cluster, you can &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-eks-operate-console-ui-delete-cluster.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;delete the cluster&lt;/a&gt; from the Amazon SageMaker AI console to stop incurring charges for the underlying compute instances. If you installed any prerequisite add-ons for testing, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/removing-an-add-on.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;uninstall them from EKS console&lt;/a&gt; to free up compute capacity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walked through how Amazon SageMaker HyperPod now provides a complete Ray experience on EKS, from cluster creation and interactive notebooks to resilient training and accelerated inference. All of this works with open-source KubeRay and standard Ray APIs, so existing scripts run without modification. We’d like to thank Dhawal Parkar, Pradeep Cruz, Mark Vinciguerra, and Giuseppe Angelo Porcelli for their contributions to this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This integration is available today in all AWS Regions where SageMaker HyperPod EKS is supported. To get started, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker HyperPod documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/sagemaker-hyperpod-ray.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Ray on HyperPod getting started guide&lt;/a&gt;. You can also explore the full workflow in this &lt;a href="https://d1dpyy0tl92esj.cloudfront.net/overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;interactive demo&lt;/a&gt;. If you’d like to discuss how Ray on HyperPod can support your workloads, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/contact-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;contact an AWS representative&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Democratizing institutional knowledge: Building an AI-powered knowledge management system with AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/democratizing-institutional-knowledge-building-an-ai-powered-knowledge-management-system-with-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Nneoma Okoroafor]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 24 Aug 2026 18:59:15 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Generative AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">514f64a1d1a2cab71210818ae36ea45416707064</guid>

					<description>Learn how to build a customizable, smart-caching knowledge management system on AWS that captures and delivers institutional (tribal) knowledge through a voice-first AI avatar. The accelerator uses Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases for retrieval-augmented generation and deploys in hours with AWS CloudFormation.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Organizations across industries struggle with managing institutional knowledge, the collective wisdom and experience accumulated over years of operations. This “tribal knowledge” often disappears when key personnel leave, creating knowledge gaps that impact efficiency and innovation. Traditional documentation methods have proven inadequate, often resulting in outdated or inaccessible information when it’s needed most.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we present a customizable, smart-caching cloud-based solution that captures, maintains, and delivers institutional knowledge through an intelligent avatar system, powered by AWS services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="who-should-use-this-solution"&gt;Who should use this solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations across diverse industries can use this system to preserve critical knowledge. For example, manufacturing organizations can capture production procedures and maintenance protocols before experienced technicians retire. Other organizations like healthcare facilities, financial services firms, energy companies, and government agencies can also tailor their use cases with the solution. Knowledge workers can access procedures and policies through natural language queries instead of searching multiple repositories, while subject matter experts and retiring employees can upload documentation to preserve their expertise for future generations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations can deploy the system with desktop browser access for detailed research, voice interaction for hands-free operation, and text-based queries for quick reference adapting to the specific operational contexts of each industry.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our solution uses AWS services to create a scalable, configurable knowledge management system that you can adapt to your organization’s needs. At its core, the architecture combines advanced AI capabilities with robust cloud infrastructure to deliver an intuitive, responsive knowledge delivery system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The foundation of our solution is a browser-based interface that supports both text and voice interactions, making knowledge accessible through natural conversation. This interface connects to a configurable avatar system that can work with any AI avatar solution, providing organizations with the flexibility to choose or change their preferred avatar technology.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-1-2.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing a browser interface connecting through Amazon Cognito and Amazon API Gateway to AWS Lambda, Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, Amazon S3, Amazon OpenSearch Serverless, and Amazon DynamoDB." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture for the knowledge management system&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Behind the scenes, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cognito/?p=pm&amp;amp;c=cog&amp;amp;z=4" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt; secures access management, while Amazon API Gateway provides controlled, monitored access to the system’s components. The knowledge-processing core uses Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases for managed Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG): institutional knowledge stored in Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) is the data source, and Amazon Bedrock handles chunking, embedding (using Amazon Titan Text Embeddings), and retrieval, grounding each answer in your own documents. The knowledge base is backed by an Amazon OpenSearch Serverless vector store. Amazon DynamoDB provides response caching, and AWS Lambda functions orchestrate the workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost note:&lt;/strong&gt; The Amazon OpenSearch Serverless vector store is created in your account by the deployment and bills per OpenSearch Compute Unit (OCU) with an always-on minimum, independent of query volume. Treat this as a standing baseline cost (on the order of a few hundred USD per month at the default floor) when budgeting. It is the largest fixed component of the solution’s cost. See Cost optimization through smart caching for how caching reduces the variable inference cost on top of this baseline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-this-solution"&gt;Why this solution?&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations seeking to preserve institutional knowledge have several approaches available, from building custom solutions on Amazon Bedrock to deploying text-based chat agents. This solution differentiates through three core capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Voice-first, avatar-driven delivery:&lt;/strong&gt; This solution is built around voice interaction with an AI-powered avatar rather than a text-only chat interface. Non-technical end users need little to no learning curve. They speak to the avatar and receive spoken answers, the same way they would ask a colleague. Workers in connected settings such as training rooms, control rooms, quality labs, and maintenance-planning offices can query the system hands-free while reviewing procedures. Avatar-based interaction can also increase adoption and trust among non-technical populations compared to text-only chatbots. (This is a cloud-connected design. See the connectivity requirement under Scalability and performance characteristics.)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Simplicity for knowledge owners:&lt;/strong&gt; Content management requires no technical expertise. Knowledge owners simply upload existing documents into Amazon S3 (Word, PDF, plain text, Markdown, or JSON). An ingestion sync then chunks and embeds each document into the vector store, after which it becomes queryable. New documents are queryable shortly after upload, once that automated sync completes. It is not instantaneous. There is no need to restructure content, tag metadata, or hand-build a retrieval pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rapid deployment with built-in cost optimization:&lt;/strong&gt; The full prototype deploys in hours through AWS CloudFormation. A built-in DynamoDB cache reuses previous answers for repeated questions, reducing variable AI inference cost. In our testing, cache hit rates of 50–70 percent were achievable for workloads dominated by repeated questions. Actual savings depend on how repetitive your query mix is.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-this-compares-to-alternatives"&gt;How this compares to alternatives&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building a custom solution on Amazon Bedrock provides full architectural control but requires independently designing and integrating voice processing, avatar rendering, caching, and retrieval pipelines. This typically takes weeks to months and assumes deep technical expertise across multiple AWS services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Text-based chat agents such as Amazon Q and custom Amazon Bedrock chat interfaces offer a faster path to deployment and excel at typed knowledge retrieval. This solution adds voice-first interaction and avatar engagement on top, which improves adoption among frontline workers who benefit from hands-free access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution fills the gap: a production-quality accelerator deployable in hours, voice-first with visual avatar engagement, and cost-optimized through smart caching. For organizations that need institutional knowledge in the hands of non-technical workers quickly and affordably, this accelerator alleviates the complexity of assembling these capabilities independently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="when-to-choose-this-solution"&gt;When to choose this solution&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Choose this accelerator when your organization needs to:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deliver institutional knowledge to non-technical or frontline workers through voice and visual interaction.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy quickly with a proven prototype rather than building from scratch.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reduce AI inference costs through intelligent caching.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Preserve knowledge from retiring experts with minimal friction.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-journey"&gt;Implementation journey&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The implementation journey consists of three main phases: knowledge foundation setup, infrastructure deployment, and AI integration. Each phase builds upon the previous one to create a comprehensive knowledge management system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 1: Knowledge foundation setup.&lt;/strong&gt; The implementation process begins with organizing institutional knowledge. Documents are uploaded to the Amazon S3 knowledge repository, and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases ingests them from there. If your source content lives in other systems or formats, you can optionally add an AWS Glue ETL job to convert it into an AI-optimized format before uploading. This ETL step is a separate, optional integration and is not created by the CloudFormation deployment. Organizations whose documents are already in the supported formats listed below can upload directly to S3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Key considerations:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Supported formats&lt;/strong&gt;: Documents can be in various formats (Word, PDF, plain text, Markdown, JSON).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optimal output&lt;/strong&gt;: Markdown (.md) or structured JSON are recommended for best AI retrieval performance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ETL handles the conversion:&lt;/strong&gt; If you use the optional AWS Glue ETL job, it transforms source documents into the recommended output format before they are uploaded to S3.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For detailed guidance on using AWS Glue for data extraction and transformation, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/glue/latest/dg/author-job-glue.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue ETL documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 2: Infrastructure deployment.&lt;/strong&gt; The core infrastructure deployment begins with AWS CloudFormation templates, facilitating consistent and repeatable deployments. This phase includes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Setting up Amazon Cognito for secure user authentication and access controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configuring API Gateway endpoints to manage component communication.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Establishing S3-based repositories for knowledge storage.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implementing DynamoDB for response caching.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Phase 3: AI integration.&lt;/strong&gt; The final phase integrates the AI capabilities and processing pipeline:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configuring Amazon Bedrock for knowledge processing and query understanding.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Setting up Lambda functions for request orchestration and response handling.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Implementing the audio processing pipeline: 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Amazon Transcribe for converting voice input to text.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;Amazon Polly for converting text responses to natural speech.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Integrating the chosen avatar system for human-like interactions.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system maintains high performance through an intelligent caching algorithm that significantly reduces response times while preserving information accuracy. The combination of these components creates an experience where you can interact with the knowledge base through your preferred method of communication, whether text or voice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Implementation steps:&lt;/strong&gt; This guide outlines the steps to deploy the Auto Demo application. The steps that follow are written and tested for &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;, which we chose because it has the broadest availability of the foundation models (FMs) and avatar streaming used here. If you deploy in another AWS Region, confirm that your chosen Amazon Bedrock models and the avatar provider are available there, and update the hardcoded Region in the source, before you begin.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Initial deployment requires technical capabilities from IT or DevOps teams for one-time setup, including basic AWS Management Console navigation, infrastructure-as-code understanding, and identity management familiarity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;End users require no technical capabilities. The conversational interface alleviates barriers. Users simply access a URL and interact naturally through text or voice as they would with a human expert.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="aws-account"&gt;AWS account&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to an AWS account with permissions to create S3 buckets, CloudFormation stacks, and invoke Amazon Bedrock models.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Appropriate AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions for deploying and managing AWS resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="required-iam-permissions-by-service"&gt;Required IAM permissions by service&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The CloudFormation stack creates and manages resources across multiple AWS services. To deploy and operate this solution, the deploying IAM role or user requires permissions for the following services:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock (foundation model invocation and Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases retrieval)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon S3 (knowledge base storage and application files)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Lambda (serverless compute for request orchestration)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Cognito (user authentication and access management)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;API Gateway (HTTP endpoint management)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Serverless (vector store backing the Amazon Bedrock knowledge base)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Polly (text-to-speech for voice responses)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Transcribe (speech-to-text for voice input)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon CloudWatch Logs (centralized logging)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;IAM PassRole (service-role delegation for the Lambda and knowledge-base execution roles)&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; For production deployments, follow the principle of least privilege by scoping resource ARNs to your specific account and Region. The CloudFormation stack automatically creates the necessary execution roles with appropriately scoped permissions for runtime operations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Foundation model access:&lt;/strong&gt; This solution uses Amazon Titan Text Embeddings (&lt;code&gt;amazon.titan-embed-text-v1&lt;/code&gt;) to embed documents into the vector store, and Amazon Nova Pro (&lt;code&gt;amazon.nova-pro-v1:0&lt;/code&gt;) and Anthropic Claude 3 Sonnet (&lt;code&gt;anthropic.claude-3-sonnet-20240229-v1:0&lt;/code&gt;) as the selectable models for generating answers. Amazon Bedrock provides automatic access to Amazon-owned serverless models, so Amazon Nova Pro and Amazon Titan Text Embeddings are available by default with no manual enablement. Anthropic Claude models aren’t covered by automatic access in all Regions: before your first Claude request, complete the one-time Anthropic model-access request in the Amazon Bedrock console and make sure your account has the required AWS Marketplace subscription permissions. Account administrators can further restrict or substitute models through IAM policies and Service Control Policies (SCPs). For more information, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/security/simplified-amazon-bedrock-model-access/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock now provides automatic access to serverless foundation models in your AWS Region&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough-code-installation-steps"&gt;Walkthrough: Code installation steps&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Get the application source code by cloning the public &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-knowledge-management-avatar-system" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository&lt;/a&gt; on the GitHub website. The application is under the &lt;code&gt;bedkbauto/&lt;/code&gt; folder (the &lt;code&gt;src&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;web&lt;/code&gt; subfolders).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="create-the-s3-bucket"&gt;Create the S3 bucket&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Log in to the AWS Management Console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to the S3 service.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a new S3 bucket to store the application files.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create additional folders to follow this structure: &lt;code&gt;s3://&amp;lt;amzn-s3-demo-bucket&amp;gt;/content/bedkbauto/&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="upload-application-files"&gt;Upload application files&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Unzip the downloaded folder containing the application source code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Upload the unzipped folder contents to the S3 bucket created in the previous step. Only upload the &lt;code&gt;src&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;web&lt;/code&gt; folders.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-2-2.jpeg" alt="The unzipped application folder showing the src and web subfolders to upload." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: The src and web subfolders to upload to Amazon S3&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="retrieve-the-s3-object-url"&gt;Retrieve the S3 object URL&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Navigate to the S3 console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Locate the &lt;code&gt;setup.json&lt;/code&gt; file in the &lt;code&gt;src&lt;/code&gt; folder of the uploaded application files.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Copy the URL of the &lt;code&gt;setup.json&lt;/code&gt; file. It should look similar to this: &lt;code&gt;https://&amp;lt;amzn-s3-demo-bucket&amp;gt;/content/bedkbauto/src/setup.json&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="create-the-cloudformation-stack"&gt;Create the CloudFormation stack&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Go to the &lt;a href="https://us-east-1.console.aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CloudFormation&lt;/a&gt; console.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create stack&lt;/strong&gt;, and then choose &lt;strong&gt;With new resources (standard)&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the &lt;strong&gt;Specify template&lt;/strong&gt; step, choose &lt;strong&gt;Amazon S3 URL&lt;/strong&gt;, and then paste the URL you copied earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-3-2.jpeg" alt="CloudFormation Create stack page with Amazon S3 URL selected and the template URL entered." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Specifying the template with the Amazon S3 URL&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;During stack creation, provide these parameters: a stack name, your &lt;strong&gt;email address&lt;/strong&gt; (which receives a one-time password for app login), a &lt;strong&gt;username&lt;/strong&gt; for app login such as &lt;code&gt;demo-user&lt;/code&gt;, and the name of the S3 bucket you created earlier such as &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;amzn-s3-demo-bucket&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-4-2.jpeg" alt="CloudFormation parameters page showing fields for stack name, email address, username, and S3 bucket name." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Providing the stack parameters&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Leave the remaining settings as default and select the two acknowledgment check boxes, &lt;strong&gt;I acknowledge that AWS CloudFormation might create IAM resources with custom names&lt;/strong&gt; and &lt;strong&gt;I acknowledge that AWS CloudFormation might require the following capability: CAPABILITY_AUTO_EXPAND&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-5-2.jpeg" alt="CloudFormation acknowledgment check boxes for creating IAM resources and the CAPABILITY_AUTO_EXPAND capability." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Acknowledging the required capabilities&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;On the review page, choose &lt;strong&gt;Submit&lt;/strong&gt; to create the stack.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-6-2.jpeg" alt="CloudFormation review page with the Submit button to create the stack." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: Reviewing and submitting the stack&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="verify-deployment"&gt;Verify deployment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;After the CloudFormation stack creation is complete, open the &lt;strong&gt;Outputs&lt;/strong&gt; tab to access the application.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-7-2.jpeg" alt="CloudFormation Outputs tab listing the FrontendURL for the deployed application." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 7: Locating the FrontendURL on the Outputs tab&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Copy the &lt;code&gt;FrontendURL&lt;/code&gt; value to a new tab and press &lt;strong&gt;Enter&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Enter the username you set during stack creation and the password sent to the email address that you provided.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-8-1.jpeg" alt="Application login page prompting for a username and the one-time password." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: Signing in with the temporary password&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Change to your preferred password and choose &lt;strong&gt;Send&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-9-2.jpeg" alt="Prompt to change to a preferred password on first sign-in." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 9: Setting a new password&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can now access the UI.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-10-1.jpeg" alt="The Knowledge Base Demo interface loaded in the browser after sign-in." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 10: The deployed Knowledge Base Demo interface&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/10/ML-18543-11.png" alt="The Knowledge Base Demo answering a question about avoiding hallucinations through the on-screen avatar." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 11: The Knowledge Base Demo answering a question through the avatar&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="optimizing-performance-and-user-experience"&gt;Optimizing performance and user experience&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Our knowledge management system employs a dual-layer caching strategy to maximize response speed and efficiency. At the front end, browser-side caching stores recent interactions directly in the user’s browser memory using an LRU (Least Recently Used) algorithm. This local cache minimizes server requests for repeated questions, providing instant responses for common queries. The backend DynamoDB cache maintains a broader knowledge repository with intelligent TTL (Time-To-Live) settings that vary based on content type, longer retention for fundamental knowledge, medium-term for operational procedures, and short-term for time-sensitive information. This tiered approach makes sure information stays fresh while reducing the processing load on AI services. Note that the current implementation matches cache entries on the exact query text, so the cache is most effective when users repeat established, frequently recurring questions. Semantic (embedding-based) matching is a natural enhancement that is not included in this prototype.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The avatar system serves as more than a visual interface. It’s a key component in making complex information accessible and engaging. Built on DeepBrain AI technology, the avatar provides real-time lip synchronization, contextual facial expressions, and natural gestures that match the conversation flow. Organizations can customize the avatar’s appearance, voice, and behavior to align with their brand identity and communication style.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The technical implementation uses WebRTC for smooth video streaming and integrates with Amazon Polly for natural speech synthesis, while WebSocket connections enable responsive avatar control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Performance monitoring through Amazon CloudWatch provides continuous insights into cache effectiveness and response times. The system automatically adjusts its caching strategy based on usage patterns, warming frequently accessed information and optimizing TTL settings. This combination of efficient caching and natural avatar interaction creates a responsive, engaging knowledge delivery system that scales with organizational needs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scalability-and-performance-characteristics"&gt;Scalability and performance characteristics&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The serverless architecture provides automatic horizontal scaling across components, making sure the system handles growth from pilot to enterprise deployment without manual intervention. Functions provision additional execution environments as concurrent requests grow, handling traffic spikes without performance degradation. The API layer provides throttling while scaling to handle varying load, and the caching layer automatically adjusts capacity as query volume fluctuates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Concurrent user support scales with configuration. The following figures are indicative estimates from our testing, not guarantees, and depend on Region, model choice, document size, cache hit rate, and configured service quotas. The default setup handled roughly 50 to 100 concurrent users with sub-second responses for cached queries. Raising the relevant service quotas extends this to roughly 500 to 1,000 concurrent users, and enterprise-scale usage of over 5,000 concurrent users is achievable with the same architecture but requires proactive service-quota increases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Performance varies with caching effectiveness. In our testing, cached responses returned in well under a second, while fresh knowledge-base queries took roughly 2–4 seconds depending on the model and document size. Voice interaction adds speech-processing time on top. These are indicative figures, so measure against your own workload before committing to a latency target. Note that this is a cloud-dependent design: every query round-trips to AWS, and the avatar relies on live WebRTC/WebSocket streaming, which needs stable, low-latency, reasonably high-bandwidth connectivity. There is no edge, offline, or degraded-mode capability in this prototype, so it fits connected environments (training rooms, engineering and maintenance-planning offices, quality labs, control rooms, kiosk stations) rather than disconnected or intermittently connected plant-floor or OT settings. Supporting those would require a separate edge pattern such as local caching, offline fallback, and graceful degradation to text.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For organizations planning growth, start with default configuration for pilots, monitor metrics to identify bottlenecks, increase capacity limits when approaching thresholds, and request service quota increases proactively for enterprise deployments. The serverless architecture makes sure you pay only for actual usage, making it cost-effective to start small and scale as adoption grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cost-optimization-through-smart-caching"&gt;Cost optimization through smart caching&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A key contributor to efficiency is the caching layer, which reduces the largest variable cost: repeated calls to foundation models. The DynamoDB cache stores and reuses answers for repeated queries, so a question that has already been answered does not trigger another model invocation. In our testing this reduced inference cost roughly in proportion to the cache hit rate. For workloads where 50–70 percent of questions were repeats, inference cost fell by a comparable amount. Because the saving is a direct function of the hit rate, we quote a single figure rather than treating the two as independent guarantees. This variable saving sits on top of the fixed Amazon OpenSearch Serverless baseline noted earlier, so budget for both.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system’s smart-caching mechanism is particularly effective for tribal knowledge use cases where many questions are related to established procedures, policies and institutional practices. By implementing time-based cache invalidation, we maintain an optimal balance between cost efficiency and information accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="maintaining-and-evolving-the-system"&gt;Maintaining and evolving the system&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key to long-term success lies in treating the knowledge management system as a dynamic that grows with your organization. To keep the knowledge base current, the deployment configures Amazon S3 event notifications on the knowledge repository: when a document is added or removed, the bucket invokes an AWS Lambda function that automatically starts a Bedrock Knowledge Bases ingestion job, re-embedding the change into the vector store with no manual step. Because ingestion is not instantaneous, updated content becomes queryable shortly after that sync completes. The content-type-aware TTL settings on the response cache complement this, so fundamental processes persist longer while dynamic information refreshes more frequently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To support accuracy, answers are generated with Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, which grounds each response in passages retrieved from your own verified documents rather than the model’s general training data. This grounding reduces the likelihood of fabricated or off-base answers, and the knowledge base can return source citations that you can surface to users for verification. Grounding reduces but does not eliminate the risk of incorrect answers: for high-consequence or safety-relevant decisions, keep a human in the loop and treat the system as decision support rather than an authoritative source. Explicit confidence thresholds or answer-validation checks are a recommended enhancement for such use cases and are not implemented in this prototype.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Security remains integral to system maintenance. Regular automated audits verify access controls and data protection measures, while modular architecture supports straightforward security updates. Organizations can integrate new security requirements or compliance measures without disrupting existing functionality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system’s flexibility extends to content management. As teams document new procedures or update existing ones, they can upload them to the knowledge base, which automatically incorporates these changes. This continuous evolution makes sure the system remains a reliable source of institutional knowledge, adapting to organizational changes while maintaining historical context where needed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With our knowledge management solution, you can build a powerful tool for preserving institutional knowledge. By using AWS services and following a modular design, organizations can maintain their intellectual capital while facilitating efficient knowledge transfer across their workforce. The system’s flexibility and scalability make it suitable for various industries and use cases, providing a foundation for sustainable knowledge management in the digital age. To get started, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-bedrock-knowledge-management-avatar-system" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt; on the GitHub website and deploy the solution in your own AWS account. To learn more, explore Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases in the Amazon Bedrock console.&lt;/p&gt;

		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Jeffrey Damick]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 24 Aug 2026 16:22:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">f61f463ab9563e66a529a7da9c9531a100084eab</guid>

					<description>AWS Agent Registry gives your organization a centralized, searchable catalog for agents, tools, and skills. It works with the open Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) standard to enable cross-environment discovery and governance at scale.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;h3&gt;How AWS Agent Registry and the Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD) specification enable cross-environment discovery for your agents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As organizations scale their use of artificial intelligence (AI) agents and tools, finding the right resource becomes the hard part. Teams build Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, deploy agents, and create specialized tools, but without a central catalog, those resources stay siloed. Developers locate a resource, vet it, connect it, and maintain that connection manually. Worse, configuring an agent for one AI client doesn’t make it available to another.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This was manageable when a team wired up a handful of tools. It doesn’t scale to a growing number of agents, MCP servers, skills, and APIs now spread across public registries and private enterprise estates.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="aws-agent-registry-a-centralized-searchable-catalog"&gt;AWS Agent Registry: A centralized, searchable catalog&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Agent Registry gives your organization a centralized catalog for agents, MCP servers, tools, agent skills, and custom resources. It’s built around two core concepts:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Registries. A registry is a catalog you create in your AWS account with its own authorization configuration and approval settings. You can run a single org-wide registry or separate registries by resource type, stage, or team. With cross-account sharing, a registry can serve your entire AWS Organization.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Registry Records. A record represents an individual resource, capturing metadata that describes what it is, what it does, and how to reach it.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-workflow-is-straightforward"&gt;The workflow is straightforward&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Create a registry&lt;/strong&gt;: An administrator creates a registry, configures approval settings, and sets up authorization using either AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) or a JSON Web Token (JWT) from your corporate identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Publish records&lt;/strong&gt;: A publisher describes their MCP servers, agents, or tools as records and submits them for approval.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Curate records and approve&lt;/strong&gt;: A curator reviews pending records, approves or rejects them, and deprecates records no longer in use.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Discover approved resources:&lt;/strong&gt; Consumers, whether human users or AI agents, search the registry for the resources they need.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-makes-it-enterprise-ready"&gt;What makes it enterprise-ready&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Curation:&lt;/strong&gt; An approval workflow ensures only records that meet your security, compliance, and quality bar are discoverable. Administrators can remove a record from discovery at any time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hybrid search:&lt;/strong&gt; Semantic understanding combined with keyword matching, so both natural-language queries and exact name lookups return relevant results.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;MCP-native access:&lt;/strong&gt; The registry is available at a remote MCP endpoint, so any MCP-compatible client can search and use it directly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Flexible authorization:&lt;/strong&gt; Control access with IAM credentials or JWTs from your corporate identity provider.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-multi-environment-challenge"&gt;The multi-environment challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Agent Registry solves discovery within your AWS environment. But most enterprises don’t operate in one place. Agents and tools are deployed across multiple clouds, on-premises infrastructure, SaaS platforms, and enterprise applications, each with its own registry, naming convention, and metadata schema.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When each environment uses its own format for describing agentic resources, bringing it all together requires bespoke connectors for every pair of registries that need to interoperate. A shared specification changes that equation: if every registry describes resources in the same format and exposes discovery through a common protocol, publishers describe once and consumers discover everywhere.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="enter-agentic-resource-discovery-ard"&gt;Enter: Agentic Resource Discovery (ARD)&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ARD is an open standard, not a product or a single registry. It’s available under the Apache License 2.0 at &lt;a href="https://agenticresourcediscovery.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agenticresourcediscovery.org&lt;/a&gt; and on &lt;a href="https://github.com/ards-project" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub&lt;/a&gt;. AWS contributed feedback during the spec’s development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Think of ARD as enabling federation across registries analogous to how the Domain Name System (DNS) enables name resolution across networks. An organization can deploy agents across environments, and each environment’s catalog surfaces these resources in a common protocol, behind an endpoint. For combined discovery, any registry can index across them using an understanding of the shared common protocol. Thus, local registries can federate through ARD without requiring bilateral agreements or proprietary connectors.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-ard-can-complement-aws-agent-registry"&gt;How ARD can complement AWS Agent Registry&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We see ARD as a natural complement to the AWS Agent Registry model:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Federate without migrating:&lt;/strong&gt; Organizations with agentic infrastructure spread across clouds, on-premises, and SaaS could expose those resources in one consistent format. We expect ARD to enable cross-environment discovery while keeping control local.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Discover globally, control locally:&lt;/strong&gt; ARD’s design mirrors the control model AWS customers expect: the organization that publishes a catalog controls what’s in it, who can see it, and when to revoke access. We expect the existing access controls of AWS Agent Registry to remain at the enforcement point, with ARD serving as the interoperability layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enable public discovery:&lt;/strong&gt; With ARD as a shared protocol, any organization can publish a catalog on its own domain, making it discoverable by any ARD-compatible client. We expect ARD to open cross-organizational discovery paths for Agent Registry customers.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="learn-more"&gt;Learn more&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Read the &lt;a href="https://agenticresourcediscovery.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ARD specification&lt;/a&gt; to understand the catalog and registry model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Explore the reference implementations on &lt;a href="https://github.com/ards-project" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Check out the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/registry.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Read more about the launch in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/the-future-of-managing-agents-at-scale-aws-agent-registry-now-in-preview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Agent Registry now in preview&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is only the beginning. We’d love your feedback as AWS Agent Registry and our support for open discovery standards evolve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;
